# Plasma 1.1 research findings (harvested)

## Optimal pretraining data mixture for Plasma 1.1 (521M params, ~10.5B tokens, 1x RTX 5080 16GB) — 2024-2026 evidence review + exact recommended mixture and annealing recipe
### Findings
- **SmolLM2's controlled English-web ablation (1.7B params, 350B tokens each) shows FineWeb-Edu and DCLM-baseline are complementary, not substitutes: FineWeb-Edu wins knowledge/reasoning, DCLM wins commonsense/conversational. A 60/40 FW-Edu/DCLM mix recovers nearly all of both.**  
  Table 1 verbatim — MMLU: FW-Edu 37.5, DCLM 35.5, 40/60 36.5, 60/40 37.0. ARC: 57.5 / 53.5 / 53.2 / 56.0. OpenBookQA: 41.9 / 40.8 / 39.0 / 41.9. HellaSwag: 60.1 / 62.3 / 61.4 / 62.2. CommonsenseQA: 36.2 / 40.1 / 39.9 / 38.5. PIQA: 76.2 / 76.9 / 75.7 / 76.4. The 60/40 mix gives up 0.5 MMLU vs pure FW-Edu but gains +2.1 HellaSwag and +2.3 CommonsenseQA. Plasma is currently ~100% FineWeb-Edu on the web slice, i.e. sitting on the column that is worst at commonsense and conversational text — exactly the axis 'short writing' and 'instruction following' load on.  
  _src: SmolLM2: When Smol Goes Big (COLM 2025), Table 1 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.02737 / https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1_
- **Aggressive quality filtering (FineWeb-Edu / DCLM-baseline style) is unambiguously correct at Plasma's compute scale; the 2026 'bitter lesson' reversal only applies many orders of magnitude above it.**  
  'A Bitter Lesson for Data Filtering' trains 15M/80M/330M/1B/7B models and finds filtered pools (DCLM-baseline retains 2.1% of raw CC; RefinedWeb 13%) beat unfiltered CC at low compute, with unfiltered only winning at 330M+ trained very long, and predicts the full 240T CC pool becomes optimal around 1e30 FLOPs. Plasma's total budget is 6ND = 6 x 5.21e8 x 1.05e10 = 3.3e19 FLOPs — roughly 11 orders of magnitude below that crossover. Separately, FineWeb's own 1.82B/350B-token ablation: MMLU 33% -> ~37%, ARC 46% -> 57% (+24% relative) going from FineWeb to FineWeb-Edu.  
  _src: A Bitter Lesson for Data Filtering — https://arxiv.org/html/2605.19407v1 ; FineWeb blogpost/paper — https://huggingfacefw-blogpost-fineweb-v1.static.hf.space/index.html , https://arxiv.org/pdf/2406.17557_
- **FineWeb-Edu's educational classifier has a known, measured cost: pushing the threshold up improves knowledge benchmarks but degrades HellaSwag and PIQA, because conversational/narrative text is filtered out. This is a direct explanation for weak 'short writing' quality in an edu-only model.**  
  FineWeb authors: using a threshold higher than 3 'improves performance on knowledge and reasoning intensive benchmarks' but 'significantly degrades performance on HellaSwag and PIQA'. Released sizes: FineWeb-Edu (score>=3) 1.3T tokens; FineWeb-Edu-score-2 (score>=2) 5.4T tokens. 92% of FineWeb was discarded to build the score>=3 version.  
  _src: FineWeb blogpost — https://huggingfacefw-blogpost-fineweb-v1.static.hf.space/index.html ; dataset card https://huggingface.co/datasets/HuggingFaceFW/fineweb-edu_
- **Every strong 2025 small-model recipe uses a staged mixture that ends with a math/code/synthetic-heavy annealing phase over the last ~10% of tokens. Plasma uses a single static mixture with cosine-to-10%-of-peak LR — it has no annealing phase at all.**  
  SmolLM2 (11T tokens): Stage1 0-6T = 90% web (60/40 FWEdu/DCLM) + 10% code; Stage2 6-8T = 75% web, 20% code, 5% math (OpenWebMath); Stage3 8-10T = ~70% web (40/60 ratio), 20% code (Stack-Edu), 10% math; Stage4 decay 10-11T = 58% web, 24% Stack-Edu, 14% math (FineMath4+, InfiWebMath3+, OWM, AugGSM8K), 4% Cosmopedia v2, LR linear to 0 over the final 10% of training. SmolLM3 (11.2T): S1 0-8T = 85% web / 12% code / 3% math; S2 8-10T = 75% / 15% / 10%; S3 decay 10-11.1T = 63% / 24% / 13%, WSD scheduler, 2000 warmup steps, linear decay to 0 over the last 10% of steps.  
  _src: SmolLM2 paper Sec.4 — https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 ; SmolLM3 blog — https://huggingface.co/blog/smollm3_
- **Annealing/mid-training is the single highest-leverage intervention for exactly the two capabilities Plasma scores ~0% on (arithmetic, multi-step reasoning). Reproduced independently at 1.3B — the closest published scale to Plasma.**  
  LLM-jp reproduced OLMo-2 mid-training on Dolmino-mix-1124: GSM8K went 1.17% -> 30.17% for a 1.3B model and 14.86% -> 59.06% for a 7.7B model, with 10/12 and 12/12 benchmarks improving respectively. Llama-3 8B: annealing the LR linearly to 0 on 40B tokens of upsampled high-quality domain data improved GSM8K by +24.0% and MATH by +6.4% (negligible for 405B). Model souping across 3-4 seeds added a further GSM8K 27.90 -> 30.17 (1.3B) and 53.53 -> 59.06 (7.7B).  
  _src: LLM-jp mid-training report — https://llm-jp.nii.ac.jp/en/blog/mid-training-llm-jp-on-olmo2-data-setup-results-and-practical-tips/ ; Llama 3 Herd of Models Sec.3.4.3 — https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.21783 ; 2 OLMo 2 Furious — https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656_
- **The exact composition of the OLMo-2 50B annealing mix is public and is a directly copyable template: ~47% high-quality web, ~21% math, ~17% instruction (FLAN), ~6% academic, ~7% wiki, ~2.5% StackExchange.**  
  Dolmino-mix-1124 sampling percentages — 50B mix: DCLM-baseline 47.2%, FLAN (decontaminated) 16.6%, pes2o 5.85%, Wikipedia 7.11%, StackExchange 2.45%, Stage-2 Math 20.8%. 100B mix: 50.2 / 16.7 / 9.52 / 3.57 / 2.47 / 17.5. 300B mix: 51.9 / 11.3 / 19.4 / 4.86 / 1.68 / 10.8. The math component itself is mostly synthetic: TinyGSM-MIND 6.48B tok, MathCoder2 3.87B, TuluMath 230M, DolminoSynthMath 28.7M, metamath-owmfilter 84.2M, GSM8K 2.74M.  
  _src: allenai/dolmino-mix-1124 dataset card — https://huggingface.co/datasets/allenai/dolmino-mix-1124_
- **Instruction/FLAN-style data belongs INSIDE pretraining (the annealing phase), not exclusively in SFT. Plasma's corpus builder explicitly excludes it ('NO instruct/synthetic data ... to avoid pretrain/SFT leakage') — this is the opposite of current best practice and is very likely a major cause of the weak instruction-following score.**  
  OLMo 2 puts decontaminated FLAN at 16.56% of the 50B mid-training mix and still runs full Tulu-3 SFT afterwards. MiniCPM states the benefit of introducing high-quality/SFT-style data at the START of annealing is 'much higher than adding it during the SFT phase after annealing'. NVIDIA/CMU 'Front-Loading Reasoning' (ICLR 2026) trains 8B models with a pretraining mix of 80% base corpus + 20% reasoning data (336B tokens of diverse reasoning): +18.57% average on expert benchmarks, +39.32% on AIME, and critically doubling the SFT data for the no-reasoning-pretrain baseline still could not match even the weakest reasoning-pretrained model.  
  _src: Front-Loading Reasoning — https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03264 , https://www.alphaxiv.org/overview/2510.03264v1 ; MiniCPM — https://arxiv.org/pdf/2404.06395 ; Dolmino card as above_
- **Adding math web data does fix math/reasoning, and FineMath-4+ is the single best-value math corpus at Plasma's scale (9.6B tokens — enough to supply the entire recommended math slice several times over).**  
  FineMath sizes: FineMath-4+ 9.6B tokens / 6.7M docs; FineMath-3+ 34B / 21.4M; InfiWebMath-3+ 20.5B / 13.9M; InfiWebMath-4+ 8.5B / 6.3M. In SmolLM2's math ablation (anneal a 3T-token checkpoint on 60B tokens), FineMath-4+ gave '2x improvement on GSM8K and 6x improvement on MATH compared to InfiMM-WebMath'; secondary reporting puts this at roughly +20.5 GSM8K and +15.6 MATH. InfiMM-WebMath peaks at 14% GSM8K vs OpenWebMath's 10%. Nemotron-CC-Math (2025) is now stronger still: at a 100B-token ablation with math upweighted to 30% of the blend — MATH: OWM 29.20 / MegaMath-Pro 34.00 / FineMath-4+ 35.80 / Nemotron-CC-Math-4+ 40.60; GSM8K 71.42 / 73.46 / 75.97 / 76.27; MMLU-STEM 58.83 / 60.86 / 61.62 / 62.67.  
  _src: HuggingFaceTB/finemath card — https://huggingface.co/datasets/HuggingFaceTB/finemath ; SmolLM2 Sec 3.3 — https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 ; Nemotron-CC-Math — https://arxiv.org/html/2508.15096v1 ; MegaMath (371B tok) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02807_
- **Math web corpora alone will NOT produce arithmetic at 521M params / 10.5B tokens. Procedurally generated step-by-step arithmetic is what actually works at sub-1B scale.**  
  SmolLM2-360M, trained on 4T tokens including a 14%-math decay phase, still scores GSM8K 3.2 (5-shot). Qwen2.5-0.5B, trained on 18T tokens with dedicated Qwen2.5-Math/Coder corpora, scores GSM8K 41.6, MATH 19.5, HumanEval 30.5, MBPP 39.3, MMLU 47.5 — proving ~500M params is NOT the binding constraint, token volume and targeted math data are. TinyGSM shows the shortcut: 12.3M synthetic GSM8K-style problems with Python solutions took a 1.3B model to 81.5% GSM8K, beating its GPT-3.5 teacher (77.4%). Plasma's tokenizer already splits digits, which is the necessary precondition (tokenization-alignment work shows subword digit grouping is a primary cause of arithmetic failure).  
  _src: SmolLM2-360M card — https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M ; Qwen2.5-LLM blog — https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen2.5-llm/ ; TinyGSM — https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.09241 ; digit tokenization — https://arxiv.org/html/2402.14903_
- **Code should be INCREASED from 13% to ~20-25%, and it should be educational-filtered code, not raw code. The best evidence for this comes from a study whose smallest model (470M) is essentially Plasma's size.**  
  'To Code, or Not To Code?' (ICLR 2025) pretrained 64 models at 470M and 2.8B on ~200B tokens, sweeping code at 0/25/50/75/90/100%. 25% code was optimal for NON-code tasks; vs text-only it gave +8.2% relative natural-language reasoning, +4.2% world knowledge, +6.6% generative win-rate, 12x code. A 50/50 mix helped code but hurt NL reasoning. Cooldown with 20% code added a further +3.6% NL reasoning, +10.1% world knowledge, +20% code (vs cooldown without code). Separately, Stack-Edu (125B tokens, educational-filtered from StarCoder2Data) beat raw StarCoder2Data on MultiPL-E at every language: Python 20.7->25.6, C++ 16.7->24.8, JavaScript 18.2->22.4, Java 17.6->22.7.  
  _src: To Code or Not To Code — https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.10914 , https://openreview.net/pdf?id=zSfeN1uAcx ; Stack-Edu — https://huggingface.co/datasets/HuggingFaceTB/stack-edu , SmolLM2 Table 2_
- **Wikipedia at 12% is roughly 3x too high, and arXiv/StackExchange at 5%/10% are heavily over-weighted. Learned domain-reweighting methods consistently strip these domains down and pour the budget into web text.**  
  DoReMi (280M proxy -> 8B target) optimized weights on The Pile, baseline -> DoReMi: Pile-CC 0.1121 -> 0.6057 (5.4x UP); Wikipedia(en) 0.0919 -> 0.0699 (24% down); StackExchange 0.0929 -> 0.0153 (6.1x DOWN); ArXiv 0.1052 -> 0.0036 (29x DOWN); PubMed Central 0.1071 -> 0.0046; Github 0.0427 -> 0.0179; Books3 0.0676 -> 0.0224. Result: +6.5pp average few-shot downstream accuracy, baseline accuracy reached with 2.6x fewer steps. RegMix (512 proxy models at 1M params/1B tokens, validated at 1B/25B) independently concludes 'web corpora rather than data perceived as high-quality like Wikipedia have the strongest positive correlation with downstream performance'. For reference, OLMo-2's annealing mix uses Wikipedia at only 7.11% and StackExchange at 2.45%; SmolLM2 and SmolLM3 do not list Wikipedia or arXiv as top-level components at all.  
  _src: DoReMi Table 1 — https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2305.10429 ; RegMix (ICLR 2025) — https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.01492 ; Dolmino card_
- **Synthetic textbook data (Cosmopedia-style) helps but has a hard, measured ceiling of roughly 5% for models at Plasma's scale — above that it actively degrades performance. Rephrased web synthetic tolerates much more (~30%).**  
  'Demystifying Synthetic Data in LLM Pre-training' trained 1000+ models up to 3B params / 200B tokens. Best-found synthetic ratios 'are all below 50% and appear to converge ~30%'. Specifically: HQ-rephrased optimal ~30% synthetic / 70% natural; QA-rephrased ~50% at small scale falling to 30%; TEXTBOOK-style optimal at <5% for smaller configurations, and pure/67% textbook 'notably underperformed CommonCrawl' with model-collapse signatures. This matches SmolLM2 empirically choosing exactly 4% Cosmopedia v2 in its decay phase. Cosmopedia v2 itself is 28B tokens / 39.1M docs generated by Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct (40% middle-school audience, 30% college, 30% other).  
  _src: Demystifying Synthetic Data — https://arxiv.org/html/2510.01631v1 ; SmolLM-Corpus card — https://huggingface.co/datasets/HuggingFaceTB/smollm-corpus ; SmolLM blog — https://huggingface.co/blog/smollm_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Split training into a WSD-style two-phase run instead of one static mixture + cosine. Phase A (stable) = steps 0-280,000 (~9.17B tokens, 87.5%); Phase B (anneal/decay) = steps 280,000-320,000 (~1.31B tokens, 12.5%). Change configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml: keep warmup_steps 3000, hold LR at 1.2e-4 through step 280,000, then LINEAR decay to 0.0 (not 1.2e-5) over the last 40,000 steps. Set min_lr: 0.0 and add a `decay_start_step: 280000` / `schedule: wsd` branch in src/train/train.py. Build two shard sets (data/shards_1.1_v3_stable and data/shards_1.1_v3_anneal) so the loader just switches directories at step 280,000.**  
  why: Every competitive 2024-2026 small-model recipe (SmolLM2, SmolLM3, MiniCPM, OLMo-2, Llama-3, Qwen3) ends with an annealing phase over the last ~10% of tokens with LR -> 0 and an upweighted high-quality mixture. Plasma has none. MiniCPM found WSD 'significantly outperforms warmup-cosine-decay' when annealing data quality is high, and decaying to 10% of peak instead of 0 forfeits most of the convergence benefit. This phase is where math, code and instruction-following are actually acquired: LLM-jp measured GSM8K 1.17% -> 30.17% at 1.3B purely from mid-training.  
  gain: The largest single win available. Expect +4-8 points overall on the honest benchmark, concentrated in instruction following, short writing and arithmetic. Llama-3 8B saw +24.0% GSM8K / +6.4% MATH from a 40B-token anneal; LLM-jp saw 10/12 benchmarks improve at 1.3B.  
  risk: Requires a small train-loop change (LR schedule branch + mid-run shard-dir switch); a bug there silently wastes the run. Mitigate by logging LR every 50 steps and asserting lr==0 at the final step. Also, decaying to exactly 0 makes the run non-resumable-into-more-steps, so checkpoint step 280,000 before the decay starts. No VRAM impact.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Replace the SOURCES dict in scripts/build_pretrain_corpus.py for Phase A with: fineweb_edu 0.44, dclm_baseline 0.18, code_stack_edu 0.18, math 0.08, cosmopedia_v2 0.04, wikipedia 0.04, stackexchange 0.04, arxiv 0.00. (Web total 62%, split 71/29 FineWeb-Edu/DCLM.) Concretely: HuggingFaceFW/fineweb-edu name='sample-10BT' (4.0B tok), mlfoundations/dclm-baseline-1.0-parquet streamed to 1.65B tok, HuggingFaceTB/stack-edu restricted to Python/JavaScript/SQL/Shell for 1.65B tok, HuggingFaceTB/finemath configs finemath-4plus + infiwebmath-3plus for 0.73B tok, HuggingFaceTB/smollm-corpus config 'cosmopedia-v2' for 0.37B tok, wiki 0.37B, StackExchange 0.37B. Delete the arXiv source entirely.**  
  why: Four separate corrections, each with a primary source. (a) Adding DCLM at ~29% of the web slice buys back the commonsense/conversational distribution FineWeb-Edu's classifier filters out (SmolLM2 Table 1: +2.2 HellaSwag, +3.9 CommonsenseQA for DCLM, and the mix keeps MMLU within 0.5). I use 71/29 rather than SmolLM2's 60/40 because at 3.3e19 FLOPs heavier filtering is still net-positive ('A Bitter Lesson for Data Filtering'). (b) Code 13% -> 18% moves toward the 25% that was optimal for NON-code tasks at 470M in Aryabumi et al., and switching to Stack-Edu is a free +4 to +8 MultiPL-E points per language. (c) 8% math where there is currently 0% matches SmolLM3 stage-2 (10%) and SmolLM2 stage-3 (10%). (d) arXiv 5% -> 0% and Wikipedia 12% -> 4% and StackExchange 10% -> 4%: DoReMi cuts ArXiv 29x and StackExchange 6.1x and trims Wikipedia 24% while pushing web 5.4x, worth +6.5pp average downstream; RegMix independently finds web beats Wikipedia. LaTeX-heavy arXiv is near-pure noise for a 521M model with a 1024-token context.  
  gain: +2-4 points overall. Reallocating 5% arXiv + 8% wiki + 6% StackExchange (~19% of 9.2B = 1.75B tokens) into DCLM/code/math/Cosmopedia is the highest-value swap available without changing the token budget. Expect commonsense and writing fluency to move most; MMLU-style factual QA should be flat-to-slightly-up (FineWeb-Edu already carries encyclopedic content — SmolLM2-360M scores TriviaQA 16.9 vs Qwen2.5-0.5B's 4.3 with no explicit Wikipedia slice).  
  risk: Cutting Wikipedia may cost a little on narrow entity-recall questions; hedge by keeping wiki at 4% in Phase A and raising it to 3% in the anneal (OLMo-2 uses 7.11% wiki in its 50B anneal). Disk/bandwidth is the real cost: stream with datasets(streaming=True) rather than downloading DCLM or Stack-Edu in full. No VRAM impact.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Phase B (anneal) mixture over ~1.31B tokens: fineweb_edu 0.30, dclm_baseline 0.12 (web 42%), math 0.20, code_stack_edu 0.20, instruction_flan 0.10, cosmopedia_v2 0.05, wikipedia 0.02, stackexchange 0.01. Math slice = 262M tokens split ~60% procedurally generated step-by-step arithmetic/word problems + ~40% FineMath-4+ / MegaMath-Web-Pro. Code slice = 262M tokens, Python-dominant, plus short docstring->function pairs. Instruction slice = 131M tokens of decontaminated FLAN-style + a HELD-OUT disjoint slice of the existing 168k-sequence SFT set, trained with FULL loss (not assistant-only masking, since this is still pretraining).**  
  why: This is a scaled-down Dolmino-mix-1124 50B blend (DCLM 47.2%, FLAN 16.6%, math 20.8%, wiki 7.11%, StackExchange 2.45%, pes2o 5.85%), cross-checked against SmolLM2 stage-4 (58% web / 24% code / 14% math / 4% Cosmopedia) and SmolLM3 stage-3 (63/24/13). I hold math at 20% (Dolmino level, well below the 30% Nemotron-CC-Math used in its ablations) and code at 20% (matching the 20%-code cooldown in Aryabumi et al. that added +3.6% NL reasoning, +10.1% world knowledge, +20% code). Cosmopedia is capped at 5% because 'Demystifying Synthetic Data' found textbook-style synthetic optimal below 5% at small configurations and actively harmful at 67%. Instruction data at 10% directly reverses the 'NO instruct/synthetic data' rule in build_pretrain_corpus.py: OLMo-2 ships FLAN at 16.56% of mid-training and still SFTs afterwards, MiniCPM says the gain from putting this data at the start of annealing is 'much higher' than adding it in SFT, and Front-Loading Reasoning showed doubling SFT data cannot recover what pretraining reasoning data provides.  
  gain: Instruction following and short writing should be the biggest movers (+5-10 points on those subscores); arithmetic on 2-4 digit add/sub/mul should go from 10-15% to roughly 35-55% if the procedural drills are included. Overall benchmark should clear 1.0's 43% with margin.  
  risk: Contamination is the real hazard, not 'leakage' in the abstract. Mandatory: 13-gram-overlap decontaminate the entire anneal mix against the evaluation prompts (Llama-3 and OLMo-2 both do this explicitly), and keep the SFT slice used here strictly disjoint from the SFT set used later. Second risk: 10% instruction data mid-pretrain can make the base model chatty in raw-completion evals — measure base-model perplexity on a held-out FineWeb-Edu split before and after to confirm no general-ability regression. No VRAM impact.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Generate ~60-80M unique tokens of procedural arithmetic and repeat them ~2-3x inside the anneal (target ~157M tokens = 12% of Phase B). Format: explicit digit-by-digit chain of thought with carries written out, in the same 'User: ...\nAssistant: ...' chat template used for SFT. Cover: integer add/sub 2-6 digits, multiplication 2x2 and 3x2, integer division with remainder, percentages, unit conversion, and 2-3 step word problems built from templates. Emit ~40% of items with a Python-solution form (`answer = 47*13`) as well as a natural-language form. Extend scripts/generate_synthetic.py to produce this; no LLM API needed — it is deterministic template generation with numpy.**  
  why: FineMath and OpenWebMath teach mathematical *language*, not arithmetic *procedure*. The evidence that web math alone fails at this scale is direct: SmolLM2-360M had a 14%-math decay phase over 4T tokens and still scores GSM8K 3.2. The evidence that procedural synthetic works at small scale is equally direct: TinyGSM's 12.3M synthetic GSM8K-style problems with Python solutions took a 1.3B model to 81.5% GSM8K, and OLMo-2's own math slice is dominated by synthetic (TinyGSM-MIND 6.48B + MathCoder2 3.87B tokens). Plasma's tokenizer already splits digits, which is the necessary precondition — the carry-propagation literature shows subword digit grouping is a primary cause of arithmetic failure, and writing carries explicitly aligns the computation with left-to-right autoregression.  
  gain: This is the specific fix for the 10-15% arithmetic score. Expect 2-4 digit add/sub to reach 60-85% and 2-step word problems to become non-zero. Be honest about the ceiling: GSM8K proper will likely stay under 5-8% at 10.5B total tokens regardless.  
  risk: Overfitting to template surface forms — mitigate by randomizing entity names, units, number ranges, and phrasing across at least 30 templates, and by holding out an unseen template family for evaluation. Repeating a 60-80M-token set 2-3x is well inside the safe repetition regime (<=4 epochs is near-free per Muennighoff et al.). Do NOT push this above ~12% of the anneal or it will crowd out general data.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Retrain the 48k SentencePiece tokenizer on a sample drawn in the SAME proportions as the new final mixture (44% FineWeb-Edu / 18% DCLM / 18% Stack-Edu / 8% FineMath / 4% Cosmopedia / 4% wiki / 4% SE), keeping byte_fallback=True and split_digits=True. Update scripts/train_tokenizer.py's input list; sample ~5-10GB of text. Do this BEFORE building the new shards.**  
  why: The current tokenizer was fit on a corpus that was ~60% FineWeb-Edu prose with essentially no math and only 13% generic code. Adding Stack-Edu, FineMath (LaTeX-heavy) and Cosmopedia will push a large fraction of the new math/code tokens through byte fallback, inflating sequence length and wasting an already-tiny token budget. Fertility on math/code text can easily be 1.3-1.6x worse with a mismatched tokenizer, which is a direct multiplier on effective epochs.  
  gain: Roughly 10-25% more effective content per token on the math and code slices, i.e. the equivalent of a few hundred million extra tokens for free. Also reduces the chance the model never learns to emit well-formed Python indentation or LaTeX.  
  risk: Invalidates every existing checkpoint and shard — this must be decided before the run starts, not mid-run. Verify by measuring bytes-per-token on held-out samples of each source before and after; if the new tokenizer is worse on plain prose, back off the code/math sampling weight in the tokenizer corpus. Keep vocab_size at 48000 so the model config and tied-embedding parameter count are unchanged (no VRAM impact).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Raise the total token budget. Build a ~14-16B unique-token corpus under the new mixture and train for 2.5-3 epochs (35-45B tokens, i.e. max_steps 1,070,000-1,370,000 at 32,768 tok/step) rather than 320,000 steps / 1.4 epochs. If wall-clock forbids that, at minimum keep the 7.5-15B corpus and raise max_steps to ~640,000 (21B tokens, ~1.5-2.8 epochs) after resolving the throughput gap.**  
  why: Plasma is at 20.2 tokens/parameter — exactly Chinchilla, and 200-1800x below every competitive small model (SmolLM3-3B 3,733; SmolLM2-360M 11,111; SmolLM2-135M 14,815; Qwen2.5-0.5B 36,000). Qwen2.5-0.5B reaching HumanEval 30.5 / GSM8K 41.6 proves ~500M params is not the constraint; 18T tokens is what bought those scores. Data-constrained scaling laws (Muennighoff et al., JMLR 2025) show repeats up to ~4 epochs cost almost nothing versus fresh tokens, and Plasma's weight_decay of 0.1 is already the recommended mitigation for repetition. The measured 56 TFLOP/s vs the RTX 5080's ~450 TFLOP/s bf16 peak means the token budget is throughput-limited, not fundamentally limited: closing even half that gap turns 45B tokens into ~16 days rather than ~38.  
  gain: Far larger than any mixture change. Going from 10.5B to 35-45B tokens is worth substantially more than every other recommendation combined — it is the difference between a model that cannot emit runnable Python and one that occasionally can. Mixture changes are worth a few points; a 3-4x token budget is worth double-digit points.  
  risk: Wall clock. At the current 13,500 tok/s, 45B tokens is ~38 days versus the ~9-day plan. This recommendation is only actionable in conjunction with fixing throughput (separate workstream: the 56 TFLOP/s effective figure suggests 2-3x is recoverable via micro_batch>2 with selective rather than full gradient checkpointing, torch.compile, and Flash/SDPA attention). Also check the shard writer: uint16 tokens x 45B = 90GB of shards, so verify disk headroom before committing. No VRAM increase from more steps alone.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **If corpus size becomes the binding constraint instead of wall clock, apply REWIRE-style rewriting: take the FineWeb documents that FAILED the quality filter (score 2, currently discarded) and rewrite them with a local instruct model into clean expository prose, then blend rewritten:raw-high-quality at roughly 1:1 within the web slice. FineWeb-Edu-score-2 has 5.4T tokens to draw from versus 1.3T at score>=3.**  
  why: REWIRE tested exactly this at 1B/3B/7B with 10B-100B token budgets — Plasma's regime — and found rewriting beats both discarding and upsampling, with the largest gains at 10-30B tokens and an optimal ~1:1 rewritten-to-raw HQ ratio. Nemotron-CC validated the same principle at scale: its HQ subset beats DCLM by +5.6 MMLU at 8B/1T tokens, with 1.9T of its 6.3T tokens synthetic. This converts Plasma's binding constraint (unique high-quality tokens) into a constraint on offline GPU time, which can run while the training GPU is busy.  
  gain: Effectively 2-4x the unique high-quality token pool without new crawling, enabling the 35-45B-token run above at <=2 epochs instead of 4+.  
  risk: Rewriting is itself GPU-expensive and competes with training on a single 16GB card — realistically it must run on a rented GPU or be done in a separate window. Rewriting quality matters: 'Demystifying Synthetic Data' found a Llama-3-70B generator produced WORSE data than an 8B generator in some settings, so do not assume bigger is better; validate on a 200M-token pilot before committing. Keep total synthetic (rewritten + Cosmopedia) under ~35% of the web slice per that paper's ~30% convergence point.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Train 2-3 anneal runs from the same step-280,000 checkpoint with different data orderings/seeds and average the resulting weights (uniform model soup) to produce the final base checkpoint before SFT.**  
  why: OLMo-2 souped 3 stage-2 models for the 7B (and 3x100B + 1x300B for the 13B). LLM-jp's reproduction quantified it: GSM8K 27.90 -> 30.17 at 1.3B and 53.53 -> 59.06 at 7.7B, purely from averaging seeds. Each anneal run is only 40,000 steps (~12.5% of the budget), so 3 runs cost ~37.5% of one full pretraining run, not 3x.  
  gain: +1-3 points, concentrated in math/reasoning, for ~25% additional wall clock on top of a single anneal. Cheapest remaining point-per-hour after the anneal itself.  
  risk: Souping only works when all runs branch from the SAME checkpoint and stay in the same loss basin — averaging runs with different LR schedules or from different base checkpoints will produce garbage. Verify by evaluating the soup before discarding the individual checkpoints; if the soup is worse than the best member, keep the best member. Averaging is done on CPU in fp32, so it needs ~4x521M x 4 bytes ~= 8GB of system RAM but zero extra VRAM.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [low] **Also evaluate LLM-jp's finding that a FIXED (non-decayed) learning rate during mid-training beat linear-decay-to-zero at 1.3B — run a 5,000-step probe of both schedules from the step-280,000 checkpoint on the anneal mixture and pick the winner on a held-out eval before committing the full 40,000 steps.**  
  why: This is the one place the sources actively disagree. SmolLM2, SmolLM3, MiniCPM and Llama-3 all decay to zero; LLM-jp's direct reproduction of OLMo-2 mid-training found 'fixed learning rate outperformed linear decay' at both 1.3B and 7.7B, and that smaller models preferred LONGER mid-training (1.3B peaked at 300B tokens while 7.7B peaked at 50B). Since Plasma is smaller than either, both the schedule choice and a longer-than-10% anneal are genuinely open questions worth 5,000 steps (~3.4 hours) to settle empirically.  
  gain: Insurance rather than gain — but if the fixed-LR result holds at 521M, it also implies extending the anneal from 12.5% to 20-25% of steps, which the LLM-jp scaling trend (smaller model -> longer mid-training) actively predicts.  
  risk: A 5,000-step probe is a noisy signal; use a fixed held-out set of at least 500 prompts and compare on the aggregate, not on a single benchmark. If the two are within noise, default to linear-decay-to-zero since that is what four independent teams shipped. Costs ~7 hours total for both probes.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Learning-rate schedules for Plasma 1.1 pretraining: WSD / trapezoidal vs cosine, decay-to-zero, decay-phase sizing, and the correct protocol for continuing from the step-40,000 cosine checkpoint
### Findings
- **Cosine is not better than constant-LR + a short cooldown (WSD); it is only better if you happen to stop exactly at the horizon you tuned the cycle for. A constant LR followed by a cooldown over 10-20% of steps matches or beats a length-matched cosine, and the required cooldown fraction SHRINKS as the run gets longer (on a 200k-step run, a 10k-step cooldown = 5% almost perfectly matched cosine).**  
  Hagele et al., NeurIPS 2024 Spotlight, 210M and 360M Llama-style models on SlimPajama: 'the cooldown surpasses cosine between 10-20% of steps ... but largely stops improving when done over a majority of training'; 'the required duration of cooldown to match cosine loss decreases with longer training'; 'just 10k cooldown steps almost perfectly match cosine in performance' on a 200k-step run. Their 210M config: d_model 768, 24 layers, ffw 2048, batch 0.1M tokens (200 x 512), warmup 300, wd 0.1, peak LR 1e-3 for cosine and 5e-4 for the constant/WSD phase.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18392 (paper) and https://github.com/epfml/schedules-and-scaling (code, exposes --wsd-fract-decay and --decay-type)_
- **The (1-sqrt) cooldown shape beats linear cooldown, which beats cosine-shaped cooldown, at cooldown fractions of 10-20%. Formula: f(n,N,N_decay) = 1 - sqrt((n - (N - N_decay))/N_decay). Its mean value over the cooldown is 1/3 of peak, vs 1/2 for linear, i.e. it spends less area at high LR and gets to low LR faster.**  
  Hagele et al. identify (1-sqrt) as outperforming standard linear decay and both as better than untuned cosine for long lengths. Corroborated by Scaling Law with LR Annealing: 'At 10-20% annealing ratios, 1-sqrt decay outperforms cosine; at 50%+ ratios, cosine proves superior' — the optimal shape is a function of the chosen fraction.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18392 and https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11029_
- **Decaying to ZERO beats the near-universal 'decay to 0.1 x peak' convention by a measurable, free margin, and the gap grows with tokens-per-parameter and as batch size SHRINKS. Plasma's min_lr = 1.2e-5 = 0.1 x peak is leaving this on the table.**  
  Bergsma et al. 'Straight to Zero' (ICLR 2025): linear decay-to-zero (D2Z) vs optimal 10x decay, both at tuned peak LR — 610M model at 20 TPP: 0.77% lower loss; 610M at 80 TPP: 1.8% lower; 111M at 200 TPP: 1.6%; 610M at 200 TPP: 2.6%; projected ~2% at 70B. 'A 610M-parameter model trained for 80 TPP using D2Z achieves lower loss than when trained for 200 TPP using 10x decay' = 60% compute saving. They explicitly state D2Z's advantage 'increase[s] as batch size decreases (higher gradient noise)'. Linear-D2Z also slightly beat cosine-D2Z in all cases.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15938_
- **MiniCPM's WSD: warmup -> long stable phase at peak -> short exponential decay, with decay over ~10% of TOTAL TOKENS being sufficient. Decay function is exponential halving: f(s-T) = 0.5^((s-T)/T). During decay 'the loss experiences a significant rapid decline and quickly decreases to be equal to or lower than the Cosine LRS'.**  
  Hu et al., MiniCPM. '10% of total tokens is sufficient to achieve the best results.' MiniCPM-2.4B: 4M-token batch, peak LR 0.01 (muP-parameterised, NOT directly transferable to a standard-parameterised model like Plasma). MiniCPM-1.2B: 2M -> 4M token batch, same LR. WSD also let them measure data-model scaling laws with linear rather than quadratic compute, yielding the 192:1 tokens-per-parameter compute-optimal ratio vs Chinchilla's 20:1.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06395_
- **DeepSeek uses WSD-family schedules explicitly BECAUSE of continual-training reuse, and reports final quality equal to cosine. DeepSeek LLM: constant to 80% of tokens, then step to 0.316x peak (80-90%), then 0.1x peak (90-100%). DeepSeek-V3: 2k-step warmup to 2.2e-4, constant to 10T of 14.8T tokens, then cosine decay to 2.2e-5 over 4.3T tokens (~29% of the run), then a final 500B-token stage (333B constant, 167B decay to 7.3e-6).**  
  DeepSeek LLM: 'despite differences in the loss reduction trend during training, the final performance using a multi-step learning rate scheduler is essentially consistent with that of a cosine scheduler' and it 'enables reuse of training from the first phase, offering a unique convenience for continual training'. 7B: LR 4.2e-4, batch 2304 seqs; 67B: LR 3.2e-4, batch 4608 seqs; 2000 warmup steps.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.02954 and https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437_
- **WSD is now the default at small scale: SmolLM2 used WSD/trapezoidal with 2000 warmup steps, peak LR 5.0e-4, and 10% linear decay. OLMo 2 anneals linearly to zero over 50B tokens (7B) / 100B tokens (13B, 32B), repeated 3-4 times over different data orders, then 'model soups' the resulting checkpoints. They report that decaying to zero over 50B vs 100B tokens gives equivalent training loss.**  
  SmolLM2 technical report (COLM 2025); OLMo 2 technical report — 'the learning rate is linearly annealed to zero from where it left off after Stage 1 for each training run', final model = average of the anneal endpoints.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.02737 and https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656_
- **Mechanistically, an un-cooled checkpoint reports an ARTIFICIALLY HIGH loss that is proportional to the current LR. The 'river valley' theory decomposes stable-phase loss into real progress along the river plus a hill term ~= (d-1)*eta*n^2/2 caused by oscillation across the valley; the cooldown does not create new progress, it collapses the oscillation and reveals progress already banked. This is why the drop looks sudden, and why the drop size scales with eta and with model size.**  
  Wen et al., 'Understanding Warmup-Stable-Decay Learning Rates: A River Valley Loss Landscape Perspective' — stable-phase Theorem 3.4 excess = (d-1)*eta*n^2/2; decay-phase Theorem 3.5 bound = (d-1)*eta_t*n^2/2. Independently reproduced on a 334K-param CNN as well as a 160M LM in 'Universal Dynamics of Warmup Stable Decay', which finds ~20% optimal cooldown for LMs and ~35% for CNNs.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.05192 and https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09000_
- **WSD-S: you do NOT have to throw away the compute spent on an exploratory cooldown. Resuming training directly from the DECAYED checkpoint at the high constant LR beats classic WSD (which rolls back to the pre-decay checkpoint) under the same compute budget, and matches per-budget optimally-tuned cosine oracle runs. Tested 0.1B-1.2B params at 50B/100B/200B token budgets. Decay-fraction sensitivity is low 'as long as it is near 10%', but do not start a decay immediately after a loss spike.**  
  Wen et al. (river valley paper), WSD-S section: 'WSD-S leads to a better validation loss than WSD under the same compute budgets'.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.05192_
- **Convex-optimization theory now predicts the WSD sudden drop quantitatively, and says linear-decay-to-zero is the theoretically optimal shape. It also gives an exact rule for how to change the peak LR when you EXTEND a run: gamma* scales as 1/sqrt(T). Extending 4k -> 8k steps requires scaling the base LR by rho ~= 0.525; 4k -> 16k requires rho ~= 0.375.**  
  Schaipp, Defazio et al., 'The Surprising Agreement Between Convex Optimization Theory and Learning-Rate Scheduling for Large Model Training'. Empirically on 124M/210M Llama models on SlimPajama, the schedule adapted from the bound bought ~0.01 nats at 100k steps (= 6k extra steps / 0.6B tokens) and ~0.01 nats at 200k steps (= 14.5k steps / 1.5B tokens). 20% cooldown was effective in their configs.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.18965_
- **The scaling law with LR annealing gives a closed form that explains why WSD beats cosine at fixed compute: L(s) = L0 + A*S1^(-alpha) - C*S2, where S1 = sum of LRs so far ('forward area') and S2 = a decay-weighted sum of LR DROPS ('annealing area', lambda ~ 0.99-0.999). Holding a high constant LR maximises S1; a short sharp decay at the end still captures nearly all of S2. Their recommendation: WSD annealing ratio ~15-25% depending on total steps.**  
  Tissue et al., 'Scaling Law with Learning Rate Annealing': WSD 'achieves lower final loss than standard cosine by increasing S1 while minimally reducing S2'. Fitting the law costs <1% of a Chinchilla-style sweep because one loss curve yields thousands of fitting points.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11029_
- **Applied to Plasma's actual numbers: continuing the current cosine from step 40,000 to 320,000 accumulates a forward area S1 = 16.53, whereas switching to constant 1.2e-4 until step 288,000 then a 32,000-step (1-sqrt) decay to zero accumulates S1 = 31.04 — 1.88x more — at IDENTICAL wall clock and identical final LR. Under L = L0 + A*S1^(-alpha) - C*S2 with alpha ~ 0.5, that shrinks the A*S1^(-alpha) term by ~27%.**  
  Direct numeric integration of Plasma's CosineScheduler (src/train/scheduler.py) vs a WSD schedule over steps 40,000-320,000 with peak 1.2e-4, min 1.2e-5, warmup 3000, max_steps 320000. Under the current cosine the LR is already down to 80.8% of peak at step 100k, 60.1% at 150k, 38.2% at 200k and 18.8% at 256k — i.e. the last 40% of the run runs at <40% of peak LR, which is exactly the compute WSD reclaims.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\scheduler.py + configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml (computed)_
- **Plasma's step-40,000 checkpoint is, for practical purposes, a STABLE-PHASE checkpoint, not a decayed one: the cosine LR there is 1.1641e-4 = 97.0% of the 1.2e-4 peak. Switching to a WSD constant phase at 1.2e-4 is a +3.1% LR change — well inside step-to-step noise, requiring no rewarm, no spike, and no loss of optimizer state. This is precisely the case the literature says is SAFE to continue from.**  
  Computed from src/train/scheduler.py: decay_ratio = (40000-3000)/(320000-3000) = 0.11672, coeff = 0.96678, lr = 1.2e-5 + 0.96678*(1.08e-4) = 1.1641e-4. Hagele et al.: 'the natural approach is to use checkpoints before the cooldown to continue training with a high LR; this avoids loss spikes and brittle training when rewarming.' src/train/train.py --resume restores AdamW m/v state via load_checkpoint(args.resume, model, optimizer), so second-moment estimates are preserved.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py lines 105-120 + https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18392_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Set min_lr: 0.0 in configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml (currently 1.2e-5). Do this regardless of which schedule you pick. If you keep the cosine, this alone converts it from a '10x decay' to a 'cosine-D2Z'.**  
  why: D2Z is the single most replicated result in this literature: decaying to zero beats decaying to 0.1x peak at every model size, every TPP, and every batch size tested, and the advantage GROWS as batch size shrinks. Plasma's 32,768-token batch is unusually small for a 521M model, so it sits in the high-gradient-noise regime where D2Z helps most. Bergsma et al. measured 0.77% at 610M / 20 TPP (Plasma is 521M / 20.1 TPP) rising to 2.6% at 200 TPP. Mechanism: with LR->0 the AdamW update EMA weights are distributed uniformly over more historical updates, averaging away gradient noise.  
  gain: 0.8-1.5% lower final validation loss vs the current 0.1x floor, i.e. roughly 0.02-0.05 nats. At a val ppl of 19.45 that is ~19.1-19.0. Larger if you extend TPP. Zero compute cost, zero VRAM cost, one-line change.  
  risk: Essentially none. The only caveat is that a checkpoint saved exactly at LR=0 is a dead end for further training — always keep the last pre-cooldown constant-LR checkpoint if you might extend.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Convert the run to WSD at step 40,000. Replace CosineScheduler with a WSDScheduler in src/train/scheduler.py: warmup 0-3,000 (already done); CONSTANT eta = 1.2e-4 for steps 3,000-288,000; (1-sqrt) cooldown for steps 288,000-320,000 to exactly 0. Cooldown formula: lr(s) = 1.2e-4 * (1 - sqrt((s - 288000) / 32000)). Resume with `python -m src.train.train --config configs/pretrain_1.1_wsd.yaml --resume checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt`. Make decay_start and decay_steps explicit config fields (NOT derived from max_steps) so you can move the cooldown later without editing code.**  
  why: At step 40,000 the cosine LR is 1.1641e-4 = 97.0% of peak, so switching to a 1.2e-4 constant phase is a +3.1% LR step — no rewarm, no spike, no discontinuity, and --resume already restores AdamW m/v. Integrating the two schedules over steps 40,000-320,000 gives a forward-area S1 of 31.04 for WSD vs 16.53 for the cosine — 1.88x more LR-area at identical wall clock. Under L = L0 + A*S1^(-alpha) - C*S2 the S1 term shrinks by ~27% at alpha=0.5, while the short sharp cooldown still captures nearly all of the S2 annealing term. 32,000 steps is 10% of the horizon, which is inside Hagele's 10-20% sweet spot and matches MiniCPM's '10% of total tokens is sufficient' and SmolLM2's 10%.  
  gain: Final loss at or modestly below the cosine endpoint (Hagele: cooldown surpasses cosine at 10-20%), PLUS the much larger practical win of retrospective cooldowns (next recommendation). Combined with min_lr=0 the loss gain is probably 0.02-0.08 nats; I would not promise more than that from the schedule shape alone.  
  risk: Low. Two implementation hazards: (1) the warmup branch must key off absolute step so it does not re-trigger on resume at step 40,000 — Plasma's get_lr(step) already uses absolute steps, so it is fine; (2) if you keep the cosine class and merely shorten max_steps you get a DOWNWARD discontinuity instead (max_steps 200,000 would drop the LR to 1.1087e-4 at step 40,000, -4.8%). Also note the run is not free-lunch: WSD's stable LR is horizon-dependent, so a huge later extension would leave you slightly off-optimum.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Run PROBE COOLDOWNS. At steps 100,000 / 160,000 / 220,000, fork the constant-LR checkpoint into a throwaway branch and cool down over 5,000 steps with (1-sqrt) to zero (3.3 h each, 0.164B tokens), then run scripts/benchmark_v2.py on the cooled branch. Resume the main run from the saved constant-LR checkpoint (classic WSD) or, to reclaim the compute, resume directly from the DECAYED checkpoint at the constant LR (WSD-S).**  
  why: This is the actual reason WSD exists and it directly addresses the project's core problem: you currently have no honest read on whether 1.1 is going to beat 1.0's 43% until day 9. An un-cooled mid-run checkpoint systematically UNDER-reports the model because the stable-phase loss carries a hill term proportional to eta — you cannot benchmark it fairly. A 3.3-hour cooldown turns a mid-run checkpoint into a deployable, honestly measurable model. Hagele et al. confirm cooldowns 'can also be initiated retrospectively from a checkpoint'; Wen et al. show WSD-S resuming from the decayed checkpoint at high constant LR actually beats plain WSD under equal budget.  
  gain: Three honest benchmark datapoints for ~10 hours of the 187-hour remaining run (5.3% overhead), with the option to abort or re-plan early. Under the current cosine this information is simply unavailable at any price short of a full restart.  
  risk: Low. Two real hazards: do NOT start a cooldown right after a loss spike (Wen et al.), and keep the probe branches in separate checkpoint prefixes so run_rebuild_v2.py's auto-resume-from-latest does not accidentally pick up a cooled-down checkpoint as the main line.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Raise AdamW beta2 from 0.95 to 0.99 (optionally 0.999) at the start of the cooldown only, keeping beta1 = 0.9. Implement as a step-conditional in the train loop next to the existing `for pg in optimizer.param_groups: pg['lr'] = lr` block: at step >= decay_start, set pg['betas'] = (0.9, 0.99).**  
  why: Dremov et al. (TMLR 08/2025) found that AdamW hyperparameter choice during the cooldown produces performance swings comparable in magnitude to the choice of cooldown shape itself, with 'surprisingly large values of beta2 (approaching 0.9999) consistently yielding improved results'. Longer second-moment timescales stabilise the descent into the narrow basin that the cooldown is exposing. Plasma's 0.95 is the short end of the usual range.  
  gain: Comparable in size to picking the right cooldown shape, per the paper — plausibly another 0.5-1% loss reduction over the cooldown. Free.  
  risk: Low-medium. Changing beta2 mid-run means the existing v (second-moment) buffer was accumulated under a different timescale; the transition is smooth in practice but watch the logged grad_norm for the first ~200 steps after the switch. Do not go to 0.9999 on a 32,000-step cooldown — the second-moment window (1/(1-beta2) = 10,000 steps) would be a third of the cooldown; 0.99 (window 100 steps) or 0.999 (1,000 steps) are the safe picks here.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Swap the data mix for the cooldown window only. Over the final 32,000 steps (1.049B tokens), train on roughly 40% FineWeb-Edu (top-quality slice) / 25% code / 15% StackExchange+arXiv / 20% instruct-formatted data drawn from data/sft_shards_1.1_v2. 20% of 1.049B = 210M tokens, which is ~1.2 epochs of the existing 176M-token SFT set — a near-perfect fit. Keep the separate finetune_1.1_v2 SFT stage afterwards; this is in addition, not instead.**  
  why: MiniCPM's headline recipe finding is that mixing high-quality SFT data with pretraining data DURING the decay phase 'fosters more comprehensive model learning' and beats applying SFT only afterward. This is now industry standard (OLMo 2 Dolmino, SmolLM2 anneal mix, Hunyuan-Large's 'highest-quality 5%', Llama 3's 40B-token anneal). It is the single highest-leverage lever available for the specific failure modes measured on Plasma: 0% on executable code and multi-step reasoning, 10-15% on arithmetic. During cooldown the model is in the regime where it is consolidating rather than exploring, so distribution matters far more per token than during the stable phase.  
  gain: This is where the benchmark points come from, not from the LR curve. MiniCPM, SmolLM2 and OLMo 2 all attribute large downstream gains to the anneal mix. Expect the code/math/reasoning categories to move off zero far more from this than from anything else on this list.  
  risk: Medium. Two real risks: (1) contamination — the SFT shards were decontaminated against the benchmark (40 dropped), so re-verify decontamination before mixing them into pretraining, or the benchmark becomes dishonest; (2) over-weighting instruct data can degrade base perplexity — keep instruct at <=20% and keep a held-out general-web val split running through the cooldown so you can see if it regresses.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **BEFORE committing to a 7.8-day stable phase, run a 3-branch LR probe from the step-40,000 checkpoint. Each branch: 600-step linear ramp from 1.1641e-4 to the target, 4,400 steps constant, then a 1,000-step (1-sqrt) cooldown to 0. Targets: eta = 1.2e-4 (control), 2.0e-4, 3.0e-4. Compare POST-COOLDOWN validation loss, not stable-phase loss. Cost ~4 h per branch, ~12 h total. Couple weight decay to hold tau_ema fixed: lambda = 0.1 at 1.2e-4, 0.06 at 2.0e-4, 0.04 at 3.0e-4.**  
  why: The literature disagrees about Plasma's correct peak LR by an order of magnitude — StepLaw gives 1.42e-3 (or ~4.7e-4 after Adam sqrt-scaling from its paired 306K-token optimal batch down to Plasma's 32,768), the 2026 WSD-specific fit gives 1.40e-4, and comparable published models sit at 3e-4 to 5e-4. That is not resolvable by picking a formula. It IS cheaply resolvable empirically, and the reason to include a cooldown in each probe branch is that a higher LR always LOOKS worse at high LR (the eta-proportional hill term) — only the post-cooldown loss is a fair comparison. This is exactly the measurement WSD makes cheap. The weight-decay coupling comes from Power Lines: what matters is tau_ema = B/(eta*lambda*D), currently 0.26; leaving lambda at 0.1 while raising eta silently halves the optimizer's averaging window.  
  gain: If 1.2e-4 turns out to be 2-3x too low, this is worth far more than every other item here combined — the Marin 0.5B sweep found 'all the high learning rate runs (peak 8e-3) are significantly better than the low learning rate baseline (8e-4)', a 10x gap. If 1.2e-4 is already right, you have spent 12 h (6.4% of the remaining run) to eliminate the biggest open question in the recipe.  
  risk: Medium. Raising eta by 2.5x at step 40,000 is a genuine rewarm: expect a transient loss bump. Gupta et al. measured ~0.1 nats when rewarming a fully-converged Pythia-410M to 3e-4, and Plasma's case is milder (mid-run, LR already at 97% of peak, same data distribution, optimizer moments preserved) so expect <=0.05 nats recovering within a few thousand steps. Abort a branch if the logged grad_norm sits above ~2-3x its baseline for more than ~200 steps, or if loss does not re-cross its pre-ramp value within ~2,000 steps.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **If you decide to finish sooner than 320,000 steps, do NOT shorten the cosine — pick a horizon H and set decay_start = 0.9*H (or 0.8*H if H <= 200,000). Ready-made options: H = 200,000 -> constant to 180,000, cooldown 180,000-200,000 (20,000 steps, 13.3 h); total remaining 160,000 steps = 4.4 days. H = 160,000 -> constant to 144,000, cooldown 16,000 steps (10.7 h); remaining 120,000 steps = 3.3 days.**  
  why: This is the concrete cash value of WSD: the horizon becomes a decision you make on day 5 with data, not on day 0 with a guess. Chinchilla documented that a cosine cycle much longer than where you actually stop 'results in sub-optimally trained models' — under the current config, stopping at step 150,000 hands you a checkpoint at 60.1% of peak LR that has never been annealed and is strictly worse than running to 135,000 and cooling down over 15,000 steps, at identical wall clock. Use 20% cooldown at the shorter horizons because Hagele shows the required fraction shrinks with total length, so a 160,000-step run wants proportionally more than a 320,000-step one.  
  gain: Converts a bad stop into a good stop for free. Concretely, at H=160,000 you get a properly annealed, deployable, honestly benchmarkable 521M model in 3.3 days instead of 7.8 — and if it beats 43% you can still extend the stable phase later from the pre-cooldown checkpoint.  
  risk: Low. Per Schaipp/Defazio the optimal base LR scales as ~1/sqrt(T), so a stable LR tuned for 320,000 steps is slightly low for a 160,000-step run (their rule: halving the horizon warrants ~1.4x the LR). Not worth chasing unless the LR probe above already shows headroom.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Keep the last 4-6 cooldown checkpoints (save every 5,000 steps during the cooldown window instead of the current 5,000-step global interval, and raise keep_last_checkpoints from 4 to 8), then average them offline into a final 'soup' before SFT. Do the averaging on CPU — an fp32 copy of Plasma is 2.086 GB.**  
  why: OLMo 2 ships model soups of its anneal endpoints; Hagele et al. show stochastic weight averaging gives a significant boost on constant-LR runs (though it does not fully replace an explicit cooldown, so use both); WSM shows that checkpoint merging is mathematically equivalent to applying a synthetic decay schedule and beat a decay baseline by +2.04 average (+3.5 MATH, +2.9 HumanEval, +5.5 MMLU-Pro).  
  gain: Typically a small but reliable improvement on top of the cooldown — call it a few tenths of a percent of loss and a point or two on noisy downstream benchmarks. Costs zero training compute and zero training VRAM.  
  risk: Low. Disk only. Do not average checkpoints from across the LR discontinuity or from different data mixes — only average within the cooldown window.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do NOT restart pretraining from scratch to adopt these changes, and do NOT later resume a fully-decayed checkpoint at a high LR as your main line.**  
  why: The step-40,000 checkpoint is a stable-phase checkpoint (LR at 97% of peak) with intact optimizer state — precisely the case Hagele et al. call out as safe to continue from with a high LR without spikes or brittleness. Restarting would discard 1.311B tokens of training (~27 h of GPU time) for zero benefit. Conversely, the expensive-to-recover case in the literature is rewarming from a decayed endpoint: Gupta et al. measured a ~0.1-nat spike doing exactly that, and Ibrahim et al. show repeated cosine rewarm cycles cause forgetting, which is why infinite/constant-plateau schedules exist.  
  gain: Saves ~27 h of GPU time and avoids a rewarming loss spike entirely.  
  risk: None. The only discipline required is checkpoint hygiene: always retain the last pre-cooldown constant-LR checkpoint as the branch point, and use distinct checkpoint_prefix values for probe branches so scripts/run_rebuild_v2.py's auto-resume cannot latch onto a cooled-down checkpoint.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [low] **If you ever extend the run beyond the horizon you tuned for, scale the stable LR down rather than leaving it flat: rho ~= 0.53 for a 2x extension, rho ~= 0.38 for a 4x extension. Alternatively replace the flat stable phase with a shifted inverse-sqrt base and keep the same final cooldown (WSqD).**  
  why: WSD is horizon-free in the sense that you need not fix the END date, but the optimal stable LR still depends on total T — convex theory gives gamma* ~ 1/sqrt(T), and Schaipp/Defazio measured rho ~= 0.525 for 4k->8k steps and rho ~= 0.375 for 4k->16k. WSqD (2026) removes this dependence entirely with a minimax-optimal O(1/sqrt(T)) horizon-independent base, and a 0.5B/160B-token practitioner sweep independently found 'inverse square root + 20% decay is better than constant + 40% decay'.  
  gain: ~0.01 nats at the 100k-200k step scale on 124M/210M models per Schaipp/Defazio — small, but it prevents the stable phase from being silently mis-set if the run is extended toward a second epoch of the 7.52B corpus.  
  risk: Low, but this is the least-settled item on the list and WSqD is very recent. If you are not extending, a flat stable phase at a probed LR is fine and is what SmolLM2, MiniCPM and DeepSeek all shipped.  
  verdict: (unverified) —

The LOWEST ratio of any model in the set is Pythia-410M at 732:1 — still 37× above Plasma. In raw training compute (6ND): Plasma = 3.29e19 FLOPs vs Pythia-410M 7.38e20 (22×), MobileLLM-350M 2.07e21 (63×), SmolLM2-360M 8.69e21 (264×), Gemma-3-270M 9.65e21 (293×), LFM2.5-350M 5.88e22 (1,787×), Qwen3-0.6B 1.29e23 (3,912×). Plasma 1.1 has roughly 1/4000th of Qwen3-0.6B's training compute.**

Note ARC-Challenge 28.1% is statistically AT random chance (25%), and MMLU 31.5% is barely above the 25% floor.**

CORE 0.3168 (vs 0.2219 for d20); SFT → ARC-Easy 0.6797, ARC-Challenge 0.4991, MMLU 0.4049, GSM8K 0.1274, HumanEval 0.1280, ChatCORE 0.2734; RL pushed GSM8K to ~20%. This shows that 11× more compute at the same tokens/param ratio takes ARC-Challenge from chance (28%) to genuinely non-random (50%) and HumanEval from 8.5% to 12.8%.**

- **PEAK LR IS 4–30× TOO LOW. Every 2024–2026 model in the 300–600M class uses a peak LR in the 2.8e-3 to 4.0e-3 range: Ai2 DataDecide-530M = 2.8e-3, MobileLLM-R1-360M = 4.0e-3, MobileLLM-350M = 2.0e-3, SmolLM2-360M ≈ 3.0e-3. Only the 2023-era models (Pythia-410M 3.0e-4, TinyLlama 4.0e-4) sit near Plasma's 1.2e-4, and both are known-weak models. Correcting for Plasma's 28× smaller batch via the square-root scaling rule for Adam (Malladi et al., 'On the SDEs and Scaling Rules for Adaptive Gradient Algorithms'): 2.8e-3 / sqrt(917,504 / 32,768) = 2.8e-3 / 5.29 = 5.3e-4. Plasma's 1.2e-4 is ~4.4× below the batch-corrected reference point.**  
  DataDecide Table 2 row '530M | Batch 448 | Hidden 1344 | LR 2.8e-03 | Layers 16 | Heads 16 | Tokens 53.0B' with 2048-token sequences → 917,504 tokens/step. MobileLLM-R1: 'Peak learning rate: 4.0e-3'. MobileLLM: 'Initial learning rate 2e-3, cosine decay, Adam, weight decay 0.1'. Plasma config: learning_rate 1.2e-4, batch_size 32 × seq_len 1024 = 32,768 tokens/step.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2504.11393v2 , https://arxiv.org/html/2509.24945v3 , https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2402.14905_
- **MISSING STABILITY MACHINERY — this is WHY the reference models can run LR 3e-3 and Plasma cannot. Four of the modern small models use QK-Norm (RMSNorm on Q and K before the attention product): Qwen3 ('we remove QKV-bias used in Qwen2 and introduce QK-Norm to the attention mechanism to ensure stable training'), Gemma-3 ('replaces the soft-capping of Gemma 2 with QK-norm'), OLMo-2 (QK-Norm from Dehghani et al. 2023, 'avoids attention logits being too large, which can lead to training loss divergence'), MobileLLM-R1 ('QK-norm in self-attention'). OLMo-2 additionally uses z-loss regularization (Wortsman et al. 2023 / Chameleon) and reordered (post-)normalization. Plasma 1.1 has NONE of these: src/model/attention.py applies RoPE directly to raw q/k with no normalization; there is no z-loss term in src/train/train.py.**  
  Read src/model/attention.py lines 33-46: q/k come straight from nn.Linear → reshape → apply_rotary_emb → F.scaled_dot_product_attention, with no norm. src/train/train.py computes only F.cross_entropy with no auxiliary logit-regularization term. Qwen3 Technical Report §Architecture; Gemma 3 Technical Report; 2 OLMo 2 Furious §Architecture/Stability.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2505.09388v1 , https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.19786 , https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.00656_
- **INITIALIZATION BUG-LEVEL ISSUE: src/model/transformer.py::_init_weights applies a flat std=0.02 to EVERY nn.Linear and nn.Embedding, with no depth scaling on residual output projections. Standard GPT-2/Llama practice scales o_proj and down_proj by 1/sqrt(2·n_layers); at 26 layers that is 0.02/sqrt(52) = 0.00277 — a 7.2× reduction. Without it, residual-stream variance grows linearly with depth, which forces a lower usable LR and is exactly the failure mode that low-LR runs mask. The Smol Training Playbook lists init std 0.02 for SmolLM3 but that is the *input* std, with residual scaling applied separately; DeepSeek uses 0.006 at larger widths.**  
  Verbatim from src/model/transformer.py:
    def _init_weights(self):
        std = 0.02
        for module in self.modules():
            if isinstance(module, nn.Linear):
                nn.init.normal_(module.weight, mean=0.0, std=std)
            elif isinstance(module, nn.Embedding):
                nn.init.normal_(module.weight, mean=0.0, std=std)
No per-module fan-in scaling and no residual/depth scaling. Note also that down_proj has fan_in=3584, for which 1/sqrt(3584)=0.0167, while q_proj has fan_in=1280 → 1/sqrt(1280)=0.0280; a single flat 0.02 is not principled for either.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\model\transformer.py_
- **THROUGHPUT: 13,500 tok/s is ~28% MFU on an RTX 5080 and there is a real 1.6–2.1× available, but not 3×. Per-token fwd+bwd math for Plasma = 6 × 521.6e6 = 3.13 GFLOP plus ~0.41 GFLOP of attention at seq 1024 → ~3.54 GFLOP/token. At 13,500 tok/s that is 47.8 TFLOP/s of useful math; gradient checkpointing adds ~33% recompute → ~63.6 TFLOP/s of actual hardware work. RTX 5080 dense BF16 tensor peak ≈ 225 TFLOP/s (the marketed 450 figure is 2:4-sparse) → 28% MFU. For calibration, TinyLlama reported 24,000 tok/s for a 1.1B model on a single A100-40G = 158 TFLOP/s of a 312 TFLOP/s peak = 51% MFU, with FlashAttention-2, fused ops, and NO gradient checkpointing.**  
  RTX 5080 specs: 16GB GDDR7, 960 GB/s bandwidth, 336 5th-gen Tensor Cores; BF16 dense 225 TFLOPS / 450 with 2:4 sparsity (equivalently reported as 112.55 dense / 225.10 sparse by some spec aggregators depending on accumulate mode). TinyLlama paper: 'throughput of 24,000 tokens per second per A100-40G GPU'.  
  _src: https://flopper.io/gpu/nvidia-geforce-rtx-5080-16gb/spec-sheet , https://arxiv.org/html/2401.02385v2_
- **FOUR CONCRETE THROUGHPUT DEFECTS IN THE TRAINING LOOP. (1) No torch.compile — `grep -E 'torch\.compile'` over the repo hits only scripts/bench_throughput.py, never src/train/train.py. (2) micro_batch_size=2 × seq_len=1024 = 2,048 tokens per forward pass, far too small to saturate 336 tensor cores; nanochat uses 32×2048 = 65,536 tokens per device forward. (3) `accum_loss += loss.item()` sits INSIDE the `for micro_step in range(grad_accum)` loop, forcing a full CUDA device synchronization 16 times per optimizer step — at 2,048-token micro-batches the kernel queue cannot stay ahead of the CPU. (4) GQA expansion in src/model/attention.py does `k.unsqueeze(2).expand(...).reshape(...)`, and reshape-after-expand materializes a real 5× copy of K and V; PyTorch ≥2.5 supports `F.scaled_dot_product_attention(..., enable_gqa=True)` which skips the copy entirely.**  
  Grep result: only scripts\bench_throughput.py:38 contains `torch.compile`. src/train/train.py inner loop:
        for micro_step in range(grad_accum):
            ...
            loss.backward()
            accum_loss += loss.item()      # <-- device sync, 16× per step
            tokens_processed += x.numel()
src/model/attention.py:
            k = k.unsqueeze(2).expand(-1, -1, self.num_kv_groups, -1, -1)
            k = k.reshape(B, self.num_heads, KV_S, self.head_dim)   # forces a copy
Config: micro_batch_size: 2, gradient_accumulation: 16, seq_len: 1024.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py , C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\model\attention.py_
- **SCHEDULE: cosine is the minority choice among 2024–2026 small models; WSD (Warmup-Stable-Decay) is now standard and is strictly better for a compute-constrained single-GPU run. SmolLM2 (all sizes), SmolLM3, index-1.9B, and Falcon-E all use WSD. Decay fractions: SmolLM2-1.7B 10%, SmolLM2-135M/360M 20%, index-1.9B decays over the final 400B of 2.8T tokens (14%) from 5e-4 down to 5e-6. The operational advantage: the stable phase can be extended indefinitely without invalidating the schedule, so if throughput improves mid-run you simply keep training and decay later — impossible with a cosine schedule pinned to max_steps=320,000. OLMo-2 gets the same benefit via a stage-2 anneal-to-zero on 50B high-quality tokens plus model souping.**  
  SmolLM2 paper: '1.7B used WSD scheduler with 2000 steps warmup, learning rate 5.0e-4 and 10% decay'; smaller models 'trained using the WSD scheduler with 20% decay'. index-1.9B: 'Schedule: Warmup–Stable–Decay (WSD); Warmup: 100 steps; Decay range 5e-4 to 5e-6; Decay phase 400B tokens with increased curated data concentration'. Falcon-Edge: 'The training used the classic WSD learning rate scheduler for pre-training'. Plasma uses src/train/scheduler.py::CosineScheduler with max_steps hard-coded to 320,000.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 , https://arxiv.org/html/2607.09885v1 , https://falcon-lm.github.io/blog/falcon-edge/_
- **CONTEXT LENGTH 1024 IS THE SHORTEST OF ANY MODEL SURVEYED. Every comparable model uses ≥2048 during pretraining: MobileLLM 2048, TinyLlama 2048, Pythia 2048, SmolLM2 2048, nanochat 2048, MobileLLM-R1 2048 (pretrain) → 4096 (midtrain), SmolLM3 4096, Qwen3 4096 → 32,768, OLMo-2 4096. Plasma's finetune_1.1_v2.yaml also runs SFT at seq_len 1024, which truncates multi-turn chat and leaves almost no room for chain-of-thought — a direct structural cause of the reported ~0% on multi-step reasoning. Going 1024→2048 costs only ~13% more attention FLOPs at this model width (attention is ~11.6% of per-token FLOPs at 1024).**  
  Plasma configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml `max_seq_len: 1024` / `seq_len: 1024`; configs/finetune_1.1_v2.yaml same. MobileLLM-R1: 'Batch size 16, sequence length 2048' pretrain, 'batch size 4, sequence length 4k' midtrain, reasoning SFT at 32k. SmolLM3 'global batch size of 2.36M tokens with 4096 sequence length'. Attention FLOP share computed as 12·L·s·d per token / (6N) = 12·26·1024·1280 / (6·521.6e6) ≈ 0.13.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\configs\pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml , https://arxiv.org/html/2509.24945v3 , https://github.com/huggingface/blog/blob/main/smollm3.md_

### Recommendations
- [high] **FIX THROUGHPUT BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE — five specific edits to src/train/train.py and src/model/attention.py. (a) Wrap the model: `model = torch.compile(model, mode='max-autotune-no-cudagraphs')` after `.to(device)`. (b) Raise micro_batch_size 2 → 8 and drop gradient_accumulation 16 → 4 (same 32-sequence effective batch, 4× larger forward). (c) Move the loss accumulation off the hot path — replace `accum_loss += loss.item()` inside the micro-step loop with `accum_loss_t += loss.detach()` (a GPU tensor) and call `.item()` only at log_interval. (d) In src/model/attention.py, delete the `unsqueeze/expand/reshape` GQA block and pass `enable_gqa=True` to `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` (PyTorch ≥2.5). (e) Add `torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_tf32 = True` and run under WSL2 rather than native Windows to escape WDDM kernel-launch overhead.**  
  why: Measured 13,500 tok/s corresponds to ~63.6 TFLOP/s of hardware work (3.54 GFLOP/token × 13,500 × 1.33 checkpointing overhead) against an RTX 5080 dense BF16 peak of ~225 TFLOP/s = 28% MFU. TinyLlama achieved 51% MFU on a single A100 for a 1.1B model. The dominant losses are (i) no kernel fusion (no torch.compile), (ii) 2,048-token forward passes that cannot fill 336 tensor cores, and (iii) 16 forced CUDA syncs per optimizer step from `.item()` in the accumulation loop. The GQA reshape materializes a 5× copy of K and V on every layer of every forward AND every recomputed forward under checkpointing.  
  gain: 13,500 → 22,000–28,000 tok/s (1.6–2.1×). Concretely: 1.17B tokens/day → 1.9–2.4B tokens/day. This is the enabling change for every other recommendation, because it converts wall-clock into tokens. Do NOT expect 3×; 45–50% MFU is the realistic consumer-GPU ceiling.  
  risk: torch.compile adds 2–5 min of first-step compile time and can fail on the KV-cache branch — guard it by compiling only the training path, or set `model.forward` compile with `dynamic=False`. micro_batch 8 raises activation and logits memory: at seq 1024, vocab 48,000, the fp32 cross-entropy logits alone are 8×1024×48000×4 = 1.57GB plus a backward copy. If it OOMs, use micro_batch 4 (×8 accum) or switch to a chunked/fused cross-entropy. The repo's own comment records that disabling gradient checkpointing previously caused an allocator-pressure collapse to 9,700 tok/s at 97% VRAM — keep checkpointing ON and buy the speed from compile + batch shape instead.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **RAISE PEAK LR from 1.2e-4 to 5.0e-4 at the current 32,768-token batch. If you also adopt micro_batch 8 × accum 8 × seq 2048 = 131,072 tokens/step, use 9.0e-4. Keep min_lr at 10% of peak. Validate with a 3-point sweep of 2,000-step runs (3e-4 / 5e-4 / 8e-4) and pick on validation loss at step 2,000 — that costs ~1.5 hours total.**  
  why: Every 2024–2026 model in the 300–600M class uses 2.0e-3 to 4.0e-3: Ai2 DataDecide-530M = 2.8e-3 at a 917,504-token batch; MobileLLM-R1-360M = 4.0e-3 at 4M tokens; MobileLLM-350M = 2.0e-3 at 2.1M; SmolLM2-360M ≈ 3.0e-3 at ~2M. Applying the square-root scaling rule for Adam-family optimizers to Plasma's 28×-smaller batch gives 2.8e-3/sqrt(28) = 5.3e-4. Plasma's 1.2e-4 matches only Pythia-410M (3e-4) and TinyLlama (4e-4), both 2023-era recipes at 60× larger batches — i.e. Plasma is roughly 4–5× under-tuned even after batch correction. Only 3,000 of 320,000 steps (0.94%) go to warmup, which is fine; the peak itself is the problem.  
  gain: At a fixed 10.5B-token budget, correcting a 4× LR undershoot typically buys 0.05–0.12 nats of validation loss — roughly the same order as a 1.5–2× increase in tokens. Free: costs zero extra compute.  
  risk: HIGH divergence risk if applied alone. A 4× LR increase on a 26-layer model with flat std=0.02 init, no QK-norm and no z-loss is exactly the configuration that produces attention-logit blowup and loss spikes. Ship this ONLY together with the init fix and QK-norm below, and watch grad_norm (already logged) — sustained grad_norm > 2.0 or a spike above 10 means back off.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **ADD DEPTH-SCALED INITIALIZATION — a 6-line change in src/model/transformer.py::_init_weights. After the current flat init, rescale the two residual output projections: for every TransformerBlock, `nn.init.normal_(block.attn.o_proj.weight, 0.0, 0.02/math.sqrt(2*config.num_layers))` and the same for `block.ffn.down_proj.weight`. At num_layers=26 that is 0.02/sqrt(52) = 0.002774.**  
  why: src/model/transformer.py currently applies std=0.02 to every nn.Linear with no depth scaling, so residual-stream variance grows linearly with depth across 26 layers. GPT-2, Llama, and every reference implementation in this comparison scale residual-path outputs by 1/sqrt(2·n_layers). This is the single cheapest change that makes a higher LR survivable, and it is a genuine defect rather than a tuning preference.  
  gain: Enables the 4× LR increase without divergence. On its own, typically 0.01–0.03 nats and visibly lower early grad_norm; combined with the LR change it is the difference between a stable run and a spiky one.  
  risk: Essentially none. Requires a restart from step 0 (init only matters at t=0), which is the intended path anyway for the v2 rebuild.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **ADD QK-NORM AND Z-LOSS. QK-norm: in src/model/attention.py add `self.q_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim)` and `self.k_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim)`, applied per-head after the reshape/transpose and BEFORE `apply_rotary_emb`. Z-loss: in src/train/train.py add `z = torch.logsumexp(logits.float(), dim=-1); loss = loss + 1e-4 * (z**2).mean()`.**  
  why: QK-Norm is used by Qwen3 ('introduce QK-Norm to the attention mechanism to ensure stable training'), Gemma-3 ('replaces the soft-capping of Gemma 2 with QK-norm'), OLMo-2 ('avoids attention logits being too large, which can lead to training loss divergence'), and MobileLLM-R1. OLMo-2 additionally uses z-loss, citing Wortsman et al. 2023 and Chameleon, and reports it 'empirically improves run stability'. These two together are what license the 3e-3-class learning rates in the reference models. The coefficient 1e-4 is the PaLM/OLMo-2 standard.  
  gain: Not a direct quality win — it is the safety harness that makes the 4× LR increase realizable, and it removes the class of mid-run loss spikes that would otherwise cost days of a 9–25 day single-GPU run.  
  risk: Adds 2·26 = 52 tiny RMSNorm params (66KB) and ~2–3% forward cost. QK-norm must be applied per-head over head_dim (64), NOT over the flattened 1280 — getting the axis wrong silently degrades the model. The z-loss logsumexp over 48,000 classes in fp32 costs memory; compute it on the same fp32 tensor cross_entropy already materializes.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **REPLACE THE COSINE SCHEDULE WITH WSD. Rewrite src/train/scheduler.py: linear warmup for 2,000 steps → constant at peak for 80% of the planned budget → 1-sqrt decay (or linear) to 10% of peak over the final 20%. Critically, make the decay START STEP a config field independent of max_steps so the stable phase can be extended mid-run.**  
  why: SmolLM2 (all sizes), SmolLM3, index-1.9B and Falcon-E all use WSD; SmolLM2-135M/360M specifically use 20% decay. For a single-GPU run whose token budget is uncertain until the throughput work lands, cosine is actively harmful: it pins the entire LR trajectory to a max_steps value chosen before you know how fast the run will go. With WSD you keep the stable phase running and decide the decay point later. OLMo-2 gets the same effect via its stage-2 anneal-to-zero on 50B curated tokens.  
  gain: WSD and cosine reach comparable final loss when both are correctly sized, so the direct quality gain is ~0. The real gain is optionality worth 2–4× the token budget: if throughput doubles, you extend the stable phase and decay at 45B tokens instead of being locked into 10.5B.  
  risk: Low. WSD's stable phase sits at a high LR for a long time, so it amplifies any instability — ship it after QK-norm/z-loss/init. Keep checkpoints from before the decay begins so you can re-decay from a different point if needed.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **SPEND THE THROUGHPUT WIN ON TOKENS, NOT ON FINISHING SOONER. Target 45–65B tokens (86–124 tokens/param) rather than 10.5B. At 25,000 tok/s that is 21–30 days. Set max_steps against the token target, not the calendar: at 131,072 tokens/step, 45B tokens = 343,000 steps; 65B = 496,000 steps. Do NOT shrink the model to buy tokens.**  
  why: Chinchilla parametric fit at N=521.6M: the DATA term (410.7/D^0.28 = 0.641 at D=10.5B) exceeds the MODEL term (406.4/N^0.34 = 0.443) by 45% — the run is data-starved, not capacity-starved. Raising D to 45.4B drops predicted loss from 2.774 to 2.559 (−0.215 nats); 64.8B gives 2.519 (−0.255). Shrinking to 360M and training 62B tokens in the same wall-clock gives 2.582 — worse than 521M @ 45B, and it costs you parameters you would want back. Empirically, nanochat's d20→d32 jump (CORE 0.222 → 0.317, MMLU 31.5% → 40.5%, HumanEval 8.5% → 12.8%) spans roughly this loss delta.  
  gain: Interpolating log-linearly between the nanochat d20 and d32 report cards at matched compute, and discounting because Plasma scales only tokens (not params): expect MMLU ~34–36% (from ~31%), ARC-Easy ~48–52% (from ~39%), GSM8K ~7–9% (from ~4.5%), HumanEval ~9–11% (from ~8.5%) at 45B tokens with the LR/init/QK-norm fixes. That is a decisive win over Plasma 1.0 on knowledge and instruction-following; code and multi-step reasoning stay weak (see the SFT recommendation).

Mitigate with WSD (so any stopping point is decayable), checkpoint_interval 5000 with keep_last 4 (already configured), and a resumable dataloader — verify that StreamingShardDataset resumes at the correct shard offset, since the config comments reference a prior 'fixed dataloader' bug. Thermals and driver stability over 30 days on a 5080 are a real operational risk.

At seq 1024 the SFT config truncates multi-turn chat and leaves essentially no room for chain-of-thought, which is a direct structural cause of the reported ~0% on multi-step reasoning. Attention is only ~11.6% of per-token FLOPs at 1024 for this width, so doubling context costs ~13% throughput, not 2×.

- [medium] **SWITCH TO MUON FOR ALL 2D HIDDEN WEIGHTS, keeping AdamW for tok_emb/output and all RMSNorm weights. Starting points scaled from nanochat d20 (same 1280 width): Muon LR 0.02 with Nesterov momentum 0.95 and 5 Newton-Schulz iterations for the q/k/v/o and gate/up/down matrices; AdamW LR 0.2 for the tied embedding (or 0.004 if you keep it tied and treat it as the unembedding). Verify against your AdamW baseline on a 2,000-step run before committing.**  
  why: Muon's memory footprint equals SGD-momentum — strictly LESS than AdamW's two moment buffers, which matters at 16GB (AdamW currently holds 2 × 521.6M fp32 states ≈ 4.2GB). Measured speedups: 1.35× on the NanoGPT speedrun record, 23% wall-clock on a 1.5B model to GPT-2-XL HellaSwag (10 vs 13.3 8×H100-hours), 2× compute efficiency on MoE up to 16B. nanochat d20 — the closest published analogue to Plasma — runs exactly this hybrid. Overhead is T·m/B = 5·1280/131,072 = 4.9% at the recommended batch.  
  gain: 20–35% fewer tokens to reach a given loss, which compounds with the throughput work: effectively another ~1.25–1.35× on the token budget. Frees ~2GB of VRAM that can go into a larger micro-batch.  
  risk: Medium implementation risk — Muon is a third-party optimizer (single-file implementations exist in modded-nanogpt and nanochat) and the Newton-Schulz coefficients must be exact (3.4445, −4.7750, 2.0315). Overhead is 19.5% at the CURRENT 32,768-token batch, so this only pays off after the batch increase. If time is short, prioritize recommendations 1–6 first; Muon is the highest-value optional change, not a prerequisite.

Reserve the highest-density subset (FineMath-4+, Cosmopedia-v2, Stack-Edu, plus AugGSM8K-style data) for the final 20% WSD decay phase, exactly as SmolLM2 stage 4 does.**

Qwen2.5-0.5B scores GSM8K 33.4 vs SmolLM2-360M's 3.2 despite similar size — that gap is the data mixture, not scale. SmolLM2's stage-4 decay mix is 58% web + 4% Cosmopedia + 24% Stack-Edu + 14% math (FineMath4+, Infi-WebMath3+, AugGSM8K).

Stage B (reasoning): OpenMathReasoning + OpenCodeReasoning-2 + OpenScienceReasoning-2, 3–4 epochs, LR 8e-5, at seq 2048 (not 32k — you do not have the context). Raise the SFT LR in configs/finetune_1.1_v2.yaml from 1.0e-5 to 5.0e-5 and drop from 3 epochs to 2 for Stage A.**

MobileLLM-R1-950M, with only 4.2T tokens but reasoning-distilled SFT (6.2M samples, 4 epochs, LR 8e-5), scores HumanEval 46.3, MATH500 74.0, GSM8K 67.5. That is the entire difference: generic instruct data cannot produce executable code at sub-1B, and distilled CoT data can. Separately, Plasma's 1.0e-5 SFT LR is ~5× below SmolLM2's batch-corrected 5.3e-5 (3.0e-4 at a 1.05M-token batch, sqrt-scaled to 32,768).

Calibrating down from MobileLLM-R1-950M (which had 400× the pretraining compute), expect HumanEval 10–18% and GSM8K 10–20% rather than 46/67 — but that is still a categorical change from zero.

Implement as a schedule on gradient_accumulation, holding micro_batch_size fixed.**

Their batch-warmup method 'reduces the number of gradient steps used to train OLMo 1B to the same loss as the original run with 43% fewer gradient steps', doubling batch when CBS grows and scaling LR by sqrt(2). Plasma's fixed 32,768 is near-optimal at step 0 and far below optimal by step 300,000.
