  why: SmolLM3 and the Smol Training Playbook both list intra-document masking as a validated default to 'prevent cross-document attention noise'. Plasma's StreamingShardDataset packs a flat token stream into fixed windows with no boundary information, so tokens attend across unrelated documents. The damage is modest at seq 1024 but grows once you move to 2048.  
  gain: Small — typically 0.005–0.02 nats — but it is a permanent, free correctness improvement and it compounds with the context-length increase.  
  risk: Passing an explicit attn_mask disables the `is_causal` fast path in SDPA and can cost 10–20% attention throughput unless you use FlexAttention, which compiles a fused block-sparse kernel. Benchmark before committing; if it costs more than 5% end-to-end, skip it — it is the lowest-priority item here.

Embedding tying costs 0.2 points for an 11.8% parameter saving (MobileLLM Table 1) and is used by SmolLM2/3, Qwen2.5/3, Gemma-3, MobileLLM-R1 and Llama-3.2. 48,000 vocab spends 11.8% of parameters on embeddings, which is proportionate (Gemma-3-270M spends 63%). Chinchilla says 521M is the compute-optimal size for a ~10B-token budget and still reasonable at 45–65B. Architecture is not the problem; the token budget and the LR are.  
  gain: Zero — this is a negative recommendation. Its value is in NOT spending the next rebuild cycle on architecture churn that buys ~1 point while the 550× token gap and the 4× LR gap go unaddressed.  
  risk: None. The one caveat: head_dim 64 with only 4 KV heads (5:1 grouping) is slightly aggressive versus Qwen3-0.6B's 2:1 and SmolLM2-360M's 3:1, but the KV-cache saving is real for inference and the quality cost at this scale is within noise per MobileLLM's GQA ablation ('reducing kv-heads from 16 to 4 resulted in comparable accuracy... only 0.2 points accuracy drop with almost 10% model size reduction').  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **CALIBRATE EXPECTATIONS AND FIX THE EVALUATION. Adopt the standard benchmark suite the reference models report (CORE, ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, PIQA, MMLU, TriviaQA, GSM8K, HumanEval) alongside the custom 'overall %' metric, and report random-chance floors explicitly. Track validation loss in nats/token against the Chinchilla prediction (2.774 at 10.5B, 2.559 at 45B) as an early sanity check that the run is on the expected curve.**  
  why: The project's current honest numbers (1.0 = 43%, old 1.1 = 41%, ~0% code, ~0% multi-step reasoning, 10–15% arithmetic) cannot be compared to any published model because the metric is custom. The closest published analogue — nanochat d20, 561M params at 11.2B tokens with a BETTER toolchain (Muon, 2048 context, torch.compile, dedicated mid-training) — lands at MMLU 31.5%, ARC-Challenge 28.1% (statistically at the 25% chance floor), GSM8K 4.6%, HumanEval 8.5%. Plasma 1.1 as configured should be expected to land at or below that. Reporting a single blended percentage hides the fact that several sub-scores are at chance, which is exactly the information needed to decide where to spend the next 30 days.  
  gain: No model-quality gain, but it converts a 9-day gamble into a measurable one: a 2,000-step validation-loss check against 2.774-at-10.5B tells you within ~2 hours whether the LR/init/QK-norm changes are working, instead of finding out on day 9.  
  risk: None, beyond the engineering time to wire up lm-evaluation-harness. Note that MMLU and ARC-Challenge will remain near chance at this scale regardless — treat CORE, HellaSwag, PIQA, TriviaQA and ARC-Easy as the signal-bearing metrics for a 521M model, and MMLU/ARC-C/GSM8K/HumanEval as directional only.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Architecture choices for Plasma 1.1 (521M decoder-only, RTX 5080 16GB): what measurably improves quality-per-FLOP at ~500M scale, 2024-2026, ranked by gain/effort
### Findings
- **Plasma's aspect ratio (d_model/n_layers = 1280/26 = 49.2) is wider and shallower than EVERY comparable 2024-2026 small model. Measured reference points: MobileLLM-350M 960/32 = 30.0; SmolLM2-360M 960/32 = 30.0; Gemma-3-270M 640/18 = 35.6; Qwen3-0.6B 1024/28 = 36.6; LFM2-350M 1024/16 = 64 (but hybrid, 10 of 16 blocks are conv not attention).**  
  Computed directly from published config.json files. Qwen3-0.6B: hidden_size 1024, num_hidden_layers 28, 16 heads, 8 KV heads, head_dim 128, intermediate 3072, vocab 151936, tie_word_embeddings true, rope_theta 1e6. Gemma-3-270M: hidden 640, 18 layers, 4 heads, 1 KV head, head_dim 256, intermediate 2048, vocab 262144, sliding_window 512, pattern 5:1, rope_local 10000 / rope_global 1e6. SmolLM2-360M: hidden 960, 32 layers, 15 heads, 5 KV heads, intermediate 2560, vocab 49152, rope_theta 100000, tied.  
  _src: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B/raw/main/config.json ; https://huggingface.co/unsloth/gemma-3-270m/raw/main/config.json ; https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M/raw/main/config.json ; https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM2-350M/raw/main/config.json_
- **MobileLLM's depth-vs-width ablation at ~350M tests almost exactly Plasma's shape and finds it is the LOSING config. 32 layers / 896 dim / 14 heads = 49.8% avg zero-shot; 15 layers / 1280 dim / 20 heads = 48.7%. The deeper-thinner config wins by 1.1 points at matched (~340M) params.**  
  MobileLLM Table 9. At 125M the same trend: 4L/1280d = 43.3%, 12L/768d = 43.9%, 30L/512d = 44.8%, 42L/448d = 44.5% (all ~134-163M params). Note 30 layers beats 42 layers — depth has a sweet spot, it is not monotonic. Final configs: MobileLLM-125M = 30L/576d/9h/3kv; MobileLLM-350M = 32L/960d/15h/5kv.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14905 (MobileLLM, ICML 2024), Tables 8/9/11_
- **The depth-vs-width picture is genuinely contested and depends on whether you optimize LOSS or DOWNSTREAM ACCURACY. Kaplan et al. found loss is nearly invariant to aspect ratio (40x variation costs <3% loss). The 2025 'Panda' conditional scaling law fits d_model/sqrt(N_non-embed) ≈ 0.077–0.082 as optimal at 1B–3B (Panda-1B: d=2560, ffn=4096, 16 layers; Panda-3B: d=4096, ffn=4096, 28 layers) — Plasma sits at 0.0597, i.e. that law says Plasma is already too NARROW.**  
  Panda: >200 architecture variants, 80M–3B params, 8B–100B tokens; optimized Panda-3B beats LLaMA-3.2-3B by loss 2.619 vs 2.625, +0.6% avg over 9 benchmarks, +42% inference throughput. Kaplan: '(n_layer, d_model) = (6, 4288) reaches a loss within 3% of the (48, 1600) model'. Resolution: loss-optimal favors width, multi-step-reasoning downstream metrics favor depth, and Plasma's stated failure modes (0% executable code, 0% multi-step reasoning) are exactly the depth-sensitive ones.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2510.18245 (Scaling Laws Meet Model Architecture, 2025) ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2001.08361 (Kaplan et al.)_
- **CompleteP (NeurIPS 2025) shows the width:depth optimum under standard/muP parametrization is N:L ≈ 38.7 at 1.5B non-embedding params, and that scaling each residual branch by 1/L (alpha=1) makes deep-narrow models (N:L = 11.8) stay within 1% of compute-optimal while saving 11.8% FLOPs at the optimal shape and 34.4% at extreme depth.**  
  Paper defines N = width (residual dim), L = depth, P_non-emb = 12*N^2*L; sweeps P_non-emb in {50M, 300M, 1.5B} at 20 tokens-per-param on SlimPajama. Under alpha=1 the depth multiplier on LR becomes m_L^0 = 1, so 'optimal hyperparameters remain approximately constant when scaling model depth'. Applying their formula to Plasma (P_non-emb = 460.1M measured): the muP-optimal shape at N:L = 38.7 is d≈1142, L≈30 — Plasma is at 49.2.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.01618 / https://arxiv.org/html/2505.01618v2 (Dey et al., 'Don't be lazy: CompleteP enables compute-efficient deep transformers')_
- **Sandwich / Peri-LN normalization (RMSNorm on the OUTPUT of each attention and FFN block, in addition to pre-norm) is the single best-evidenced quality win at exactly Plasma's scale: at 400M params it gave +1.88 avg benchmark points and 0.09 lower loss vs Pre-LN, with more than half the seed-to-seed variance.**  
  Peri-LN Table 2: 400M — Pre-LN loss 3.43 / avg 49.69, Peri-LN loss 3.34 / avg 51.57. 1.5B — Pre-LN 3.29 / 53.71, Peri-LN 3.18 / 56.55. 3.2B — Pre-LN 3.20 / 56.69, Peri-LN 3.11 / 58.56. Post-LN is far worse everywhere (400M loss 7.46). Mechanism: Pre-LN residual variance grows exponentially in depth, Post-LN is constant (vanishing gradients), Peri-LN grows ~linearly. Gemma 2/3 ship exactly this as four RMSNorms per block: input_layernorm, post_attention_layernorm, pre_feedforward_layernorm, post_feedforward_layernorm. OLMo 2 calls it 'reordered norm'.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02732v1 (Peri-LN, 2025) ; https://arxiv.org/html/2503.19786v1 (Gemma 3 Technical Report) ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 (2 OLMo 2 Furious)_
- **QK-normalization (per-head RMSNorm over head_dim applied to Q and K before the attention logits) is now universal in 2025 frontier models — Gemma 3, Qwen 3, Llama 4, DeepSeek V3, OLMo 2 — and Gemma 3 explicitly REPLACED Gemma 2's logit soft-capping (attn cap 50.0, final cap 30.0) with it. Its main measured payoff is tolerating 1.5x+ higher learning rates without divergence.**  
  Gemma 3 report: 'the soft-capping of Gemma 2 is replaced with QK-norm'. OLMo 2: layer-norm reordering + QK-norm improved the gradient-norm spike score (fraction of >7-sigma outliers) from 0.108 to 0.069. Reported perplexity effect is small but positive (~3% PPL vs bf16 baseline in one QK-norm+cap study); models with QK-norm handled 1.5x higher LR without divergence. Cost: 2 * head_dim params per layer (for Plasma: 128 params/layer, 3,328 total — 0.0006% of the model).  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.19786v1 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 ; https://ishanjmukherjee.github.io/qk-norm ; https://sebastianraschka.com/llm-architecture-gallery/qk-norm/_
- **Plasma's initialization is materially wrong for a 26-layer pre-norm residual network: src/model/transformer.py:62-68 applies nn.init.normal_(std=0.02) to EVERY Linear and Embedding with no fan-in scaling and no depth scaling of residual output projections. down_proj has fan_in = 3584, so 0.02 is LARGER than the fan-in-preserving 1/sqrt(3584) = 0.0167 — the FFN branch amplifies variance at every one of 26 layers.**  
  Read of the file: `std = 0.02; for module in self.modules(): if isinstance(module, nn.Linear): nn.init.normal_(module.weight, mean=0.0, std=std)`. GPT-2/nanoGPT scale residual-writing projections by 1/sqrt(2*n_layer) precisely to keep residual-stream variance from growing with depth (nanoGPT: `std=0.02/math.sqrt(2 * config.n_layer)` on c_proj). OLMo 2 independently found their initialization change alone eliminated loss spikes in their ablation setting.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\model\transformer.py lines 62-68 ; https://github.com/karpathy/nanoGPT/blob/master/model.py ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656_
- **There is no z-loss anywhere in the training loop, and Plasma ties embeddings — the exact configuration where logit drift is most likely. OLMo 2 and PaLM both use z-loss with coefficient 1e-4 applied as coeff * mean(logsumexp(logits)^2).**  
  Grep of src/train/train.py shows only plain `F.cross_entropy(logits.view(-1, logits.size(-1)), y.view(-1))` at lines 216 and 283 — no auxiliary term. OLMo 2 uses '10^-4 * log^2 Z' regularization, adopted from Chowdhery et al. (PaLM), Chameleon, and Wortsman et al., 'empirically shown to improve run stability'. One study reports z-loss at 1e-3 has no impact on final quality — it is a free insurance policy, not a quality lever.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py lines 207-216 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2202.08906 (ST-MoE, z-loss origin)_
- **Training at ctx 1024 is NOT crippling Plasma — the 2025 SkyLadder result is that at a FIXED token budget, shorter pretraining context beats longer context on standard short benchmarks, and the gap is large.**  
  SkyLadder, 1B models / 100B tokens: 512-token context ~50-51% avg, 2K ~49%, 8K 46.3%, 16K ~44%. 'Models pretrained using shorter contexts always outperform long-context models, when assessed by their average performance across popular benchmarks.' Their fix is a schedule (grow 32 -> 8192 tokens at alpha = 1/8, i.e. +1 token per 8 steps, over ~64B tokens), giving +3.7% avg over 9 benchmarks vs fixed 8K, plus 13.1% (8K) to 22.2% (32K) wall-clock savings. Separately, I computed that for Plasma's exact config the FLOP cost of ctx 1024 -> 2048 is only +6.1% per token (1.111 -> 1.179 GFLOP/token; attention-score FLOPs are 6.1% of total at 1024 and 11.6% at 2048), so context is cheap but buys nothing on short benchmarks.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.15450v2 (SkyLadder, 2025) ; own FLOP computation from Plasma config_
- **Plasma's RoPE theta of 10000 is correct for context 1024 and raising it would be a small negative. At theta=10000 the longest RoPE wavelength is 2*pi*10000 ≈ 62,832 positions, ~61x the training context — every frequency band is already in the safe regime. Raising theta compresses the usable resolution of low-frequency bands for zero benefit at 1024.**  
  Gemma 3 keeps theta = 10,000 on its LOCAL (1024-token sliding-window) layers and only raises to 1,000,000 on global layers that see 32K-128K. SmolLM2-360M uses 100,000 for ctx 8192; SmolLM3 uses 1.5M for the 32K stage and 5M for 64K; SmolLM2-1.7B used 130k for its 2k->8k extension. Theta is a function of target context, not of model size.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.19786v1 ; https://huggingface.co/blog/smollm3 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 (SmolLM2)_
- **Plasma's GQA setting (20 heads / 4 KV heads, ratio 5, head_dim 64) is already in the safe zone and needs no change. The HuggingFace Smol Training Playbook's 1B / 45B-token ablation shows GQA ratio 4-8 matches full MHA, while ratio 16 and MQA (ratio 32) both degrade measurably.**  
  Playbook table (1B baseline, 45B tokens, ~1.2B params held constant): MQA 1 KV head — 'significant degradation across benchmarks'; GQA ratio 16 (2 KV) — 'noticeable decline'; GQA ratio 8 (4 KV) — baseline, matches MHA; ratio 4 (8 KV), ratio 2 (16 KV), MHA (32) — all equivalent. Recommendation: 'GQA with 4 groups preserves performance while being more efficient'. MobileLLM Table 11 agrees at 125M: 16 heads / 4 KV = 44.7% vs 16 heads / 16 KV = 44.6%, and recommends head_dim close to 64.  
  _src: https://gist.github.com/unclecode/e5da5fb6a1d37022b089e243e0d9e00e (full crawl of the Smol Training Playbook) ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14905 Table 11_
- **Tied embeddings are correct at Plasma's scale and untying would be a waste. The Smol playbook's 1B parameter-matched ablation found tied ≈ untied on HellaSwag, MMLU, ARC, PIQA, OpenBookQA. Plasma's embedding fraction is only 11.8% (61.4M of 521.6M), far below the ~21-35% where the question even becomes interesting.**  
  Playbook: tied = 1.2B params / 21.3% embedding ratio; untied 16-layer = 1.46B; untied 12-layer parameter-matched = 1.2B. 'Tied embeddings achieve comparable performance to untied variants despite having fewer parameters... Use tied embeddings for models under 1B to save ~17% on embedding parameters with no performance loss.' Embedding fractions I computed: Gemma-3-270M 62.6%, Qwen3-0.6B 26.1%, SmolLM2-360M 13.0%, Plasma 11.8%, MobileLLM-350M 8.9%.  
  _src: https://gist.github.com/unclecode/e5da5fb6a1d37022b089e243e0d9e00e ; own computation from config files_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Fix initialization in src/model/transformer.py::_init_weights. Replace the flat std=0.02 with fan-in scaling for residual-reading projections and depth-scaled init for residual-WRITING projections. Concretely, for d=1152 / L=32: q_proj, k_proj, v_proj, gate_proj, up_proj -> std = 1/sqrt(d_model) = 0.02946; o_proj -> std = (1/sqrt(d_model))/sqrt(2*L) = 0.02946/8.0 = 0.003683; down_proj -> std = (1/sqrt(d_ffn))/sqrt(2*L) = (1/sqrt(3072))/8.0 = 0.002256; tok_emb -> std = 0.02 (keep small, it is tied to the output head); all RMSNorm weights -> 1.0. If you keep the current d=1280/L=26 shape: o_proj std = 0.02795/7.211 = 0.003876, down_proj std = 0.016703/7.211 = 0.002316. This is a ~10-line change with zero runtime cost.**  
  why: The current code initializes down_proj (fan_in 3584) at std 0.02, which is 20% LARGER than the variance-preserving 1/sqrt(3584) = 0.0167, so every one of 26 FFN branches amplifies the residual stream. There is no 1/sqrt(2L) damping either, which GPT-2/nanoGPT introduced specifically so residual branch magnitude stays small relative to the identity path. OLMo 2 reports their initialization change alone removed loss spikes in their ablation. This is the cheapest correctness fix in the whole list and it is a prerequisite for safely raising the learning rate.  
  gain: -0.01 to -0.03 nats on its own, but its real value is as an enabler: it is what makes a 2-3x LR increase safe. Removes the tail risk of a mid-run divergence 5 days into a 7-day job.  
  risk: Requires restart from step 0 (it is an initialization). Low technical risk. Minor caveat: a smaller-init model converges slightly slower in the first few thousand steps before overtaking — OLMo 2 saw exactly this and compensated with other hyperparameters.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add z-loss to the training objective: `logits_f = logits.float(); lse = torch.logsumexp(logits_f, dim=-1); loss = F.cross_entropy(logits_f.view(-1, V), y.view(-1)) + 1e-4 * (lse ** 2).mean()`. Coefficient exactly 1e-4, matching OLMo 2 and PaLM. Apply to both the masked and unmasked branches in src/train/train.py (lines 209-216).**  
  why: Plasma ties embeddings and trains in bf16 with no logit control whatsoever — the exact setup where logit magnitude drifts upward over a long run and eventually produces a bf16 overflow or a near-one-hot softmax. z-loss is the standard cheap insurance (OLMo 2, PaLM, ST-MoE, Chameleon). One study found z-loss at 1e-3 has zero effect on final quality, so the downside is bounded at zero.  
  gain: ~0 quality on its own. Prevents a class of run-ending failure. Combined with QK-norm it is part of what lets you raise LR from 1.2e-4 toward 2.5-3e-4.  
  risk: Essentially none, and uniquely among these recommendations it does NOT require a restart — it adds no parameters and can be switched on mid-run. If the coefficient is set too high (>1e-3) it starts flattening the logit distribution and costs a little accuracy.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add QK-norm to src/model/attention.py: `self.q_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim); self.k_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim)` in __init__, then after the reshape to (B, H, S, head_dim) and BEFORE apply_rotary_emb, do `q = self.q_norm(q); k = self.k_norm(k)`. Per-head over head_dim (64), not over the full projection — this is the Gemma 3 / Qwen 3 / Llama 4 placement. Cost: 128 params per layer, 4,096 total at L=32.**  
  why: Every 2025 frontier model that trains from scratch at any scale now ships this: Gemma 3 (which explicitly dropped Gemma 2's attn softcap 50.0 / final softcap 30.0 in favor of it), Qwen 3, Llama 4, DeepSeek V3, OLMo 2. OLMo 2 measured the gradient-norm spike score dropping 0.108 -> 0.069 with reordered-norm + QK-norm. The direct quality effect is small; the leverage is that it removes attention-logit blowup as the binding constraint on learning rate.  
  gain: 0 to -0.02 nats at the current LR. The real gain is compounding: it is what makes LR 2.5-3.0e-4 (vs 1.2e-4) safe, which at a fixed 320k-step budget is worth substantially more than any single architectural tweak.  
  risk: Requires restart from step 0 (adds parameters and changes the function; injecting an identity-weight RMSNorm mid-run still rescales q and k magnitudes and would shock the model). ~1-2% throughput cost from two extra small kernels per layer. Do NOT also add logit soft-capping — Gemma 3's finding is that the two are redundant and stacking them is pure overhead.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Adopt sandwich / Peri-LN normalization. In TransformerBlock.forward: `x = x + self.post_attn_norm(self.attn(self.attn_norm(x), freqs)); x = x + self.post_ffn_norm(self.ffn(self.ffn_norm(x)))`, with post_attn_norm and post_ffn_norm as learnable RMSNorm(hidden_size). Also normalize the embedding output once: `x = self.emb_norm(self.tok_emb(tokens))`. Keep the existing final norm. Total added: 2*1152*32 + 1152 = 74,880 params (0.015% of the model). This is exactly the Gemma 2/3 four-norms-per-block layout (input_layernorm, post_attention_layernorm, pre_feedforward_layernorm, post_feedforward_layernorm).**  
  why: This is the best-evidenced quality change at Plasma's exact scale. Peri-LN at 400M params: loss 3.34 vs Pre-LN 3.43, avg benchmark 51.57 vs 49.69 (+1.88 points), with seed-to-seed benchmark standard deviation cut by more than half. The mechanism is concrete: Pre-LN residual variance grows exponentially in depth while Peri-LN grows linearly, and the gradient-norm bound gains a damping factor. Gemma 2, Gemma 3, and OLMo 2 all ship a variant of this. It also partially subsumes the depth-scaled init fix, so the two are complementary and safe together.  
  gain: -0.09 nats and +1.9 avg benchmark points, extrapolating the 400M row of Peri-LN Table 2 directly to Plasma's 521M. Expect the gain to be larger, not smaller, if you also increase depth to 32 layers.  
  risk: Requires restart from step 0. ~2-3% throughput cost (2 extra RMSNorm per layer). Keep the norm scale gamma LEARNABLE — the paper shows freezing it at 1.0 degrades performance. Slight VRAM increase from the extra activations under gradient checkpointing (negligible, <50 MB).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Reshape to hidden_size 1152 / num_layers 32 / num_heads 18 / num_kv_heads 6 / intermediate_size 3072, keeping vocab 48000 and max_seq_len 1024. This gives 508.4M total params (vs 521.6M now), 453.1M non-embedding, aspect ratio d/L = 36.0 (vs 49.2), head_dim 64, GQA ratio 3, MLP/attn ratio 3.00, and d_model/sqrt(N_non-emb) = 0.0541. If you prefer to keep ~521M exactly, use 1152 x 34 layers = 536.7M (d/L = 33.9). Both 1152 and 3072 are multiples of 128, so tensor-core alignment is preserved.**  
  why: MobileLLM's ~350M ablation tests almost exactly Plasma's current shape and finds it loses: 15 layers / 1280 dim / 20 heads = 48.7% vs 32 layers / 896 dim / 14 heads = 49.8%. Plasma at d/L = 49.2 is wider than every 2024-2026 reference small model (MobileLLM-350M and SmolLM2-360M at 30.0, Gemma-3-270M at 35.6, Qwen3-0.6B at 36.6). CompleteP's muP-optimal width:depth of ~38.7, applied to Plasma's 460M non-embedding params, gives d≈1142 / L≈30. 1152 x 32 sits at 36.0, inside the consensus band, and 18 heads keeps head_dim at 64 which MobileLLM Table 11 identifies as the sweet spot. Depth is specifically the axis that buys multi-step reasoning and code — the two benchmarks where Plasma currently scores ~0%.  
  gain: +0.5 to +1.0 points on the 43%-scale overall benchmark, weighted toward the reasoning and code subsets. MobileLLM's measured delta at ~350M for a much larger shape change was +1.1; Plasma is moving only part of the way, so I would not claim the full 1.1.  
  risk: Requires restart from step 0. Expect a 5-15% throughput LOSS: 23% more sequential layers with 19% smaller GEMMs, and Plasma is already only at ~25% MFU where kernel-launch and small-matmul overhead dominate. Budget the 7-day run becoming ~8 days unless you also land torch.compile. GENUINE COUNTER-EVIDENCE: the 2025 Panda scaling law fits d_model/sqrt(N) ≈ 0.077-0.082 as loss-optimal at 1B-3B, and Plasma at 0.0597 is already BELOW that — that law says go wider, not deeper. I am recommending deeper because (a) MobileLLM measured downstream accuracy at 125M-350M, which is Plasma's actual regime and objective, whereas Panda measured loss at 1B-3B, and (b) Plasma's specific failure modes are depth-sensitive. Do not treat this as settled — this is the lowest-confidence item in the top five.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add a fused / chunked cross-entropy that never materializes the full logit tensor: either Liger-Kernel's LigerFusedLinearCrossEntropyLoss, Apple's cut-cross-entropy (github.com/apple/ml-cross-entropy), or a hand-rolled chunked-over-tokens CE under torch.compile. Also move loss.item() out of the micro-step loop (accumulate a detached tensor and .item() once per optimizer step) at src/train/train.py:214.**  
  why: cross_entropy currently allocates [2*1024, 48000] fp32 logits = 393 MB plus the log_softmax saved tensor, roughly 1 GB of a 16 GB budget for one scalar. CCE cuts the loss-computation footprint from 24 GB to 1 MB on Gemma 2 2B and the classifier-head training memory from 28 GB to 1 GB; Liger reports ~84% peak-memory reduction on that layer. Freeing ~1 GB is what makes micro_batch_size 4 (better GEMM shapes at 4096 tokens per micro-step) or a 64k-96k vocabulary affordable at all. The 16 loss.item() calls per optimizer step force 16 full GPU syncs and are a pure throughput tax.  
  gain: ~1 GB VRAM freed; 5-15% throughput from removing the syncs and the logit materialization traffic. Enables micro_batch 2 -> 4 (tokens/step 32,768 -> 65,536, permitting LR ~1.7e-4 under sqrt scaling) and unblocks the vocabulary change below.  
  risk: Low. Adds a dependency (liger-kernel or cut-cross-entropy) — verify it builds on Windows/CUDA before committing; a hand-rolled chunked CE with no dependency is a safe fallback. No restart needed; this is loss-path only, mathematically identical output.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Keep rope_theta at 10000.0 and keep max_seq_len at 1024 for the main pretraining run. Do NOT raise theta and do NOT train at 2048 or 4096. Instead, if you want longer inference context, add a SHORT context-extension phase at the very end: the last 2-5% of tokens at seq_len 4096 with rope_theta raised to 100,000-500,000, plus intra-document masking for that phase only.**  
  why: At theta=10000 the longest RoPE wavelength is ~62,832 positions, 61x the training context — every frequency band is already comfortably in range, and raising theta at ctx 1024 only degrades the model's resolution on nearby positions. Gemma 3 confirms this by design: it keeps theta=10,000 on its 1024-token local layers and only raises to 1e6 on layers that actually see 32K-128K. On context length, SkyLadder's 1B/100B-token result is that shorter pretraining context WINS at fixed token budget: 512-ctx ~50-51%, 2K ~49%, 8K 46.3%, 16K ~44% average across 9 benchmarks. I separately computed that ctx 1024 -> 2048 costs Plasma only +6.1% FLOPs/token (1.111 -> 1.179 GFLOP/token), so the objection to longer context is not cost — it is that it actively hurts short-benchmark accuracy, which is Plasma's stated goal. Plasma's 1024 context is not crippling it; it is one of the few things that is currently right.  
  gain: 0 (this is a 'do not change' recommendation) — but it protects roughly 1-2 benchmark points that would be lost by naively 'fixing' the context length, and saves ~6-25% of the compute budget. The optional tail extension phase gets you 4K inference context for ~2-5% of total compute.  
  risk: None for the keep-as-is part. For the tail extension phase: freqs_cis is a non-persistent buffer sized to max_seq_len (src/model/transformer.py:53-57), so it recomputes on construction — you must rebuild it at the new length AND the new theta, and re-warm the LR for a few hundred steps. Also note src/model/rope.py uses the interleaved (Meta LLaMA) convention rather than HF's rotate_half, which is self-consistent but blocks HF weight interop if that ever matters.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do NOT change GQA, do NOT untie embeddings, do NOT add MoE, MLA, sliding-window/local-global interleaving, layer sharing, or logit soft-capping. Keep num_kv_heads at 4-6 with head_dim 64, keep tied embeddings, keep the dense FFN. Keep SwiGLU at roughly 2.67-2.80x.**  
  why: Each of these is a measured non-win or an active loss for Plasma's constraints. GQA: the Smol playbook's 1B/45B-token ablation shows ratio 4-8 matches full MHA while ratio 16 and MQA degrade; Plasma's ratio 5 is already in the safe band, and MobileLLM Table 11 agrees (16h/4kv = 44.7% vs 16h/16kv = 44.6%). Tied embeddings: the same playbook's parameter-matched 1B ablation found tied ≈ untied, and Plasma's embedding fraction is only 11.8% so untying would add 61.4M params for nothing. MoE: the 2025 tiny-scale study found dense wins at matched TOTAL params, which is exactly what 16 GB constrains, and Plasma is already at 97% VRAM. MLA / sliding window: Plasma's entire KV cache at ctx 1024 is 27 MB — there is nothing to compress. Layer sharing: +0.7-0.8 points at 125M but doubles compute per parameter, and Plasma is compute-bound not memory-bound. Logit soft-capping: Gemma 3 removed it in favor of QK-norm; stacking both is redundant overhead. SwiGLU ratio: the 8/3 = 2.67x convention exists to hold parameter parity with a 4x ungated FFN; the measured spread across every reference model is 2.67x-3.20x and Plasma's 2.80x is unremarkable.  
  gain: 0 by construction — the value is avoiding 4-6 weeks of implementation on changes that measure flat or negative in this regime.  
  risk: The one adjacent change worth considering later is Qwen3's trick of decoupling head_dim from hidden_size (Qwen3-0.6B uses head_dim 128 with hidden 1024, making q_proj 1024->2048 and pushing its MLP/attn ratio down to 1.50 vs Plasma's 3.50). Plasma's 3.50 is the highest of every reference model measured, and Panda's fit points lower. But the evidence for a specific target value is weak and the change is expensive (it perturbs every parameter count), so treat it as a future ablation, not a v1.2 decision.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [low] **Vocabulary 48,000 -> 64,000 (at d=1152: 508.4M -> 526.8M total, embedding fraction 10.9% -> 14.0%). Only do this if you land the fused cross-entropy first and are willing to retrain the SentencePiece tokenizer and re-tokenize all 7.52B tokens of corpus. Do NOT go to 96,000+ on 16 GB.**  
  why: Tao et al.'s vocabulary scaling law fits N_v_opt proportional to N_nv^0.83 and predicts ~60-67K optimal vocab at 300M non-vocabulary parameters; Plasma has 460M non-vocab params, extrapolating to ~86-96K, and overtraining past Chinchilla raises the optimum further. Their empirical validation showed 32K -> 43K improved ARC-Challenge from 29.1 to 32.0 at identical FLOPs. 64K is the conservative point on that curve: it captures most of the compression benefit (fewer tokens per document means more effective text per training step) without spending 20% of the parameter budget on an embedding table that each row of only gets updated a handful of times at 7.5B unique tokens.  
  gain: +0.5 to +1.5 points overall, mostly via better token compression (roughly 5-8% fewer tokens for the same text, i.e. ~5-8% more effective data per step) rather than raw capacity. Lower confidence than the paper's headline number because that was measured at compute-optimal training, and Plasma is 500x under-trained per parameter relative to SmolLM2-360M.  
  risk: HIGH cost, MEDIUM payoff — this is the worst gain/effort item I am still recommending. Requires: retrain tokenizer, re-tokenize and re-shard 7.52B tokens (hours to days of wall clock), restart from step 0. VRAM: at 64K, fp32 logits go 393 MB -> 524 MB and optimizer state +0.3 GB — safe only with fused CE, given the run already peaks at 15.87-16.30 GB. At 96K vocab the model is 563.7M params (9.02 GB of fp32 AdamW state) plus 786 MB of fp32 logits — that WILL OOM at 16 GB without fused CE, so flag 96K as VRAM-infeasible on this hardware unless the loss path is fixed first. If you are not going to retokenize anyway for other reasons, skip this and spend the effort on tokens instead.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Before spending any of the above effort: raise the token budget. Increase max_steps so the run sees 25-30B tokens (roughly 3-4 epochs over the 7.52B-token corpus) instead of 10.5B, i.e. ~800,000-900,000 steps at the current 32,768 tokens/step, or ~400,000-450,000 steps if you double micro_batch to 4 after the fused-CE fix. Raise LR to ~2.5-3.0e-4 at the larger batch (StepLaw gives 3.0e-4 for 350M at 0.5M-token batch; sqrt-scaled to 65,536 tokens that is ~1.7e-4, and QK-norm plus sandwich-norm plus corrected init buy the headroom to push toward 2.5e-4). Keep cosine to min_lr, or switch to WSD so you can stop at any point.**  
  why: This is architecture-adjacent rather than architecture, but it dominates the ranking and it changes how much the architecture work is worth. Plasma trains at 20 tokens/param; SmolLM2-360M trains at 11,111 and SmolLM3-3B at ~3,700. Muennighoff et al. show up to 4 epochs of repeated data produces negligible loss degradation versus fresh unique data, with meaningful gains out to ~16 epochs — so 3-4 epochs of the existing 7.52B corpus costs nothing but time. Every architecture change above is worth +0.5 to +2 points; going from 20 to ~58 tokens/param is worth considerably more than all of them combined, and the two compound.  
  gain: -0.15 to -0.25 nats validation loss, which at this scale should be several benchmark points — larger than the entire architecture stack. Combined with the architecture changes I would target moving from 41-43% to the low-to-mid 50s, though I would not stake a number on the exact figure.  
  risk: Wall clock: 2.5-3x longer. At 13,500 tok/s a 30B-token run is ~26 days; landing torch.compile plus fused CE plus SDPA enable_gqa (call it 1.5-2x throughput, from ~25% MFU toward 40-50% of the real 225 TFLOP/s BF16 peak) brings it back to ~13-17 days. That throughput work is therefore a prerequisite, not a nice-to-have. Repeated-data risk: past ~4 epochs the returns decay sharply, so do not push beyond ~30B tokens on 7.52B unique without adding corpus.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Optimal peak learning rate and batch size (and their joint scaling) for Plasma 1.1 — 521M-param LLaMA-style decoder, 1024 ctx, single RTX 5080, ~10.5–40B token budget
### Findings
- **DeepSeek's hyperparameter scaling law, evaluated at Plasma's EXACT compute budget, prescribes peak LR 1.12e-3 and batch 731,000 tokens — not 1.2e-4 / 32,768 tokens. Plasma's compute (C = 3.64e19) sits inside the law's fitted/validated range (1e17–1e20), so this is interpolation, not extrapolation.**  
  Eq. 1 of the DeepSeek LLM paper: eta_opt = 0.3118·C^-0.1250, B_opt = 0.2920·C^0.3271 (B in tokens), where C = M·D and M = 72·n_layer·d_model^2 + 12·n_layer·d_model·l_seq. For Plasma: M = 72·26·1280^2 + 12·26·1280·1024 = 3.476e9 FLOPs/token; D = 320,000 × 32,768 = 1.0486e10; C = 3.645e19 → eta_opt = 1.119e-3, B_opt = 731,161 tokens. I re-derived their own published models to validate the law: DeepSeek-7B predicted (4.25e-4, 9.22M tok) vs actual (4.2e-4, 2304×4096 = 9.44M tok); DeepSeek-67B predicted (3.12e-4, 20.7M tok) vs actual (3.2e-4, 18.9M tok). Both within 3–10%. The fit was made from models within 0.25% of minimum generalization error over C from 1e17 to 2e19, validated at 1e20.  
  _src: Bi et al., "DeepSeek LLM: Scaling Open-Source Language Models with Longtermism", §2.3, §3.1, Table 2, Eq. 1–2 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.02954_
- **The Step Law (Predictable Scale, 3,700 models) independently predicts peak LR 1.42e-3 and batch 306,000 tokens for Plasma's (N, D). Critically, Plasma sits at the extreme bottom corner of their sweep: their LR grid BOTTOMS OUT at 2^-10.5 = 6.9e-4 (5.8x above Plasma's 1.2e-4) and their batch grid bottoms out at exactly 32,768 tokens.**  
  eta(N,D) = 1.79·N^-0.713·D^0.307 and B(D) = 0.58·D^0.571 (both fit on N = 60M–1B non-embedding params, D = 2B–100B tokens, seq len 2048, cosine schedule, 2000-step warmup). Plasma's N_non-emb = 460,130,560 (computed exactly: 521,570,560 total − 48000×1280 tied embedding), D = 1.0486e10 → eta = 1.421e-3, B = 305,630 tokens. Plasma's (N=460M, D=10.5B) falls squarely between their 430M and 540M models at their 8B/20B data points — interpolation, not extrapolation. LR grid: "powers of 2, exponents -10.5 to -7.0" = 6.9e-4 to 7.8e-3. Batch grid: "32,768 to 4,194,304".  
  _src: Li et al., "Predictable Scale: Part I — Optimal Hyperparameter Scaling Law in LLM Pretraining" (Step Law) — https://arxiv.org/html/2503.04715v1_
- **Cross-checking DeepSeek's law against 9 published models shows every real model uses an LR between 0.36x and 5.5x of the predicted value (median ≈ 1.0x) and a batch between 0.21x and 1.08x of predicted. Plasma is at 0.11x on LR and 0.045x on batch — a simultaneous outlier on BOTH axes, further out than any published model on either.**  
  Computed table (predicted vs actual): Pythia-410M 7.70e-4 vs 3.0e-4 (0.39x), B 1.94M vs 2.10M (1.08x); Pythia-1B 6.92e-4 vs 2.5e-4 (0.36x), B 2.57M vs 2.10M (0.82x); SmolLM2-1.7B 4.20e-4 vs 5.0e-4 (1.19x), B 9.51M vs 2.0M (0.21x); MobileLLM-350M 6.48e-4 vs 2.0e-3 (3.09x), B 3.05M vs 2.10M (0.69x); SmolLM2-360M 5.45e-4 vs 3.0e-3 (5.51x); OLMo2-7B 3.86e-4 vs 3.0e-4 (0.78x); OLMo2-13B 3.47e-4 vs 9.0e-4 (2.60x); DeepSeek-7B (0.99x); DeepSeek-67B (1.03x). Plasma-521M: 1.12e-3 vs 1.2e-4 = 0.11x; B 731k vs 32,768 = 0.045x.  
  _src: Model cards / papers: Pythia (https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.01373, https://huggingface.co/EleutherAI/pythia-410m), SmolLM2 (https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1), MobileLLM §3.1 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14905), OLMo 2 Table 3 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656), DeepSeek Table 2_
- **Peer models at Plasma's parameter scale use LRs 2.5x–25x higher than 1.2e-4: SmolLM2-360M = 3.0e-3, MobileLLM-350M = 2.0e-3, OLMo 2 1B = 4.0e-4, OLMo 2 13B = 9.0e-4, Pythia-410M = 3.0e-4, and a Jan-2026 Shanghai AI Lab sweep found the optimal hidden-layer LR for a 550M model at 120B tokens = 1.05e-3. Nothing in the modern literature at this scale uses 1.2e-4.**  
  SmolLM2 paper: 135M and 360M "use the WSD scheduler with 20% decay and a learning rate of 3.0×10^-3"; SmolLM2-1.7B: peak 5.0e-4, WSD, 2000-step warmup, ~2M-token global batch, seq 2048, AdamW (0.9, 0.95). MobileLLM §3.1: "initial learning rate is set to 2e-3", cosine decay, Adam wd 0.1, 32 A100 × batch 32 = 1024 seqs × 2048 = 2.1M tokens, 480k iters × 1T tokens. OLMo 2 Table 3: 7B peak 3.0e-4 (batch 1024×4096 = 4.19M tok), 13B 9.0e-4 (2048×4096 = 8.39M tok), 32B 6.0e-4; Appendix B: "For OLMo 2 1B... We use a higher peak learning rate of 4.0·10^-4", warmup 2000 steps, cosine calibrated to 5T truncated at 4T. Shanghai AI Lab Table 7 ("Hidden Optim LR at 120B"): 500M → 1.05e-3, 1B → 6.99e-4, 2B → 5.80e-4, 3B → 4.79e-4, 4B → 3.78e-4 (Qwen3-MoE arch, 4M-token global batch, seq 4096, WSD, init std 0.02, AdamW β=(0.9,0.95), ε=1e-8).  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14905 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 (Table 3, App. B) ; Zhou et al., "How to Set the Learning Rate for Large-Scale Pre-training?" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05049_
- **32,768 tokens is 13x below the loss-minimizing batch size and 125x below the critical batch size for this (N, D). It is NOT harmful for step-efficiency — you get essentially perfect linear returns per step — but it does cost final loss, and the direct empirical evidence at Plasma's exact scale says roughly 0.02 nats per 4x you go below optimum.**  
  Power Lines (Cerebras, 400 μP-trained models, 111M–3.3B, TPP 20–1280, seq 2048): B_opt(D) = 0.0306·D^0.383 and B_crit(D) = 0.0471·D^0.462, both in SEQUENCES of 2048 → for D = 1.0486e10: B_opt = 431,464 tokens, B_crit = 4,110,291 tokens. Their Table 1 is a near-exact match for Plasma's regime — 610M model, 20 TPP, D = 12.2B, λ tuned per batch: B = 63 seqs (129k tok) → loss 2.579; 126 (258k) → 2.565; 252 (516k) → 2.563 (min); 504 (1.03M) → 2.570; 1008 (2.06M) → 2.592; 2016 (4.13M) → 2.637; 4032 (8.26M) → 2.733; 8064 (16.5M) → 2.891. The bowl is shallow on the left (−0.016 nats over a 4x reduction from optimum) and steep on the right. Kaplan's B_crit(L) = 2.1e8/L^(1/0.21) gives 826k tokens at L=3.2, 1.56M at L=2.8, 2.22M at L=2.6 — all far above 32,768. Zhang et al. (ICLR 2025) show CBS scales with data size D, not model size N (B* = 93.20·N^0.47 along the Chinchilla-optimal line; B* = 621.34·N^0.087 at fixed D — nearly N-independent).  
  _src: Bergsma et al., "Power Lines: Scaling Laws for Weight Decay and Batch Size in LLM Pre-training", Fig. 1, Table 1, §3 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738 ; Kaplan et al. §5.1 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08361 ; Zhang et al., "How Does Critical Batch Size Scale in Pre-training?" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.21676_
- **On a SINGLE GPU, raising the batch from 32,768 to 262,144 tokens is completely free in wall-clock. Throughput is fixed at 13,500 tok/s regardless of gradient_accumulation, so accum 16→128 changes nothing but the number of optimizer steps (320,000 → 40,000). This makes the batch-size fix a strictly-dominant, zero-cost change.**  
  Plasma's measured 2.4 s/step = 32,768 tok / 13,500 tok/s. The AdamW fused step over 521M params is ~15–25 ms, i.e. ~1% of a 2.43 s step at accum 16 and ~0.1% at accum 128 — so larger accumulation is marginally FASTER, never slower. Note the counterpoint from the small-batch literature: gradient accumulation is "wasteful" only when you have multiple devices/replicas; here there is one replica and one GPU, so accumulation is the only way to reach B_opt at all.  
  _src: Marek et al., "Small Batch Size Training for Language Models: When Vanilla SGD Works, and Why Gradient Accumulation Is Wasteful" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07101 ; measured throughput from the project brief_
- **Plasma's current (LR=1.2e-4, B=32,768, λ=0.1) accidentally sits at a near-optimal AdamW timescale — τ = 0.260 vs τ_opt = 0.223. That means you CANNOT change LR or batch in isolation: fixing one without re-deriving weight decay will move τ 8–15x off-optimum.**  
  Power Lines: τ = B/(η·λ·D), and τ_opt(TPP) = 1.084·TPP^-0.527 (fit across 111M–3.3B, generalizes over 3 orders of magnitude in compute; validated at 111M-1280TPP, 610M-320TPP, 1.7B-80TPP, 3.3B-30TPP). Plasma TPP = 1.0486e10/5.2157e8 = 20.1 → τ_opt = 0.2229. Current config: τ = 32768/(1.2e-4 × 0.1 × 1.0486e10) = 0.2604 (17% high — fine). If you raise B to 262,144 and keep η=1.2e-4, λ=0.1 → τ = 2.08 (9x too high). If you raise η to 1.0e-3 and keep B=32,768, λ=0.1 → τ = 0.031 (7x too low). Power Lines Finding 2: "With AdamW, we should adjust λ, not η, as B changes" and λ_opt scales LINEARLY with B for fixed N, D, η.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738 (Eq. 3–4, Fig. 1 left, Fig. 2, Finding 1–2); Wang & Aitchison, "How to set AdamW's weight decay as you scale model and dataset size" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.13698_
- **β2 = 0.95 is badly mistuned for a 32,768-token batch. It gives a second-moment EMA half-life of only 0.44M tokens — 60x shorter than what every reference model uses (Pythia 28M, SmolLM2 27M, DeepSeek-7B 127M tokens). This injects avoidable gradient noise and is a free fix.**  
  β2 half-life in optimizer steps = ln2 / (−ln β2) = 13.51 steps at β2=0.95; ×32,768 tokens = 442,800 tokens. At B=2.1M (Pythia/SmolLM2) the same β2 gives 28.3M tokens; at DeepSeek-7B's 9.44M-token batch it gives 127M tokens. The correct scaling rule is to hold the β2 half-life fixed IN TOKENS: β2* = β2^(B*/B). Targeting a 20M-token half-life gives β2 = 0.99886 at B=32,768, 0.99547 at 131,072, 0.99096 at 262,144, 0.98199 at 524,288. The paper also confirms β1 = 0.9 is fine across all batch sizes and that LR scales SUB-square-root with batch: over a 1024x batch increase the sqrt rule predicts 32x LR increase, but "a factor of only about 3 empirically works better" (exponent ≈ log3/log1024 = 0.16).  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07101 (Eq. 2 and §on β2); Malladi et al., "On the SDEs and Scaling Rules for Adaptive Gradient Algorithms" (√B rule for Adam, and scale β1,β2 → 1−δ(1−β)) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.10287_
- **Below the critical batch size, the optimal LR is set by STABILITY, not by batch size — it does not scale with B. This resolves the apparent contradiction between "√B says 1.2e-4 is right for 32k" and "the scaling laws say 1.1e-3": the √B rule is a rule for moving ABOVE B_crit, not for shrinking below B_opt.**  
  Power Lines, Finding 1 discussion: "If η is tuned instead, η_opt fails to scale with B up to B_crit; we observe instead a certain maximum η, above which training becomes unstable... Consequently, η has less flexibility to scale with B; training stability is more fundamental than timescale." Table 1 confirms this empirically: at 610M/20TPP the same μP base LR (1.6e-2, i.e. a fixed η) is within 0.004 nats of the per-B tuned η across B = 63 → 2016 sequences (a 32x batch range). Corroborating evidence from real models: SmolLM2-1.7B runs 5.0e-4 at a 2M-token batch that is 4.7x SMALLER than DeepSeek's predicted B_opt of 9.5M — yet uses an LR 1.19x ABOVE the predicted value, not below.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738 §2.3, Table 1; https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1_
- **The current schedule (cosine to 10% of peak) is the wrong shape. Linear decay-to-zero beats cosine-to-10x by 1.6–3.05% in loss at matched, optimally-tuned peak LR, and the advantage is largest exactly in Plasma's situation — small batch and/or high tokens-per-parameter. D2Z also makes the optimal peak LR far more robust to batch-size and dataset-size changes, which de-risks the LR jump.**  
  Cerebras study, hundreds of models, 111M–1.7B, datasets to 137B tokens: "when all schedules use their optimal peak LR, linear decay-to-zero (D2Z) works best at compute-optimal TPP"; D2Z exceeds the best 10x setting "by 1.6% at 200 TPP" (111M) and "roughly 3.05% lower loss than 10× at the proxy-tuned η" (1.7B, 80 TPP); "an 80 TPP D2Z model can surpass a 200 TPP 10× model in validation loss" (≈2.5x compute saving); "with higher TPP or smaller batches, a vanishing LR works best"; "when dataset or batch size changes, optimal peak LRs are much more stable when using D2Z compared to using lesser decay." They also find linear D2Z > cosine D2Z > cosine-10x > linear-10x > WSD > InvSqrt > constant.  
  _src: Bergsma et al., "Straight to Zero: Why Linearly Decaying the Learning Rate to Zero Works Best for LLMs" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15938_
- **muP is NOT the right call here, and Plasma's existing flat std=0.02 init is already the OLMo 2-validated choice for width transfer and stability. A Jan-2026 head-to-head at 4B and 12B scale found direct LR fitting beats μTransfer on downstream benchmarks.**  
  Zhou et al. 2026 trained a 4B (530M active) and a 12B model from scratch on 500B tokens comparing μTransfer (proxy tuned at 2B params) against a directly-fit LR law, and report "the model trained via the Fitting Paradigm consistently and significantly outperforms the one using μTransfer" on MMLU/CMMLU throughout training; "Our empirical results challenge the scalability of the widely adopted μTransfer in large-scale pre-training scenarios." They also found module-wise LRs give no gain over a single global LR ("the loss curves for both settings are virtually indistinguishable"). Separately, OLMo 2 §3.2 argues the flat init that Plasma already uses is superior: "we initialize every parameter from a normal distribution with a mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 0.02" — better growth exponent λ (activation/gradient norms flat across layers) and gradient norm positively correlated with √d_model, which per Yang et al. is the sufficient condition for width transfer with a width-independent LR. Note: for reference, if you DID adopt μP, Cerebras' proxy-tuned base LR is η̃ = 1.6e-2 at proxy width d_p = 256, and η = (d_p/d_t)·η̃ = (256/1280)·1.6e-2 = 3.2e-3 for width 1280.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05049 §5, §6.1 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 §3.2 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13738 App. C ; Yang et al., μTransfer — https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.03466_
- **src/train/train.py forces a GPU→CPU sync on every micro-step via accum_loss += loss.item() inside the gradient-accumulation loop. This is a real throughput tax today (16 syncs/step) and would become 64–128 syncs/step under the recommended larger batch. betas are also hardcoded (0.9, 0.95), so the β2 fix requires a code edit, not a config edit.**  
  src/train/train.py, inner loop: `loss.backward()` then `accum_loss += loss.item()` — .item() blocks the CPU until the backward completes, serializing the H2D pipeline every 2048 tokens. Optimizer construction: `torch.optim.AdamW(..., betas=(0.9, 0.95), fused=device.type=='cuda')` — β2 is a literal. src/train/scheduler.py `CosineScheduler.get_lr` implements cosine to `min_lr` with no linear/D2Z option. Fix: accumulate `accum_loss_t += loss.detach()` on-GPU and call `.item()` only at `log_interval`.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py ; C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\scheduler.py_

### Recommendations
- [high] **PRIMARY (do all four together — they are one coupled change). In configs/pretrain_1.1_rebuild.yaml (and pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml): learning_rate: 1.2e-4 → 1.0e-3 ; micro_batch_size: 2 → 4 ; gradient_accumulation: 16 → 64 ; batch_size: 32 → 256 (= 262,144 tokens/step) ; max_steps: 320000 → 40000 ; warmup_steps: 3000 → 750 ; weight_decay: 0.1 → 0.112 ; min_lr: 1.2e-5 → 0.0. Total tokens is unchanged at 10.49B and wall-clock is unchanged at ~9 days.**  
  why: Three independent, at-scale scaling laws converge on this region for Plasma's exact (N, D, C): DeepSeek (1.12e-3, 731k tok), Step Law (1.42e-3, 306k tok), Power Lines B_opt (431k tok). 1.0e-3 is deliberately at the conservative end of that cluster. B = 262,144 tokens sits between Step Law's B_opt (306k) and Power Lines' B_opt (431k), is 15.7x below B_crit (4.11M tok), and — crucially — costs nothing in wall-clock because throughput on one GPU is token-rate-bound, not step-bound. weight_decay 0.112 is the value that puts the AdamW timescale τ = B/(ηλD) at exactly τ_opt = 1.084·TPP^-0.527 = 0.2229 for TPP = 20.1; leaving λ at 0.1 gives τ = 0.250, also fine. warmup_steps 750 = 197M tokens = 1.9% of budget (vs OLMo 2's 2000 steps and DeepSeek's 2000 steps at much larger batch).  
  gain: 0.06–0.15 nats lower final pretrain loss vs the current config, i.e. the same result you would otherwise need 1.4x–2.3x more tokens (13–21 days instead of 9) to reach. On downstream evals this is the difference between 'another 41–43% run' and a genuinely better model — the 0% on executable code and multi-step reasoning at 1.0/old-1.1 is exactly the kind of capability that only appears once loss crosses a threshold.  
  risk: Loss spikes at 1.0e-3. The LR bowl is asymmetric: below-optimal costs slowly, above-optimal hits an instability cliff. Mitigations: keep max_grad_norm 1.0; monitor grad_norm (logged already) — if it sustains >2x its post-warmup baseline or you see a spike that doesn't recover within ~200 steps, restart from the last checkpoint at 7.0e-4. Also verify micro_batch_size 4 fits: rough budget is 8.35GB optimizer+params+grads (fp32 master) + ~1.2GB activations w/ checkpointing + ~1.2GB logits (4×1024×48000) ≈ 10.8GB of ~17.1GB — should fit, but if it OOMs use micro_batch 2 / accum 128 (identical math, same throughput).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Change src/train/scheduler.py from cosine-to-10% to LINEAR decay-to-zero. Replace the decay branch with: return self.min_lr + (1.0 - decay_ratio) * (self.lr - self.min_lr), and set min_lr: 0.0 in the config. Keep the existing linear warmup.**  
  why: Bergsma et al. (Cerebras), hundreds of models 111M–1.7B: at matched optimally-tuned peak LR, linear D2Z beats cosine-to-10x by 1.6% (111M @ 200 TPP) up to 3.05% (1.7B @ 80 TPP) in loss, and beats WSD and cosine-D2Z as well. They explicitly note the benefit grows with higher TPP AND with smaller batches — Plasma is small-batch, and if throughput improves you will be at 40–80 TPP. Second-order benefit that matters here: 'when dataset or batch size changes, optimal peak LRs are much more stable when using D2Z' — D2Z widens the safe LR window, which directly de-risks the jump from 1.2e-4 to 1.0e-3.  
  gain: 1.6–3% lower loss at zero cost; Cerebras report an 80-TPP D2Z run matching a 200-TPP 10x-decay run, i.e. up to ~2.5x compute-equivalent at high TPP. At Plasma's 20 TPP expect the low end (~1.5%, ≈0.04 nats).  
  risk: Very low. One caveat: with min_lr = 0 the very last checkpoints are the only usable ones — you lose the ability to extend the run. If you want extendability instead, use WSD: constant LR for the first 80% of steps then linear to zero over the last 20% (SmolLM2-360M's exact recipe: WSD with 20% decay). WSD scales 'predictably and reliably similar to cosine' per Hägele et al. (https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18392) but is ~0.5–1% behind linear D2Z in the Cerebras study.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **In src/train/train.py, make AdamW betas configurable and set betas: [0.9, 0.99] for the 262,144-token batch. Concretely: replace the hardcoded betas=(0.9, 0.95) with betas=tuple(train_cfg.get('betas', [0.9, 0.99])). General rule to use at any batch size: beta2 = exp(-ln2 · B_tokens / 2e7), i.e. hold the β2 half-life at 20M tokens. That gives 0.9989 @ 32,768 tok, 0.9955 @ 131,072, 0.9910 @ 262,144, 0.9820 @ 524,288, 0.95 @ ~2.1M.**  
  why: β2's EMA horizon is meaningful in TOKENS, not steps. β2=0.95 is a convention borrowed from runs with 2–9M-token batches (Pythia 28M-token half-life, SmolLM2 27M, DeepSeek-7B 127M). At Plasma's 32,768-token batch it gives a 0.44M-token half-life — 60x too short, so the second-moment estimate is estimated from ~13 noisy micro-batches and effectively adds gradient noise to every update. The scaling rule β2* = β2^(B*/B) (hold the token half-life fixed) is exactly what Marek et al. derive and validate across batch sizes spanning 4 orders of magnitude. β1 = 0.9 is confirmed correct at all batch sizes and should NOT change.  
  gain: Small but free: mainly reduced update noise and fewer spikes, which is what buys headroom for the 1.0e-3 LR. Marek et al. show correcting β2 is what makes small-batch training 'stable and consistently more robust to hyperparameter choices'.  
  risk: Low. β2=0.99 is a well-trodden value (used by many <1B runs). Do NOT combine β2=0.999 with a large batch — at 262,144 tokens that would be a 182M-token half-life, far too sluggish. Ensure bias correction is on (PyTorch AdamW does this by default).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **FALLBACK — if you must keep the 32,768-token batch (e.g. resuming an in-flight run, or micro_batch/accum can't change): learning_rate: 6.0e-4 (not 1.2e-4), weight_decay: 0.023 (not 0.1), betas: [0.9, 0.999], warmup_steps: 6000, min_lr: 0.0 with linear D2Z, max_steps unchanged at 320000.**  
  why: Even at B = 32,768 the batch is 125x below B_crit, and Power Lines shows η_opt does not scale down with B below B_crit — it is capped by stability, not by batch. 6.0e-4 is 5x above the current value and still 1.9x below DeepSeek's η_opt, deliberately conservative for the noisier small-batch gradient. The weight decay MUST come down with it: τ = B/(ηλD) = 32768/(6e-4 · 0.1 · 1.0486e10) = 0.052 with λ=0.1, which is 4.3x below τ_opt = 0.223; λ = 0.023 restores τ = 0.223 exactly. β2 = 0.999 gives the 22.7M-token half-life that matches Pythia/SmolLM2 practice at this batch. Warmup 6000 steps = 197M tokens, same token budget as the primary recommendation.  
  gain: Recovers most of the LR gain (est. 0.03–0.10 nats) while leaving the ~0.05 nats of batch-size penalty on the table. Roughly 1.2–1.6x compute-equivalent.  
  risk: Higher spike risk than the primary option because a 32k-token batch gives a noisier gradient at the same LR. The λ = 0.023 change is the least-tested piece of this recommendation (it follows Power Lines' τ law, which was fit with μP; Plasma uses standard parametrization). If you are uneasy, use λ = 0.05 and η = 6.0e-4 — τ = 0.104, half of optimal, which is much closer than the 0.052 you'd get by leaving λ at 0.1.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Use this lookup table for any (batch, tokens) combination, at D ≈ 10.5B. Rule: eta(B) = 1.0e-3 · (B/262144)^0.16 ; lambda(B) = B / (0.2229 · eta · D) ; beta2(B) = exp(-ln2·B/2e7). B=32,768 → η 7.2e-4, λ 0.020, β2 0.9989, 320,007 steps, warmup 6100. B=65,536 → η 8.0e-4, λ 0.035, β2 0.9977, 160,003 steps, warmup 3050. B=131,072 → η 9.0e-4, λ 0.063, β2 0.9955, 80,001 steps, warmup 1550. B=262,144 → η 1.0e-3, λ 0.112, β2 0.9910, 40,000 steps, warmup 750. B=524,288 → η 1.1e-3, λ 0.201, β2 0.9820, 20,000 steps, warmup 400. B=1,048,576 → η 1.25e-3, λ 0.359, β2 0.9643, 10,000 steps, warmup 200.**  
  why: The B^0.16 exponent is the empirically measured LR-vs-batch coupling from Marek et al. (a 3x LR change over a 1024x batch range), which is far weaker than the √B rule (Malladi et al.) and reflects that below B_crit the LR is stability-limited rather than noise-limited. The λ(B) formula is Power Lines' τ law (λ_opt scales linearly with B at fixed η, D — verified stable over their B ∈ [63, 2016] sequence range). Warmup is held at ~200M tokens (1.9% of budget) in all rows. Using this table means you can pick a batch size for hardware reasons and still be on the joint optimum.  
  gain: Keeps you within the DeepSeek 'near-optimal band' (within 0.25% of minimum loss) for any batch you choose, rather than being 9x off on one axis and 22x off on the other.  
  risk: The B^0.16 exponent is the flattest defensible reading; if you prefer the √B rule you would get 3.5e-4 at 32k and 1.4e-3 at 512k instead. For B ≥ 524,288 the λ values (0.20–0.36) are unusually large by convention; they are what the τ law implies, but if a run looks over-regularized (train loss tracking val loss too tightly, slow late-training progress) cut λ to 0.1 and accept the τ mismatch.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **If throughput improves (the brief notes 56 TFLOP/s vs ~450 peak, so 2–3x headroom) and D grows: at D = 20B tokens use η = 1.0e-3, B = 524,288 tokens, λ = 0.165, β2 = 0.982, 38,146 steps. At D = 40B use η = 9.5e-4, B = 786,432 tokens, λ = 0.188, β2 = 0.973, 50,862 steps. Do NOT keep λ = 0.1 when D changes.**  
  why: DeepSeek's η_opt = 0.3118·C^-0.125 falls slowly with compute (1.12e-3 → 1.03e-3 → 9.5e-4 across D = 10.5/20/40B), while both B_opt laws rise (Power Lines: 431k → 553k → 721k tokens; DeepSeek: 731k → 903k → 1.13M). Meanwhile τ_opt = 1.084·TPP^-0.527 falls sharply (0.223 → 0.159 → 0.110 as TPP goes 20 → 38 → 77), so λ_opt must RISE. Note also that at 20–40B tokens against a 7.52B-token corpus you are at 2.7–5.3 epochs; Muennighoff et al. (https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16264) find repeated data is nearly as good as fresh up to ~4 epochs, so 20B is safe and 40B is at the edge.  
  gain: Keeps the run on the joint optimum as the token budget grows, rather than drifting further off as TPP rises. Also note the Straight-to-Zero result that D2Z's advantage over 10x-decay grows with TPP, so the linear-D2Z change becomes worth more at 20–40B tokens.  
  risk: The τ law was fit on single-epoch training; Wang & Aitchison observed different optimal τ under multi-epoch training with data repetition, so at 2.7–5.3 epochs the λ values are less certain. Bias λ downward (0.12–0.15 rather than 0.165–0.19) if you repeat data heavily.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Add two cheap stability guards before committing 9 days at 1.0e-3: (a) QK-norm — RMSNorm on q and k after the projections in src/model/attention.py, ~0 params and <1% compute; (b) z-loss with coefficient 1e-4 added to the cross-entropy (loss += 1e-4 * logsumexp(logits, -1).pow(2).mean()). Keep max_grad_norm at 1.0 and keep the flat std=0.02 init exactly as-is.**  
  why: OLMo 2 §2.1 adopts both specifically to survive higher peak LRs: QK-norm 'avoids attention logits being too large, which can lead to training loss divergence', and z-loss 'has been empirically shown to improve run stability'. They run 9.0e-4 at 13B with these in place. Plasma has neither, has no QK-norm, and is about to run 1.0e-3. Conversely, do NOT touch the initialization: OLMo 2 §3.2 shows their flat std=0.02-for-everything scheme (identical to Plasma's _init_weights in src/model/transformer.py) has a growth exponent nearer 0 and gradient norms better correlated with √d_model than depth-scaled (1/√(2·d·layer_idx)) init — i.e. Plasma's init is already the stability-optimal choice and adding a 1/√(2L) residual scaling would be a step backwards.  
  gain: Removes most of the divergence risk of the LR increase. Not a loss improvement per se — an insurance policy on a 9-day single-GPU run where a divergence at step 250k costs days.  
  risk: QK-norm changes the architecture, so old 1.1 checkpoints are not resumable — this must be a fresh run. QK-norm adds two RMSNorm ops per layer (26×2 = 52 extra norms) for maybe 0.5–1% throughput. z-loss is architecture-neutral and resumable; if you only do one, do z-loss.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Before the 9-day run, spend ~6 GPU-hours on a 3-point LR probe: train 1,500 optimizer steps at B = 262,144 tokens (= 393M tokens, ~8 hours at 13,500 tok/s — or 750 steps / 4 hours) at η ∈ {5e-4, 1.0e-3, 2.0e-3}, with warmup 200 steps and a constant LR (no decay), and compare validation loss. Fit L(η) = L_min + C·(log η − log η*)² through the three points to locate η*.**  
  why: This is exactly the Fitting Paradigm from Zhou et al. 2026, who validate that 'for fixed N and D, the relationship between validation loss L and learning rate η approximates an invex profile' and fit it with a quadratic in log η, achieving R² = 0.996–0.999 across 500M–7B models. Three points are the minimum to identify a parabola. It costs 3–4% of the total run budget to de-risk the single highest-leverage hyperparameter, and it is the only way to know whether YOUR data mix and architecture put η* at 7e-4 or 1.5e-3.  
  gain: Converts a 'the literature says ~1e-3' recommendation into a measured value for this exact model/corpus. Zhou et al. report their fitted optimum reproduces the true global optimum closely; Step Law reports 0.07% relative loss error for predicted vs. searched HPs.  
  risk: Short probes can mislead: the LR that wins at 400M tokens is usually slightly higher than the one that wins at 10.5B (optimal LR decreases with D — Zhou et al. Eq. 6 has a D^-0.35 term; Power Lines and Shen et al. agree). Shade the probe winner DOWN by ~20–30% for the full run. Also run the probe with the same schedule shape you will use, or at minimum constant-LR for all three arms so the comparison is apples-to-apples.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Fix the per-micro-step GPU sync in src/train/train.py: replace `accum_loss += loss.item()` (inside the `for micro_step in range(grad_accum)` loop) with an on-GPU accumulator, e.g. `accum_loss_t += loss.detach()` initialized as `torch.zeros((), device=device)`, and only call `.item()` inside the `if step % log_interval` block.**  
  why: `.item()` forces a full device synchronize. At grad_accum 16 that is 16 blocking syncs per 2.4s step; under the recommended grad_accum 64 it would be 64 syncs per step, partially eating the (otherwise free) batch-size increase. This is also one plausible contributor to the 56 TFLOP/s vs ~450 TFLOP/s peak gap flagged in the brief.  
  gain: Directly a throughput fix, not a loss fix — but any throughput gain converts 1:1 into more tokens D, and every scaling law above says more D is worth more than any HP tweak. Prevents the batch-size change from regressing throughput.  
  risk: None; it is a pure refactor. Verify the logged `loss` value is unchanged for the first 100 steps after the edit (loss is already pre-divided by grad_accum, so the sum over micro-steps is still the mean).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [low] **Out of scope but coupled: scale the SFT config too. In configs/finetune_1.1*.yaml raise learning_rate from 1.0e-5 to 5.0e-5 and raise the batch from 8 sequences (8,192 tokens) to 64 sequences (65,536 tokens) via micro_batch_size 4 / gradient_accumulation 16, with max_steps reduced to ~2,700 for the same ~176M-token single epoch, warmup ~100 steps, linear decay to zero, weight_decay 0.05.**  
  why: The comment in finetune_1.1.yaml cites 'Llama-2 SFT range (1e-5 to 2e-5)' — but that range was tuned for 7B–70B models. SFT LR should scale with pretrain LR, not be copied across a 13–130x parameter gap; the conventional ratio is 5–10% of the pretrain peak, which for a 1.0e-3 pretrain peak is 5e-5 to 1e-4. An 8,192-token SFT batch is 4x smaller than the already-too-small pretrain batch and gives a very noisy gradient on a 176M-token, 168k-sequence set.  
  gain: Faster and more complete instruction-following acquisition on a small model; the current 1e-5 over 30k steps on a 521M model is likely under-fitting the SFT set, which is consistent with the reported ~0% on executable code and multi-step reasoning surviving fine-tuning.  
  risk: Higher SFT LR risks catastrophic forgetting of pretrain knowledge and degenerate/repetitive outputs. Note max_grad_norm is already tightened to 0.5 in the SFT config — keep it. Evaluate at 1e-5, 3e-5 and 5e-5 on the held-out set before committing; this recommendation is a scaling heuristic, not a cited measurement, and is the weakest item on this list.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Knowledge distillation & synthetic-data training for Plasma 1.1 (521M, RTX 5080 16GB, API-text-only teacher)
### Findings
- **TRUE logit/KD distillation is architecturally foreclosed for Plasma: every logit-based method (forward/reverse KL, JSD, GKD, MiniLLM, DistiLLM-2, Gemma-3's 256-sampled-logits recipe) requires the teacher and student to share a tokenizer/vocabulary. Plasma uses a bespoke 48,000-token SentencePiece BPE; Gemma-3 uses 256K, Qwen3 151K, Llama-3 128K. No open teacher emits probabilities over Plasma's 48K vocab.**  
  Gemma 3 pretrains ALL sizes with distillation by 'sampling 256 logits per token, weighted by teacher probabilities', with non-sampled logits set to zero and renormalized, then cross-entropy against that softened target — a per-token operation over a shared vocab. GKD/MiniLLM/DistiLLM-2 all minimize a divergence between per-token teacher and student distributions over the same token set. Cross-tokenizer KD (ULD/DSKD) exists but is lossy and unproven at this scale.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2503.19786v1 (Gemma 3 Technical Report, §Distillation); https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.13649 (GKD); https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.08543 (MiniLLM)_
- **Even if logits were available, no teacher that fits alongside training on 16GB is worth distilling from. Student memory alone is ~8.3GB (521M bf16 params 1.04GB + fp32 master 2.08GB + AdamW m/v fp32 4.16GB + grads 1.04GB) plus ~1-2GB activations with checkpointing. A frozen bf16 teacher adds 2.0GB (1B) to 3.4GB (1.7B) — technically fits at ~12-14GB, but costs ~30-40% throughput and the teacher would only be a 1-2B model.**  
  Measured throughput is already 13,500 tok/s (~56 TFLOP/s effective vs ~450 TFLOP/s bf16 peak on RTX 5080). Adding a 1.7B frozen teacher forward roughly triples the forward FLOPs per token relative to the 521M student.  
  _src: Plasma configs/pretrain_1.1_rebuild.yaml (batch 32 = 2x16, gradient_checkpointing: true) + task-provided throughput measurement_
- **Sequence-level KD (SeqKD) — i.e. exactly 'train the student on teacher-generated text', which is all a text-only API gives you — measurably HURT students at Plasma's scale in a controlled pretraining study. At 500M it scored below plain pretraining with no KD.**  
  MiniPLM Table 1 (avg zero-shot over 9 tasks, Qwen-1.5 1.8B teacher, Pile): 200M — no-KD 39.9 / vanilla KD 39.9 / SeqKD 39.7 / MiniPLM 41.3. 500M — no-KD 43.2 / vanilla KD 43.6 / SeqKD 42.3 / MiniPLM 44.8. 1.2B — no-KD 44.9 / vanilla KD 45.4 / SeqKD 44.3 / MiniPLM 46.6.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2410.17215v2 (MiniPLM, ICLR 2025)_
- **The distinction that decides ROI: free-generation from a topic list (SeqKD-like) is weak; GROUNDED rephrasing/transformation of real documents is the strongest measured lever in synthetic pretraining data. WRAP rephrased C4 with a frozen Mistral-7B-Instruct into 4 styles (Easy / Medium / Hard / Q-A) and mixed real:synthetic 1:1.**  
  WRAP: ~3x pre-training speed-up on C4; >50% average perplexity improvement across Pile subsets at matched compute; +2% average zero-shot accuracy across 13 tasks (49.4% synthetic+C4 vs 47.4% C4-only); a 350M model trained on 15% of C4 with rephrases outperforms a 1.3B trained on all of C4; ~3x perplexity reduction on ArXiv/HackerNews domains.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.16380 (Rephrasing the Web / WRAP, ACL 2024); https://aclanthology.org/2024.acl-long.757/_
- **BeyondWeb (Aug 2025) is the current state of the art on scaled synthetic pretraining data and gives two decisive practical numbers for a solo GPU budget: (a) rephraser quality saturates around 3B parameters, so a cheap/fast generator is sufficient; (b) style diversity matters more than any single style — conversational content saturates past ~20% of the mix.**  
  BeyondWeb beats Cosmopedia by up to 5.1pp and Nemotron-CC's synthetic subset by 2.6pp averaged over 14 benchmarks; up to 7.7x faster training than open web data and 2.7x faster than Nemotron-Synth; a 3B model trained on 180B BeyondWeb tokens outperforms an 8B trained on 180B Cosmopedia tokens. 'Increasing rephraser size from 1B to 3B yields substantial gains, but improvements plateau beyond 3B.' 'Gains saturate beyond 20% conversational content.'  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10975 (BeyondWeb, Datology AI)_
- **NVIDIA's Nemotron-CC confirms the rephrasing recipe at trillion-token scale and shows synthetic rewriting recovers most of the content aggressive quality filtering throws away.**  
  6.3T-token English CC dataset of which 1.9T tokens are synthetically generated. Nemotron-CC-HQ scores +5.6 MMLU over DCLM. An 8B model trained on 15T tokens (7.2T from Nemotron-CC) beats Llama 3.1 8B by +5 MMLU and +3.1 ARC-Challenge. Synthetic rephrasing restores up to 90% of content removed by heuristic filters.  
  _src: https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/announcing-nemotron-cc-a-trillion-token-english-language-dataset-for-llm-pretraining/_
- **The single closest proof point to Plasma's goal on executable code: a 350M-parameter model reached 45% pass@1 on HumanEval using the phi-1 'textbooks are all you need' pipeline. Nothing else in the literature gets a sub-500M model anywhere near that.**  
  phi-1 is 1.3B params trained 4 days on 8xA100 on 6B tokens of filtered 'textbook-quality' web code + 1B tokens of GPT-3.5-generated synthetic textbooks and exercises (7B tokens total, ~7 epochs) → 50.6% HumanEval, 55.5% MBPP. 'phi-1-small, 350M parameters, trained with the identical pipeline, achieves 45% on HumanEval.'  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.11644 (Textbooks Are All You Need)_
- **phi-4 formalized the seeding discipline that separates working synthetic data from junk: synthetic data is never generated from thin air, it is seeded from real organic text chunks, and question difficulty is filtered by answer-consistency across multiple generations.**  
  ~400B tokens of synthetic data across 50 broad dataset types for a 14B model. Pipeline: identify web/code seeds, chunk and filter for factual/reasoning content; collect forum questions and filter by difficulty by generating multiple answers and measuring consistency, dropping very easy and very hard; rewrite seeds into exercises, discussions and structured reasoning tasks. Web data is still required for general-knowledge benchmarks.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.08905 (Phi-4 Technical Report); https://graphcore-research.github.io/phi-4/_
- **Cosmopedia is the fully-documented open recipe for prompt diversity at scale and its published failure modes are exactly what a naive generation loop will hit: repeated openers and template collapse.**  
  25B tokens / 30M+ files from Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct-v0.1, >10,000 H100-hours. 145 web clusters reduced to 112 topics; 4 target audiences (young children / high school / college / researchers) x 3 formats (textbook / blog post / WikiHow) = up to 12x prompt multiplication per topic; topic conditioning applied only 50% of the time to increase diversity; target <1% duplicate content. Explicit fix required for collapse: initial generations reused identical openers ('Once upon a time', 'The sun hung low in the sky') until the model was explicitly told to avoid them. 'Most of the time for Cosmopedia was spent on meticulous prompt engineering.' Cosmo-1B beat TinyLlama-1.1B on ARC-e/ARC-c/OpenBookQA/MMLU but lagged phi-1.5.  
  _src: https://huggingface.co/blog/cosmopedia; https://huggingface.co/datasets/HuggingFaceTB/cosmopedia_
- **Honest counterpoint: HuggingFace substantially DE-emphasized free-form synthetic pretraining data between SmolLM1 and SmolLM2. Once they had well-filtered web (FineWeb-Edu, DCLM) and domain-filtered math/code, Cosmopedia v2 fell to 4% of the mix and only in the final decay stage.**  
  SmolLM2 (1.7B, 11T tokens) staged mixture: Stage 1 (0-6T) 90% web / 10% code / 0% math; Stage 2 (6-8T) 75/20/5; Stage 3 (8-10T) ~60-65/20-25/10 with Stack-Edu replacing StarCoderData; Stage 4 decay (10-11T) 58% web / 24% code / 14% math / 4% Cosmopedia v2. Domain-filtering beat synthesis on math: GSM8K ~10% (OpenWebMath) → 14% (InfiMM-WebMath) → ~20% (FineMath4+, 10B tokens).  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.02737 / https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1 (SmolLM2, COLM 2025)_
- **For instruction data, aggressive filtering beats volume by a wide margin, and the winning ratio is roughly 300K kept out of 1-4M generated (~8-30%).**  
  Magpie generated 3M instructions from Llama-3-8B-Instruct (Air) and 1M from Llama-3-70B-Instruct (Pro) by feeding only the pre-query chat template, then filtered each to 300K on quality, difficulty, instruction-embedding min-neighbor-distance, reward-model score and task category. Llama-3-8B-base fine-tuned on Magpie-Pro-300K-Filtered: AlpacaEval 2 LC 25.08% and Arena-Hard 52.12%, vs OpenHermes 2.5 (1M examples) 12.89% LC, ShareGPT (112K) 9.73% LC / 27.26% Arena-Hard, Evol-Instruct (143K) 8.52% LC, UltraChat (208K) 8.29% LC.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2406.08464v2 (Magpie, ICLR 2025); https://magpie-align.github.io/_
- **Self-Instruct establishes the cheap, reproducible dedup threshold for instruction synthesis and the realistic error rate you must plan to filter around.**  
  175 seed tasks bootstrapped to 52K instructions / 82K instance input-output pairs; new instructions are discarded when ROUGE-L overlap with any existing instruction is too high (0.7 threshold in the paper's pipeline). +33% absolute on SUPER-NATURALINSTRUCTIONS over vanilla GPT-3, within 5% of InstructGPT-001. The authors' own quality audit found ~46% of data points had some problem.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.10560 (Self-Instruct, ACL 2023); https://github.com/yizhongw/self-instruct_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Formally drop all logit-KD options (forward/reverse KL, JSD, top-k logit KD, GKD, MiniLLM, DistiLLM-2) from the Plasma 1.1 roadmap. Do not spend a day on cross-tokenizer KD. Reason to record in the repo: the 48,000-token custom SentencePiece vocab is not shared with any open teacher (Gemma-3 256K, Qwen3 151K, Llama-3 128K), and the logit-free variant of KD (SeqKD) measured WORSE than no KD at 500M.**  
  why: Logit distillation is a per-token divergence over a shared vocabulary. Retokenizing a teacher's distribution into a foreign 48K vocab requires ULD/DSKD-class alignment that is lossy and unvalidated below 1B. MiniPLM's controlled study at exactly 200M/500M/1.2B shows SeqKD at 500M scores 42.3 vs 43.2 for plain pretraining — the naive text-only KD path is a regression, not a gain.  
  gain: Avoids 1-3 weeks of engineering with a measured expected value of roughly -0.9 points at 500M.  
  risk: None to model quality. Only risk is missing a future opportunity if Plasma ever adopts a standard tokenizer.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Reallocate the existing 320,000-step budget into a staged mixture instead of a flat one. Steps 0-272,000 (85%, ~8.9B tokens): current 7.52B corpus as-is. Steps 272,000-320,000 (final 15%, ~1.57B tokens, the LR-decay window): 40% original web/wiki/arXiv, 35% WRAP-style rephrased FineWeb-Edu, 15% synthetic code textbooks + exercises, 10% synthetic instruction/QA-formatted text. Generate 250-400M unique synthetic tokens for this window (~2.4-3.8 epochs inside it).**  
  why: This is the SmolLM2 decay-stage pattern (Stage 4 = 58% web / 24% code / 14% math / 4% Cosmopedia) combined with WRAP's 1:1 real:synthetic guardrail and the model-collapse result that synthetic must ACCUMULATE alongside real data, never replace it. Concentrating high-value tokens in the low-LR decay window is where they imprint hardest and where every published small-model recipe puts them.  
  gain: WRAP measured +2.0 points average zero-shot over 13 tasks at 1.3B/300B tokens with a full-run 1:1 mix; BeyondWeb measured up to +5.1pp over Cosmopedia across 14 benchmarks. Applying it to only 15% of the run, expect +2 to +4 points on Plasma's overall honest benchmark, concentrated in factual QA and instruction following.  
  risk: Repeating a 300M-token synthetic pool ~3x inside the decay window risks memorization of synthetic phrasings; mitigate with the 40% real-data floor and by holding out 5% of synthetic for a perplexity check. If the synthetic pool is stylistically narrow it can flatten output diversity.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Build the rephrasing pipeline as document-seeded, never topic-seeded. For each of ~500K sampled FineWeb-Edu/StackExchange/Wikipedia documents, emit exactly one API call that rewrites it in ONE randomly chosen style drawn from: (1) 'explain to a curious 10-year-old', (2) 'clean encyclopedic prose, Wikipedia register', (3) 'dense technical exposition for a graduate reader', (4) 'question-and-answer pairs extracted from the passage', (5) 'a short worked example or exercise with a full solution'. Cap output at ~600 tokens per call.**  
  why: WRAP's exact four-style design (Easy / Medium / Hard / Q-A) with a frozen instruct model and 1:1 real:synthetic mixing is the recipe that produced the 3x speedup and >50% Pile perplexity improvement. BeyondWeb found that seed quality alone is insufficient and 'multiple rephrasing and transformation prompts' are required to avoid stylistic and topical homogeneity; conversational style saturates past ~20% of the mix, so cap style (1) at 20%.  
  gain: The 3x-7.7x token-efficiency multiplier from WRAP/BeyondWeb is the specific mechanism; Plasma at 20 tokens/param is the most token-starved model in this comparison set, so it captures more of this multiplier than a 1.7B model would.  
  risk: Hallucination injection — Cosmopedia explicitly flagged Mixtral hallucinating on historical facts and math. Rephrasing (which is grounded in the source document) is materially safer than free generation, but factual drift still occurs. Mitigate by keeping style (2) grounded ('use only facts present in the passage; do not add outside information').  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Use Claude Haiku 4.5 via the Message Batches API for bulk rephrasing (batch rate $0.50 in / $2.50 out per MTok), and reserve Sonnet 5 or Opus 5 only for code-exercise and math-solution generation where correctness is verifiable. Cache the shared style/system prompt (must be >=1024 tokens for Sonnet 5, >=512 for Opus 5) and vary only the seed document below the cache breakpoint. Budget: ~$900 for 300M rephrased tokens on Haiku batch; ~$400-600 for 40-60M verified code/math tokens on Sonnet 5 batch.**  
  why: BeyondWeb found rephraser gains 'plateau beyond 3B' parameters — paying frontier-model rates for bulk rephrasing buys nothing measurable.
