  why: NEFTune's headline numbers (LLaMA-2-7B Alpaca 29.79% -> 64.69%) are all GPT-4-judged AlpacaEval win-rate, which is strongly length-sensitive; the authors report OpenLLM Leaderboard scores as essentially unchanged and note they could not sweep hyperparameters. Plasma's benchmark is factual QA / code / reasoning / arithmetic - the axis NEFTune does not move. No sub-1B results exist.  
  gain: Zero expected gain on Plasma's stated benchmark; avoids spending a run slot on it.  
  risk: Low either way - NEFTune costs no VRAM and no throughput. The risk is opportunity cost and misreading a length-driven win as a quality win.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do not build a sequence-length curriculum. Instead, handle length per source at build time: for the 48.8% of UltraChat and 50.0% of Capybara conversations over 1024 tokens, keep the first N complete turns that fit and terminate them with a real <|im_end|>/EOS, rather than truncating mid-answer or wrapping into the next row.**  
  why: Context is fixed at 1024 in both pretrain and SFT, so there is no length curriculum to schedule; median conversation is already 257 tokens. None of SmolLM2, Tulu 3, or SmolTulu use a length curriculum - all use fixed max length plus packing. The real length problem is the measured 11.1% overflow rate, which is a packing/truncation problem, already covered by BFD.  
  gain: Recovers most of the ~11% of conversations that currently become corrupted fragments, as valid shorter conversations.  
  risk: Truncating a multi-turn conversation to its first turns changes the distribution toward shorter dialogues. Given the 1024 context this is unavoidable and preferable to fragments.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **During the last ~10% of pretraining (the LR decay tail, roughly steps 288,000-320,000 of 320,000), mix 2-5% chat-formatted instruction data into the pretrain corpus so the ChatML control tokens receive real gradient before SFT begins and the base model has seen the turn structure.**  
  why: SmolLM2's multi-stage curriculum upsamples high-quality math/code sources in the final decay stage rather than keeping the mixture fixed, and mid-training/annealing on task-shaped data before post-training is now standard practice for small models. For Plasma specifically there is a mechanical reason: with tied embeddings, newly reserved <|im_start|>/<|im_end|> rows would otherwise be pure init decayed toward zero by weight_decay 0.1 over 320k steps, so SFT must learn both the embedding and the (tied) output row from scratch.  
  gain: Makes the chat tokens usable from step 0 of SFT and gives the base a head start on turn structure. Reported mid-training gains in the small-model literature are on the order of a few points, but the token pre-warming benefit here is mechanical and specific.  
  risk: Medium confidence - I did not find a primary source that isolates the effect of instruct data in the decay phase at 500M scale. Keep the fraction at 2-5% and hold out the same eval prompts to avoid contaminating the benchmark. If pretraining is already past step ~290,000, skip it. No VRAM impact.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **If the chat special tokens end up added AFTER pretraining rather than reserved before, initialize their embedding rows to the mean of the trained embedding matrix plus N(0, 0.02 * std(E)) rather than the default random init, and keep vocab a multiple of 64 (pad 48000 -> 48064 with dummy tokens rather than going to 48002).**  
  why: With tied embeddings the same random row serves as both input embedding and output projection, so a fresh random row produces uncontrolled logits for the exact tokens the model must emit to stop. Mean-initialization is the standard fix for adding tokens to a pretrained model. A vocab of 48002 breaks the 64-alignment that 48000 already has and costs tensor-core efficiency in the 1280x48000 tied matmul, which is the largest single GEMM in the model.  
  gain: Prevents the model from emitting the new control tokens randomly during early SFT, and preserves the current matmul alignment.  
  risk: Only relevant in the fallback path; the preferred route (user_defined_symbols in SentencePiece before pretraining) keeps vocab at exactly 48000 and avoids this entirely.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Keep dropout at 0.0 for SFT. Only if you end up running 3+ epochs on the un-expanded 168k-row set should you add LIMA-style residual dropout, linearly ramped from 0.0 at layer 0 to 0.1 at layer 25 (LIMA used 0.2 for its smaller models and 0.3 for 65B, over 15 epochs on 1,000 examples).**  
  why: Dropout is a defense against memorization at LIMA-like data scale (1k examples x 15 epochs). At 176-250M SFT tokens x 2 epochs, SmolLM2 and Tulu 3 both use zero dropout. Adding it preemptively just slows convergence.  
  gain: None directly; avoids a needless regularizer.  
  risk: If the expanded mixture is not built and 3 epochs are run on 168k rows, watch for held-out assistant loss turning up after ~1.5 epochs - that is the signal to add dropout or stop earlier.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Confirm the SFT run resets AdamW optimizer state (fresh moments, fresh cosine schedule starting at step 0) rather than resuming pretrain optimizer state, and that betas stay (0.9, 0.95) with eps 1e-8.**  
  why: Carrying pretrain second-moment estimates into SFT at a 6-8x higher LR produces unpredictable effective step sizes in the first few hundred steps, which is exactly when the model is learning the chat format. Standard practice across SmolLM2, Tulu 3 and SmolTulu is a fresh optimizer for the SFT stage.  
  gain: Removes a silent source of instability in the warmup window.  
  risk: None; this is a one-line verification in the --finetune code path in scripts/run_1.1*.py / src/train/train.py.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Optimizers beyond AdamW for Plasma 1.1 (521M dense LLaMA-style, single RTX 5080 16GB, 32,768 tokens/step, ~10.4B token budget): Muon, Adam-mini, Sophia, Lion, Shampoo/SOAP/Kron, AdEMAMix, Schedule-Free AdamW, LAMB, plus Adam betas/eps, weight decay, gradient clipping, and bf16 vs fp32 master weights at this exact scale
### Findings
- **Your run is an almost exact match for a published, rigorously-tuned optimizer benchmark: Llama-520M at 1x Chinchilla (10B tokens). At that point the tuned-AdamW vs tuned-Muon gap is 3.110 -> 3.073 C4 loss (0.037 nats), i.e. ~1.35x data efficiency, NOT 2x.**  
  Wen/Hall/Ma/Liang benchmark 11 optimizers on Llama-130M/300M/520M/1.2B, seq_len 4096, DCLM-baseline+StarCoderV2+ProofPile2, fp32 params + bf16 activations. Final C4 loss at 520M across data budgets 10B/21B/42B/85B: AdamW 3.110/3.023/2.958/2.913; Muon 3.073/3.002/2.945/2.900; Scion 3.080/3.007/2.952/2.904; Soap 3.079 (10B); Kron 3.084/3.009/2.946/2.900; NAdamW 3.100/3.013/2.955/2.907; Lion 3.108/3.029/2.965/2.915; Adam-Mini 3.112/3.027/2.966/2.912. Fitting the AdamW loss-vs-tokens slope between 10B and 21B (dL/dlnD = -0.117) gives Muon @10B == AdamW @13.7B, i.e. 1.37x. Paper's own headline: matrix-based optimizers give ~1.3x at <=0.5B, decaying to 1.1x at 1.2B; 'the speedup of alternative optimizers does not exceed 1.4x'.  
  _src: Fantastic Pretraining Optimizers and Where to Find Them, arXiv:2509.02046 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02046 (full text/tables: https://arxiv.org/html/2509.02046v1)_
- **Translated to downstream accuracy at your exact scale/budget, AdamW->Muon is worth about +1.7 accuracy points on a 10-task suite, equivalent to ~1.7x more tokens on some tasks. It will not fix 0% executable code or multi-step reasoning.**  
  520M @ 10B tokens, AdamW -> Muon: LAMBADA 0.500->0.520, OpenBookQA 0.320->0.328, Winogrande 0.539->0.560, PIQA 0.705->0.713, BoolQ 0.555->0.590, WSC273 0.604->0.659, HellaSwag 0.456->0.482, ARC-C 0.299->0.300, ARC-E 0.613->0.623, COPA 0.740->0.730. 10-task mean 53.31% -> 55.05% (+1.74 pts). AdamW needs ~17-18B tokens to reach Muon's 10B average. Also, at 1.2B/8x Chinchilla the downstream average is AdamW 67.15 vs Muon 66.98 — no significant downstream win at larger scale.  
  _src: arXiv:2509.02046 Tables 11/32 (Evaluation Performance for Muon/AdamW, Model Size = 520m) and Table 5 — https://arxiv.org/html/2509.02046v1_
- **Muon is the ONLY matrix-preconditioned optimizer that fits in 16GB at 521M params. SOAP and Kron/PSGD do not fit, full stop.**  
  Computed for your exact shapes (26 x {q 1280x1280, k 256x1280, v 256x1280, o 1280x1280, gate 3584x1280, up 3584x1280, down 1280x3584}): SOAP state (L, R, Q_L, Q_R + Adam m,v) = 3,698M elements unblocked = 13.77 GB fp32; with block size 512 = 3,558M = 13.25 GB (7.7x matrix params). Kron/PSGD (Q_L, Q_R, momentum) = 1,849M = 6.89 GB (4.0x). Muon needs one momentum buffer = 460M elements = 1.71 GB fp32 / 0.86 GB bf16 (1.0x / 0.5x). Your current AdamW m+v over all 521.57M params = 3.89 GB fp32.  
  _src: Derived from your config (configs/pretrain_1.1.yaml) + SOAP algorithm in arXiv:2409.11321 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.11321) and Kron/PSGD as described in arXiv:2509.02046 Table 4_
- **Your model splits 88.2% / 11.8% between Muon-eligible 2D hidden matrices and AdamW-only params, so Muon covers essentially the whole model.**  
  Exact parameter count for hidden=1280, 26 layers, 20 heads/4 KV heads (head_dim 64), SwiGLU 3584, vocab 48000, tied embeddings: 17,694,720 matrix params/layer x 26 = 460,062,720 (88.2%) go to Muon; tied embedding 61,440,000 + 26x2 RMSNorm gains (66,560) + final norm 1,280 = 61,507,840 (11.8%) go to AdamW. Total 521,570,560 — matches your stated 521M exactly.  
  _src: Computed from configs/pretrain_1.1.yaml + src/model; Muon param-split rule from https://kellerjordan.github.io/posts/muon/ and MoonshotAI/Moonlight ('all the matrices except the token classification head and the embedding')_
- **Newton-Schulz overhead on your model is 14.16 TFLOP per optimizer step = 10.4% of your fwd/bwd+recompute FLOPs, which lands at roughly 3-10% wall-clock depending on achieved matmul throughput. Because you use grad_accum=16, NS runs once per 2.43 s step, not per micro-batch.**  
  NS5 cost per matrix (after transposing to rows<=cols, a=min, b=max) = 5*(4*a^2*b + 2*a^3): q 62.91 GFLOP, k 1.85, v 1.85, o 62.91, gate 138.41, up 138.41, down 138.41 -> 549 GFLOP/layer x 26 = 14.16 TFLOP. Your fwd/bwd with checkpointing = 8*N*D = 8*521.57e6*32768 = 136.7 TFLOP. NS = 10.36% of that in FLOPs. At 56 TFLOP/s (your current effective rate) = 253 ms = 10.4% of step; at 150 TFLOP/s = 94 ms = 3.9%; at 200 TFLOP/s = 71 ms = 2.9%. Moonshot report optimizer end-to-end latency 'usually 1% to 3%' of fwd/bwd; Keller Jordan reports <1% for nanoGPT's 768-dim model.  
  _src: Computed; corroborated by Moonlight (arXiv:2502.16982 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16982) and https://kellerjordan.github.io/posts/muon/_
- **The exact Muon algorithm and reference implementation: quintic Newton-Schulz with coefficients (a,b,c)=(3.4445,-4.7750,2.0315), 5 steps, run in bfloat16, Nesterov momentum 0.95, applied only to >=2D hidden weights.**  
  Reference code (KellerJordan/Muon/muon.py): X = G.bfloat16(); if G.size(-2)>G.size(-1): X = X.mT; X = X / (X.norm(dim=(-2,-1),keepdim=True)+1e-7); for _ in range(steps): A = X @ X.mT; B = b*A + c*A@A; X = a*X + B@X. Then muon_update: momentum.lerp_(grad, 1-beta); update = grad.lerp_(momentum, beta) if nesterov; update = NS5(update); update *= max(1, update.size(-2)/update.size(-1))**0.5; p.mul_(1 - lr*wd); p.add_(update, alpha=-lr). Defaults lr=0.02, momentum=0.95, weight_decay=0; aux Adam group defaults lr=3e-4, betas=(0.9,0.95), eps=1e-10. Docstring: 'Muon should only be used for hidden weight layers. The input embedding, final output layer, and any internal gains or biases should be optimized using a standard method such as AdamW.' Note the iteration deliberately does NOT converge to UV^T but to US'V^T with S'~Uniform(0.5,1.5), 'which turns out not to hurt model performance at all'.  
  _src: https://github.com/KellerJordan/Muon/blob/master/muon.py and https://kellerjordan.github.io/posts/muon/_
- **There are two incompatible LR conventions for Muon. Moonshot's (0.2*sqrt(max(A,B)) rescale) makes Muon's update RMS a constant 0.2, which is why they can literally reuse the AdamW-optimal learning rate. Keller Jordan's (max(1,rows/cols)^0.5) makes update RMS = lr/sqrt(fan_in) and needs its own LR ~0.02-0.05.**  
  Moonlight code: adjusted_ratio = 0.2*math.sqrt(max(A,B)); adjusted_lr = lr*adjusted_ratio; p.mul_(1 - lr*wd); p.add_(u, alpha=-adjusted_lr). A semi-orthogonal AxB matrix has RMS = 1/sqrt(max(A,B)), so the rescale yields update RMS exactly 0.2 for every matrix. Paper: they maintain 'a consistent update RMS across different matrix and non-matrix parameters' and 'directly reuse the hyper-parameters that are optimal for the AdamW baseline'. For your shapes the multiplier is 0.2*sqrt(1280)=7.155 for q/k/v/o and 0.2*sqrt(3584)=11.973 for gate/up/down. Keller's rescale gives multiplier 1.0 for q/k/v/o/down and 1.673 for gate/up, i.e. update RMS = lr/sqrt(1280)=lr*0.02795 (or lr/sqrt(3584) for down). Conversion: lr_keller = 0.2 * lr_moonlight * sqrt(fan_in) = 7.155 * lr_moonlight at fan_in 1280.  
  _src: MoonshotAI/Moonlight examples/toy_train.py (https://github.com/MoonshotAI/Moonlight) + arXiv:2502.16982; KellerJordan/Muon muon.py_
- **Muon's tuned hyperparameters at 520M are remarkably insensitive except for the learning rate — LR is the only scaling-sensitive knob, and there is roughly 4x headroom above optimum before divergence.**  
  arXiv:2509.02046 Table 87 (Muon, 520m, 1x Chinchilla), best config eta_adam=0.0024, beta1=0.8, beta2=0.98, eps=1e-25, eta_muon=0.008, linear schedule, g_norm=1, eta_min=0, beta_muon=0.98, BSZ=128 (524,288 tokens), warmup=0, lambda=0.1 -> loss 3.073. Sensitivity: eta_muon 0.008->3.073, 0.016->3.095, 0.032->3.147, 0.064->7.886 (diverged), 0.128->7.900. beta_muon 0.8->3.098, 0.9->3.080, 0.95->3.073, 0.98->3.073. grad clip 0->3.072, 1->3.073, 2.0->3.071. eps_muon 1e-25..1e-10 all 3.072. weight decay 0->3.134, 0.1->3.073, 0.2->3.091. Batch 128->3.073, 256->3.080, 512->3.115, 1024->3.188. Table 4 lists Muon's scaling-sensitive hyperparameters as 'learning rate' only (vs AdamW: learning rate, warmup, weight decay, batch size).  
  _src: arXiv:2509.02046, Tables 4, 41, 87 — https://arxiv.org/html/2509.02046v1_
- **Muon's reported 2x is a compute-optimal-FLOPs claim from Moonshot at MoE scale, not a 2x at 520M dense with a tuned baseline. Moonshot themselves quantify it as 52% of AdamW's FLOPs on 399M-1.5B dense scaling-law models.**  
  Moonlight: 'Muon only requires about 52% training FLOPs to match the performance of AdamW under compute-optimal setting'; scaling-law models 399M-1.5B params, 8.92B-38.91B tokens, LRs 9.5e-4 down to 8.3e-4; weight decay lambda=0.1; memory claim 'Muon uses only one momentum buffer, while AdamW uses two ... the additional memory used by Muon is half of Distributed AdamW'; NS steps N=5; optimizer latency 'usually 1% to 3%' of fwd/bwd. Kimi K2 (1.04T MoE, 32B active) trained 15.5T tokens with MuonClip, WSD schedule, constant LR 2e-4 for 10T tokens then cosine 2e-4 -> 2e-5, weight decay 0.1, global batch 67M tokens, 500-step warmup, QK-Clip tau=100.  
  _src: arXiv:2502.16982 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16982); Kimi K2 tech report arXiv:2507.20534 (https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20534)_
- **Your beta2=0.95 is badly mismatched to your 32,768-token batch. Holding the second-moment half-life fixed in tokens — the validated scaling rule — puts you at beta2 ~= 0.999, i.e. a ~50-80x longer memory than you have now.**  
  Marek/Lotfi/Somasundaram/Wilson/Goldblum (NeurIPS 2025) derive beta2* = beta2^(B*/B) to hold token half-life t2 fixed; they show beta1=0.9 transfers unchanged across batch sizes but fixed beta2 'does not scale effectively to smaller batches, which require a significantly larger value'. Applying it: from the tuned 520M AdamW point (beta2=0.98 at 256x4096 = 1,048,576 tokens/step, t2=36.0M tokens) -> beta2* = 0.98^(32768/1048576) = 0.999369 (half-life 1,098 steps). From GPT-3/LLaMA (beta2=0.95 at 2M tokens/step, t2=27.0M) -> 0.999160. From LLaMA at 4M tokens/step -> 0.999580. All three independent anchors land on beta2 ~ 0.999. Your current 0.95 at 32,768 tokens gives t2 = 13.5 steps = 442k tokens.  
  _src: Small Batch Size Training for Language Models, arXiv:2507.07101 §4.3 Eq. 2 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07101 (https://arxiv.org/html/2507.07101v2); code https://github.com/martin-marek/batch-size_
- **AdamW learning rate scales much more slowly with batch size than sqrt — this makes it very likely your 1.2e-4 is 2-10x below optimum for a 521M model at 32k tokens/step.**  
  Marek et al. §4.3: 'as we scale the batch size from 1 to 1024, the square root rule would indicate that the corresponding learning rate should be scaled by a factor of 32, whereas we find that a factor of only about 3 empirically works better' (i.e. LR ~ B^0.16). Anchors: tuned AdamW at Llama-520M/1x Chinchilla is eta=0.004 at 1,048,576 tokens/step (eta=0.008 diverges to 7.837). Applying B^0.16 over a 32x batch reduction gives ~2.3e-3; even naive sqrt scaling gives 7.1e-4. Published production recipes are more conservative (GPT-3 1.3B: 2e-4; LLaMA-7B: 3e-4; Kimi K2 Muon: 2e-4), so the truth is bracketed but 1.2e-4 sits at the very bottom of the bracket.  
  _src: arXiv:2507.07101 §4.3 and Fig. 4; arXiv:2509.02046 Table 41 — https://arxiv.org/html/2509.02046v1_
- **Gradient clipping is essentially irrelevant at 520M for both AdamW and Muon; weight decay is decisive and its absence can outright diverge AdamW.**  
  arXiv:2509.02046 Table 41 (AdamW, 520m, 1x): max grad norm 0 -> 3.114, 1 -> 3.110, 2.0 -> 3.118 (spread 0.008 nats). Table 87 (Muon, 520m): 0 -> 3.072, 1 -> 3.073, 2.0 -> 3.071. In contrast weight decay for AdamW at 520M: lambda=0 -> 7.270 (diverged), 0.1 -> 3.135, 0.2 -> 3.110 (optimum). For Muon: 0 -> 3.134, 0.1 -> 3.073 (optimum), 0.2 -> 3.091. Moonlight explicitly names 'adding weight decay' as one of the two techniques required to scale Muon.  
  _src: arXiv:2509.02046 Tables 41 and 87; arXiv:2502.16982_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Implement Muon in a single ~120-line file using the Moonshot parameterization, and split parameters exactly as: MUON = all 26x7 = 182 hidden matrices (q_proj 1280x1280, k_proj 256x1280, v_proj 256x1280, o_proj 1280x1280, gate_proj 3584x1280, up_proj 3584x1280, down_proj 1280x3584) = 460,062,720 params; ADAMW = tok_emb.weight/output.weight (61,440,000, tied) + all RMSNorm gains (67,840). Concretely, replace the block at src/train/train.py:98-116 with three lists keyed on `p.ndim < 2` -> adam_1d, `name.endswith('tok_emb.weight') or name.endswith('output.weight')` -> adam_2d, else -> muon. Core update per Muon param:
  buf.mul_(0.95).add_(g); g = g.add(buf, alpha=0.95)              # Nesterov
  u = zeropower_via_newtonschulz5(g, steps=5)                      # (3.4445, -4.7750, 2.0315), bf16, 5 iters
  adj = lr * 0.2 * math.sqrt(max(p.shape[0], p.shape[1]))          # 7.155*lr for q/k/v/o, 11.973*lr for gate/up/down
  p.mul_(1 - lr * 0.1); p.add_(u.view_as(p), alpha=-adj)           # decay uses UNadjusted lr
NS kernel verbatim: X = G.bfloat16(); if G.size(-2) > G.size(-1): X = X.mT; X = X / (X.norm(dim=(-2,-1), keepdim=True) + 1e-7); for _ in range(5): A = X @ X.mT; B = -4.7750*A + 2.0315*(A@A); X = 3.4445*X + B@X; if transposed: X = X.mT. Keep AdamW (fused) for the embedding+norm groups with betas (0.9, 0.999), eps=1e-10. Start Muon lr = 1.2e-4 (identical to your AdamW lr — this is exactly what Moonlight and Kimi K2 do under this rescaling), momentum 0.95, nesterov True, ns_steps 5, weight_decay 0.1.**  
  why: At Llama-520M / 1x Chinchilla — the closest published match to your exact model size and 10.4B-token budget — tuned Muon reaches 3.073 C4 loss vs tuned AdamW's 3.110, worth ~1.35x data efficiency and +1.74 downstream accuracy points across 10 tasks. Muon is the only matrix-preconditioned method whose state fits in 16GB (1x params vs SOAP's 7.7x and Kron's 4.0x). The 0.2*sqrt(max(A,B)) rescale forces update RMS to a constant 0.2 for every matrix, matching AdamW's empirical update RMS, which is why Moonshot could reuse AdamW-optimal LRs unchanged — this removes the single riskiest part of adopting Muon.  
  gain: ~0.035-0.040 nats lower validation loss at 10.4B tokens, equal to reaching your current final loss with ~7.7B tokens instead of 10.4B; ~+1.5 to +2.0 points on a HellaSwag/ARC/PIQA/LAMBADA-style suite. Plus 1.71 GB of freed VRAM.  
  risk: Two real risks. (1) No published Muon result validates a batch as small as 32,768 tokens at 520M — the tuned 520M Muon point used 524,288 tokens/step. Orthogonalization rescales O(1/sqrt(B)) noise singular directions up to signal magnitude, and Moonshot's own docstring warns about small batches; the gain could shrink toward 1.1x. (2) ~3-10% wall-clock overhead from Newton-Schulz. Mitigation: run a 3,000-step A/B against your current AdamW config before committing the 9-day run.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Before anything else, run a 4-point learning-rate sweep of AdamW at {1.2e-4, 3e-4, 6e-4, 1.2e-3} for 4,000 steps each (fixed seed, fixed data order), comparing held-out loss at step 4,000. Budget: 4 x 4,000 x 2.43 s = 10.8 GPU-hours out of a ~216-hour run (5%).**  
  why: Tuned AdamW at Llama-520M/1x Chinchilla is eta=0.004 at 1.05M tokens/step. The validated small-batch LR scaling exponent is ~B^0.16 (batch 1 -> 1024 needs only ~3x LR, not the 32x sqrt rule would give), which maps a 32x batch reduction to only a 1.7x LR reduction, i.e. ~2.3e-3. Even naive sqrt scaling gives 7.1e-4. Your 1.2e-4 is 6-19x below both extrapolations and at the very bottom of the production-recipe bracket (GPT-3 1.3B 2e-4, LLaMA-7B 3e-4). An under-set LR is the single most common cause of 'my 521M model underperforms my 235M model'.  
  gain: Potentially larger than everything else combined. Moving from a 4x-too-low LR to optimum is typically worth 0.05-0.15 nats at fixed tokens — 1.5-4x the entire Muon gain. If 1.2e-4 turns out to be right you have spent 5% of the run to eliminate the biggest open question.  
  risk: Higher LRs can diverge: at 520M, AdamW went from 3.110 at eta=0.004 to 7.837 at eta=0.008 — a hard cliff, not a gentle degradation. Keep warmup >= 2,000 steps and grad clip 1.0 during the sweep, and treat any run whose loss exceeds ~5.0 at step 2,000 as diverged. Do not extrapolate a 4,000-step winner blindly to 318,000 steps; higher LRs look better early (the benchmark warns loss curves in the initial phase 'exaggerate performance gaps' and can 'reverse the eventual ranking'), so re-check at 12,000 steps for the top two.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Change AdamW betas from (0.9, 0.95) to (0.9, 0.999) at src/train/train.py:114, and set eps=1e-10 explicitly (currently defaulting to PyTorch's 1e-8). If you ever change tokens-per-step from 32,768 to B*, recompute beta2 as 0.999^(B*/32768).**  
  why: The validated scaling rule is beta2* = beta2^(B*/B), which holds the second-moment half-life fixed in tokens; beta1 = 0.9 transfers unchanged across all batch sizes. Three independent tuned anchors agree: 0.98 at 1.049M tokens/step -> 0.999369; 0.95 at 2M tokens/step -> 0.999160; 0.95 at 4M tokens/step -> 0.999580. Your current beta2 = 0.95 at 32,768 tokens/step gives a half-life of 13.5 steps = 442k tokens, roughly 50-80x shorter than every tuned reference. This is a one-line change with a first-principles derivation behind it, and Marek et al. show it is exactly what turns 'small batches are unstable' into 'small batches are more robust'. Epsilon is flat from 1e-10 to 1e-25 in the 520M sweep (3.110 -> 3.116) with 1e-10 marginally best; the reference Muon aux-Adam also uses 1e-10.  
  gain: The reproduction in the paper's Fig. 5 shows this single change lifts batch-16/32 runs from clearly-worse-than-batch-128/256 to on-par. Expect a meaningful reduction in loss-spike frequency and a small (0.005-0.03 nats) loss improvement; eps contributes at most ~0.005 nats.  
  risk: With beta2 = 0.999 the second moment adapts ~74x more slowly, so a genuine distribution shift in your mixed corpus (FineWeb-Edu -> code -> arXiv shard boundaries) will be absorbed more sluggishly and could produce a transient. Bias correction handles the cold start (PyTorch does it), but keep the 3,000-step warmup. Sanity-check with a 2,000-step run first. This rule is validated at 30M-1.3B params, not specifically at 521M.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Raise weight_decay from 0.1 to 0.2 IF you stay on AdamW; keep 0.1 if you switch to Muon. Separately, run one 4,000-step A/B applying weight decay to the tied embedding (currently excluded at src/train/train.py:103) at the same rate as the matrices.**  
  why: lr*wd integrated over training is the invariant, not wd. Your run: 320,000 steps x mean lr 6.60e-5 = sum(lr) 21.1; x 0.1 = 2.11. The tuned 520M reference: ~9,900 steps x mean lr 0.002 = 19.8; x 0.2 = 3.96. You are at 53% of the reference decay budget; wd = 0.2 brings you to 4.22. The 520M ablation is unambiguous that this matters: AdamW lambda 0 -> 7.270 (diverged), 0.1 -> 3.135, 0.2 -> 3.110. Under Muon the optimum flips to 0.1 (0 -> 3.134, 0.1 -> 3.073, 0.2 -> 3.091). On embeddings: because your embeddings are TIED, every one of the 48,000 rows receives gradient at every step from the softmax denominator, so the usual 'rare rows get decayed to zero without ever being updated' objection does not apply — an undecayed tied embedding instead lets the logit scale drift upward, which is effectively an untracked softmax-temperature schedule.  
  gain: wd 0.1 -> 0.2 under AdamW: ~0.025 nats at 520M/1x by direct measurement. Decaying the tied embedding: unmeasured here, plausibly 0-0.02 nats plus better logit-scale stability.  
  risk: wd 0.2 under Muon is measurably WORSE (3.091 vs 3.073) — do not carry the AdamW value over. Decaying tied embeddings shrinks logit scale, which can slightly under-confidence the model early; if you see the loss plateau higher, revert. Optimal wd is coupled to optimal LR, so re-check wd after the LR sweep converges, not before.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Leave max_grad_norm at 1.0 and stop treating it as a tunable. Keep the clip purely as spike insurance and keep logging grad_norm (you already do at src/train/train.py:236-238).**  
  why: Across all four model sizes, the clip threshold is the least consequential hyperparameter tested. At 520M: AdamW {0, 1, 2.0} -> {3.114, 3.110, 3.118}; Muon {0, 1, 2.0} -> {3.072, 3.073, 3.071}. Total spread 0.008 nats for AdamW and 0.002 for Muon — below run-to-run noise. Spending sweep budget here has near-zero expected value.  
  gain: Zero loss change; saves you 2-4 wasted sweep runs (5-11 GPU-hours) that should go to the LR sweep instead.  
  risk: None. Note that global clip_grad_norm_ across all parameters is a near no-op for the Muon group's update direction (orthogonalization discards the global scale), but it still rescales what enters the momentum buffer, so keep it consistent across your A/B comparisons.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Keep fp32 master weights and fp32 gradient accumulation. Do NOT move parameters to bf16 storage. Do optionally store the Muon momentum buffer in bf16 (torch.zeros(p.shape, dtype=torch.bfloat16)) to save an additional 0.86 GB, but keep Adam's m and v in fp32.**  
  why: At lr = 1.2e-4 your per-step update RMS is 2.4e-5 against a weight RMS of 0.0280 — a relative magnitude of 8.59e-4, which is 4.5x SMALLER than one bf16 ULP (2^-8 = 3.91e-3). With round-to-nearest, essentially every update would be discarded. fp32's relative ULP of 5.96e-8 gives ~14,000x headroom. Marek et al. show the bias is worse at small batch specifically ('reducing the batch size might actually be detrimental with standard deterministic rounding') and that stochastic rounding is what closes the gap — but they also keep optimizer state in fp32 because it is cheap. Muon's momentum is the one buffer that tolerates bf16, because the orthogonalization step discards magnitude and the reference implementation already casts to bf16 inside Newton-Schulz. Adam's v spans many orders of magnitude and should stay fp32.  
  gain: Prevents a catastrophic silent failure mode; bf16 Muon momentum frees 0.86 GB (total Muon savings 2.57 GB vs your current AdamW).  
  risk: If you ever do want bf16 master weights (to free 0.97 GB), you must write a stochastic-rounding kernel — compute the update in fp32, then round to bf16 with probability proportional to the fractional part. That is a real implementation and testing cost for less than 1 GB, and you do not need it. bf16 momentum is low-risk but verify the first 500 steps match an fp32-momentum reference run.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **After adopting Muon, spend the freed 1.7-2.6 GB on throughput: set gradient_checkpointing: false, raise micro_batch_size from 2 to 8 and drop gradient_accumulation from 16 to 4 (keeping batch_size 32 sequences = 32,768 tokens/step), and set PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True before importing torch.**  
  why: Your own config comment records that gradient_checkpointing=false gave 16,700 tok/s vs 13,500 with it (+23.7%) but hit 15.87-16.30 GB and collapsed to 9,700 tok/s from allocator fragmentation. Muon drops static memory from 7.77 GB to 6.05 GB (fp32 momentum) or 5.20 GB (bf16 momentum), which is exactly the headroom that failure needed. micro_batch_size=2 x 1024 gives every GEMM an M dimension of only 2,048 across 26 layers — that is launch- and bandwidth-bound, and is the most likely explanation for your 56 TFLOP/s (~25% MFU against a realistic ~220 TFLOP/s dense bf16 ceiling, not the 450 headline). expandable_segments is what modded-nanogpt uses to avoid exactly the fragmentation collapse you logged. Crucially, keep some accumulation: dropping to grad_accum=1 would multiply the 14.16 TFLOP Newton-Schulz cost by 16.  
  gain: Compounding with Muon: ~1.24x from checkpointing off, plus a further 1.1-1.4x if micro_batch 8 lifts MFU from 25% toward 35-40%. Combined with Muon's ~1.35x sample efficiency and minus ~3-6% NS overhead, the realistic end-to-end speedup to a target loss is 1.7-2.2x — i.e. your 9-day run becomes 4-5 days, or the same 9 days buys ~1.8x the effective tokens.  
  risk: Memory is the binding constraint; measure actual peak over 1,000+ steps, not just a pre-flight, since your logged failure appeared at step 550. If micro_batch 8 without checkpointing exceeds ~14.5 GB peak, fall back to micro_batch 4 or re-enable checkpointing. Verify torch.compile on the Newton-Schulz function works on sm_120 (Blackwell needs PyTorch >= 2.7 / CUDA 12.8); if it fails, run NS eager — that only moves the overhead from ~3% to ~10%.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [low] **Run a Muon-momentum sweep of beta_muon in {0.95, 0.98, 0.995} over 4,000 steps, alongside the LR sweep. If the token-half-life logic extends to Muon, 0.995 (half-life 138 steps = 4.5M tokens) or higher should win at your batch size.**  
  why: Muon has no second moment, so momentum is its ONLY noise filter — which is precisely the mechanism that makes small batches risky for it (noise singular directions scale as O(1/sqrt(B)) and orthogonalization rescales them to signal magnitude). More momentum averaging before orthogonalization directly attacks that. The tuned 520M values were beta_muon 0.95/0.98 (tied at 3.073) at 524,288 tokens/step, corresponding to token half-lives of 7.1M and 18.0M; matching those half-lives at your 32,768 tokens/step implies beta_muon between 0.9968 and 0.9987. The degradation from too-low momentum is measurable (0.9 -> 3.080, 0.8 -> 3.098), and no published work has tested the high end at small batch.  
  gain: Speculative but cheap: 0.01-0.03 nats if the half-life logic transfers, and it is the single most plausible fix if Muon underperforms at your batch size.  
  risk: This extension of the half-life rule to Muon's momentum is my inference, not a published result — the rule is validated only for Adam's beta2. Very high momentum increases the lag before the buffer reflects the current loss landscape and could hurt during warmup; use a momentum warmup (0.85 -> target over the first 500 steps) if you see early instability. Test at 0.98 first since that is a measured-optimal value.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do NOT adopt SOAP, Shampoo, distributed Shampoo, Kron/PSGD, Sophia, LAMB, or AdEMAMix for this run. If you want a zero-effort consolation prize instead of Muon, switch torch.optim.AdamW to NAdamW (Nesterov-momentum Adam) — one import change, identical memory and step cost.**  
  why: Hard eliminations: SOAP needs 13.25 GB of optimizer state at your shapes even with block size 512 (7.7x matrix params) and Kron needs 6.89 GB (4.0x) — neither fits alongside 1.94 GB params + 1.94 GB grads in 16GB. Shampoo adds periodic inverse-pth-root computations designed for multi-node. Sophia measurably underperforms AdamW below 0.5B in a properly randomized-data-loader setting (130M: 3.544 vs AdamW 3.529) and its original 2x claim traced to a non-randomized data loader depressing the baseline. LAMB targets 32k-65k sequence batches — 1000x your regime. AdEMAMix needs 3 optimizer states (5.83 GB, worse than AdamW's 3.89 GB), was never benchmarked against a tuned AdamW at 520M, and requires beta3/alpha warmup over ~250-500k steps to avoid early divergence. NAdamW by contrast delivers 3.100 vs AdamW's 3.110 at 520M for free.  
  gain: Avoids 2-5 days of wasted implementation and OOM debugging. NAdamW alone: ~0.010 nats (roughly 1.08x data efficiency) for one line of code.  
  risk: You give up SOAP/Kron, which do beat Muon in the overtrained regime (>=8x Chinchilla) — but you are training at exactly 1x Chinchilla (521M params, 10.4B tokens), where Muon is the measured winner at every model size. If you later decide to overtrain to 40B+ tokens, revisit this. NAdamW's scaling-sensitive hyperparameters are learning rate and warmup, so it needs its own short LR re-sweep.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Add a per-head max-attention-logit metric to your logging loop (cheap: max over the pre-softmax scores, reduced per head, logged every 50 steps alongside grad_norm at src/train/train.py:249). Only implement QK-Clip if it ever exceeds ~100.**  
  why: Muon's documented failure mode is exploding attention logits, 'an issue that occurs more frequently with Muon but less with AdamW'. Moonshot observed max logits exceeding 1000 in a 9B-activated MoE with vanilla Muon. QK-Clip is: after the Muon step, for each head h, if S_max^h > tau then gamma = tau/S_max^h and scale W_q^h and W_k^h each by sqrt(gamma). Kimi K2 used tau = 100 across 15.5T tokens with zero loss spikes, and their Appendix D shows it does not impair quality and self-deactivates once logits settle (~30% into training). At 521M this almost certainly never triggers, so logging-only is the right cost/benefit.  
  gain: Insurance against a class of loss spike that would otherwise cost you a multi-day restart. Zero cost if it never fires.  
  risk: Adds a small logging hook inside attention. Make sure the metric is computed under no_grad and only on the logging step so it does not become a throughput tax. Note you use GQA (4 KV heads for 20 query heads), so a QK-Clip implementation must scale the shared K projections carefully — another reason to log first and implement only on demand.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Structure the decision as a single 4,000-step, fixed-seed, fixed-data-order A/B: arm A = current config; arm B = Muon (lr 1.2e-4, momentum 0.95, wd 0.1) + AdamW(0.9, 0.999, eps 1e-10) on embeddings/norms. Also log tokens/sec for both. Adopt Muon only if arm B is >= 0.02 nats better at step 4,000 AND the tokens/sec penalty is < 8%.**  
  why: The expected effect size is 0.037 nats at 10.4B tokens; at 4,000 steps (131M tokens) the gap should already be visible and directionally larger, since matrix-optimizer advantages are widest early. The 0.02-nat threshold gives margin over seed noise. Gating on measured tokens/sec is essential because the Newton-Schulz cost on your specific hardware is the one number no paper can give you — it spans 3% to 10.4% of step time depending on achieved matmul throughput (14.16 TFLOP/step against a 2.43 s step).  
  gain: Converts a 9-day bet into a 3-hour experiment. Also produces the tokens/sec measurement you need to decide whether to keep gradient checkpointing off.  
  risk: 4,000 steps at 1x-Chinchilla scale is only 1.25% of the run and early curves can mislead — the benchmark explicitly warns that early loss curves 'exaggerate performance gaps' and can 'reverse the eventual ranking'. Muon's advantage, however, is one of the cases where the early signal has held up across 130M/300M/520M/1.2B, so the reversal risk is low. Re-verify at step 20,000 before committing past the first checkpoint.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Evaluation methodology for ~500M-parameter base and instruct LLMs (2024–2026), applied to Plasma 1.1 (521M, 10.5B tokens, RTX 5080 16GB)
### Findings
- **Plasma 1.1's compute budget is 100-1600x smaller than every ~500M reference model you named, so their published scores are NOT the right target. Plasma: 6ND = 6 x 521e6 x 10.5e9 = 3.3e19 FLOPs at 20.2 tokens/param (Chinchilla-optimal). SmolLM2-360M: 4T tokens = 8.7e21 (265x Plasma, 11,050 tok/param). Gemma-3-270M: 6T tokens = 9.6e21 (294x). Qwen2.5-0.5B: ~18T tokens = 5.3e22 (1600x). Pythia-410M: 300B tokens = 7.4e20 (22x). Even Pythia-70M (300B tokens, 1.26e20) has 3.8x Plasma's compute.**  
  SmolLM2-360M card: 'trained on 4 trillion tokens'. Gemma-3-270M card: 'the 270M with 6 trillion tokens'. Pythia trained 300B tokens (Mamba Table 1 comparison note: 'Pythia models trained for 300B tokens'). Qwen2.5 pretrained on 18T tokens. Arithmetic mine: 6*5.21e8*1.05e10=3.28e19 vs 6*3.62e8*4e12=8.69e21.  
  _src: https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M , https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-3-270m , https://arxiv.org/html/2312.00752v2 , https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen2.5-llm/_
- **The single closest published reference point to Plasma 1.1 is the DCLM 400M-1x scale: 412M params, 8.2B training tokens, 2.0e19 train FLOPs, 26 H100-hours. Plasma is 1.6x that FLOP budget. DCLM explicitly validates that 400M-scale models still give reliable ranking signal ('models as small as 400M parameters can still provide signal on which training sets perform better at larger scales', high rank correlation with 7B-1x).**  
  DCLM Table 1 scales: 400M-1x = 412M params / 8.2B tokens / 2.0e19 FLOPs / 26 H100-hrs; 1B-1x = 1.4B / 28.8B / 2.4e20; 7B-1x = 6.9B / 138B / 5.7e21.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2406.11794v3_
- **Actual reported ~500M-class scores (base models). SmolLM2-360M (0-shot, lighteval, cloze): HellaSwag 54.5, ARC-avg 53.0, PIQA 71.7, MMLU-cloze 35.8, CommonsenseQA 38.0, TriviaQA 16.9, WinoGrande 52.5, OpenBookQA 37.4, GSM8K(5-shot) 3.2. Qwen2.5-0.5B same harness: HellaSwag 51.2, ARC-avg 45.4, PIQA 69.9, MMLU-cloze 33.7, CSQA 31.6, TriviaQA 4.3, WinoGrande 54.1, OBQA 37.4, GSM8K 33.4. SmolLM2-135M: HellaSwag 42.1, ARC-avg 43.9, PIQA 68.4, MMLU 31.5, CSQA 33.9, TriviaQA 4.1, WinoGrande 51.3, OBQA 34.6, GSM8K 1.4.**  
  Verbatim from the SmolLM2-360M and SmolLM2-135M HF model cards' 'Base Pre-Trained Model' tables. All zero-shot unless noted; 'we use lighteval to run them'.  
  _src: https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M , https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M_
- **Gemma-3-270M PT (Google's own shot settings): HellaSwag 40.9 (10-shot), BoolQ 61.4 (0-shot), PIQA 67.7 (0-shot), TriviaQA 15.4 (5-shot), ARC-c 29.0 (25-shot), ARC-e 57.7 (0-shot), WinoGrande 52.0 (5-shot). IT variant: HellaSwag 37.7, PIQA 66.2, ARC-c 28.2, WinoGrande 52.3, BBH 26.7, IFEval 51.2. Google reports NO MMLU, GSM8K, HumanEval or MBPP for 270M — a tell that those are uninformative at that size.**  
  Gemma-3-270M model card evaluation tables, reproduced with shot counts.  
  _src: https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-3-270m_
- **Qwen2.5-0.5B's official code/math numbers are outliers and should NOT be used as a target: MMLU 47.5 (5-shot), MMLU-pro 15.7, BBH 20.3 (3-shot), ARC-C 35.6 (25-shot), TruthfulQA 40.2, WinoGrande 56.3 (5-shot), HellaSwag 52.1 (10-shot), MATH 19.5 (4-shot), GSM8K 41.6 (4-shot), HumanEval 30.5 (0-shot), MBPP 39.3, MultiPL-E 18.9. A 0.5B model scoring 30.5 HumanEval / 41.6 GSM8K is far outside what any other 500M model achieves (SmolLM2-360M GSM8K = 3.2) and is consistent with the documented pattern of Qwen models showing anomalously high math scores attributed to GSM8K/MATH exposure.**  
  Qwen2.5-LLM blog base-model table (exact values above). Independent contamination note: 'Several Qwen models showed anomalously high mathematical reasoning scores, suggesting exposure to GSM8K and MATH training data during pretraining.'  
  _src: https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen2.5-llm/ , https://arxiv.org/html/2404.09937v2_
- **Pythia-410M (300B tokens, 22x Plasma compute) is the realistic ceiling-ish anchor for a Pile-quality run: LAMBADA-OpenAI ppl 10.84 / acc 51.4, HellaSwag 40.6, PIQA 66.9, ARC-e 52.1, ARC-c 24.6, WinoGrande 53.8, avg 48.2 (Mamba Table 1, zero-shot). Independent harness run (MobiLlama Table 4): HellaSwag 40.85, TruthfulQA 41.22, MMLU 27.25, ARC-C 26.19, CrowsPairs 64.20, PIQA 67.19, RACE 30.71, SIQA 41.40, WinoGrande 53.12, avg 43.57. Note ARC-c 24.6-26.2 vs 25% random and MMLU 27.25 vs 25% random — both are at chance.**  
  Mamba paper zero-shot table and MobiLlama Table 4, both reproduced verbatim.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2312.00752v2 , https://arxiv.org/html/2402.16840v1_
- **Well-trained sub-500M models (OpenELM, 1.5T tokens): OpenELM-270M zero-shot ARC-c 26.45, ARC-e 45.08, BoolQ 53.98, HellaSwag 46.71, PIQA 69.75, SciQ 84.70, WinoGrande 53.91. OpenELM-450M: ARC-c 27.56, ARC-e 48.06, BoolQ 55.78, HellaSwag 53.97, PIQA 72.31, SciQ 87.20, WinoGrande 58.01. On the 5-shot OpenLLM-leaderboard suite these same models score MMLU 25.72 and 26.01 — i.e. exactly random — while Pythia-410M gets 25.99 and Pythia-1.4B 25.56. MMLU in MCF form is pure noise below ~1.5B params.**  
  OpenELM Table 4(a) and the OpenLLM-leaderboard table: MMLU column reads 25.72 (OpenELM-270M), 26.01 (OpenELM-450M), 25.99 (Pythia-410M), 26.09 (MobiLlama-0.5B), 25.56 (Pythia-1.4B), 23.87 (MobiLlama-1.26B) — all within +/-1 point of the 25% random baseline.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2404.14619v1_
- **TinyLlama's checkpoint progression is the best public data on WHEN benchmarks emerge. At 103B tokens (1.1B params, 6.8e20 FLOPs ~ 21x Plasma): HellaSwag 43.50, OBQA 29.80, WinoGrande 53.28, ARC-c 24.32, ARC-e 44.91, BoolQ 59.66, PIQA 67.30, avg 46.11. At 3T tokens: HellaSwag 59.20, OBQA 36.00, WinoGrande 59.12, ARC-c 30.12, ARC-e 55.25, BoolQ 57.83, PIQA 73.29, avg 52.99. Across a 29x increase in tokens, ARC-c moved only 24.3->30.1, WinoGrande 53.3->59.1, and BoolQ went DOWN (59.66->57.83, and 63.21 at 2T). HellaSwag (+15.7), ARC-e (+10.3), OBQA (+6.2) and PIQA (+6.0) carry nearly all the signal.**  
  TinyLlama EVAL.md, acc_norm, zero-shot, lm-evaluation-harness. Full table reproduced above.  
  _src: https://github.com/jzhang38/TinyLlama/blob/main/EVAL.md_
- **Ai2's Signal-and-Noise framework quantifies which benchmarks are usable for decisions at small scale. Highest SNR (primary metric): TriviaQA 27.9, SQuAD 23.8, ARC-Easy 21.0, Jeopardy 20.2. Lowest: PIQA 4.2, AGI-Eval 2.5, MBPP 2.0, Minerva-MATH 1.9, GSM8K 1.2. Crucially PIQA is far above random chance yet has terrible SNR — 'above chance' and 'informative' are different properties. ARC-Challenge showed a 1.7% accuracy range across the final 30 checkpoints of a 1B model purely from checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise.**  
  Signal and Noise (Heineman et al., Ai2, Aug 2025), Table 4 SNR values and Sec. 5.3; noise defined as relative std dev of the final n checkpoints. Repo README confirms signal = max score dispersion / mean, noise = std across final checkpoints.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.13144v1 , https://github.com/allenai/signal-and-noise , https://allenai.org/blog/signal-noise_
- **Switching from discrete accuracy to bits-per-byte (BPB) of the gold answer is the single biggest measurement win at small scale. Ai2 measured SNR improvements: TriviaQA 27.9 -> 61.8, MBPP 2.0 -> 41.8, GSM8K 1.2 -> 7.0. Decision accuracy improved for 90.0% of all benchmarks: GSM8K 46% -> 77%, MBPP 68% -> 93%, Minerva MATH 51% -> 90%.**  
  Signal and Noise, Fig. 2 and Sec. 5; Ai2 blog reproduces the same numbers.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2508.13144v1 , https://allenai.org/blog/signal-noise_
- **DataDecide (Ai2, 2025; 14 model sizes 4M-1B, 25 data recipes, 3 seeds, 100:1 token/param) confirms: continuous likelihood metrics beat accuracy at small scale, ranked Correct Prob > Total Prob > Norm Correct Prob > Margin > Accuracy. Character-normalized variants consistently outperform token-normalized. Using Correct Prob instead of discrete accuracy lets HumanEval and MBPP reach 80% decision accuracy — otherwise they are useless. ARC-Easy is 'consistently predictable with 5 orders of magnitude less compute'; SocialIQA and BoolQ have minimal signal at small scale; math benchmarks stay at 'near trivial decision accuracy regardless of metric'.**  
  DataDecide paper: 10 OLMES tasks, decision-accuracy analysis; 'Correct Prob and Total Prob leads to decision accuracy at least as good as any other metric for most small scales'; at 150M params several benchmarks hit >80% decision accuracy at 0.01% of full-1B compute.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2504.11393v1 , https://arxiv.org/html/2504.11393v2_
- **Multiple-choice formulation (MCF, 'answer A/B/C/D') is a trap below ~1-3B params. OLMo's task-scaling-ladder paper states 'MC capability does not emerge until the model has several billion parameters' and observes three phases in MC accuracy (random -> sudden jump -> steady growth). OLMES therefore evaluates BOTH cloze (CF) and MCF and takes the better one; below ~7B, CF wins. SmolLM2 notes that above-random MMLU under MCF only appeared after extremely long training, which they treat as a surprising emergent effect.**  
  OLMo ladder paper: they exclude BoolQ and Winogrande 'as the task loss and accuracy are noisier for these tasks'; predictable tasks MMLU/HellaSwag/PIQA/SocialIQA reach <2 point absolute error using bits-per-byte as the intermediate feature. OLMES: 'evaluate each model using both the MCF and CF formulations, and the best performing one is used'; weaker models are near-random on MCF.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/html/2412.04403v2 , https://arxiv.org/html/2406.08446v1 , https://arxiv.org/html/2502.02737v1_

### Recommendations
- [high] **STOP using scripts/benchmark_1.1_vs_1.0.py (n=25, 0/1/2 keyword scoring) and scripts/eval_holdout.py (n=16, gate at 13/16) as the headline metric or as a training gate. Retire the '1.0 = 43%, 1.1 = 41%' figure entirely — it has an SE of ~9.9pp and a 95% CI of roughly +/-19pp. Keep both scripts, but demote them to smoke tests printed with an explicit '(n=25, SE ~10pp, NOT a decision metric)' banner, and change eval_holdout.py's exit gate from `passed >= 13` to `passed >= 8` (i.e. only fail on catastrophic regression, ~2 SE below the midpoint).**  
  why: With 25 items on a 0-2 scale and near-bimodal outcomes, per-item sd ~0.99 => SE = 0.99/2/sqrt(25) = 0.099. Paired across models at r=0.5 the SE of the difference is unchanged at ~9.9pp. Miller (Anthropic, 2024) shows you need ~969 items to detect 3pp at 80% power and recommends 'new evals should contain at least 1,000 questions'. Every training decision made off these two scripts so far was made off noise.  
  gain: Removes a class of false conclusions. Prevents you from re-running 9-day trainings chasing a 2-point difference that is 0.2 SE. Directly explains why 1.0 (235M) 'beat' 1.1 (521M).  
  risk: You temporarily lose your only end-to-end behavioural number until the harness suite (rec #2) is wired up. Mitigate by doing rec #2 first.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Wire Plasma into EleutherAI lm-evaluation-harness with a ~150-line adapter: subclass `lm_eval.api.model.LM`, implement `loglikelihood(self, requests) -> list[tuple[float, bool]]`, `loglikelihood_rolling(self, requests) -> list[tuple[float, bool]]`, `generate_until(self, requests) -> list[str]`; decorate `@register_model('plasma')`; import in `lm_eval/models/__init__.py`. Batch the loglikelihood requests at micro-batch 16-32 (forward-only, no grad, no checkpointing, bf16 autocast) and left-truncate contexts to 1024 tokens to respect Plasma's context. Invoke with `python -m lm_eval --model plasma --model_args ckpt=...,cfg=...,tok=... --tasks <suite> --batch_size 32 --log_samples --output_path runs/eval/<step>`.**  
  why: The harness is the only way to get numbers comparable to SmolLM2/Pythia/Qwen published tables, and its per-task bootstrap stderr is reported automatically. `loglikelihood_rolling` is the exact hook for `bits_per_byte`/`byte_perplexity`, which finding #10 shows is the highest-value metric at this scale. Plasma is a custom PyTorch model with a SentencePiece tokenizer, so HFLM won't work — the LM subclass is the documented path.  
  gain: Turns 25 noisy prompts into ~30,000 scored items with SEs of 0.4-2.1pp per task. Makes 1.0 vs 1.1 resolvable at the 1-2 point level instead of the 20-point level.  
  risk: Plasma's 1024 context truncates a few long BoolQ/RACE contexts (BoolQ passages average ~110 words, fine; MMLU 5-shot prompts can exceed 1024 — use 0-shot or 2-shot for MMLU). Left-truncation silently changes scores if you're not consistent; pin the truncation policy and record it in the results JSON.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Define 'Plasma-CORE' as the headline aggregate: exactly 6 tasks that clear chance at 3e19 FLOPs — LAMBADA-OpenAI (acc), SciQ (acc_norm), ARC-Easy (acc_norm), PIQA (acc_norm), HellaSwag (acc_norm), OpenBookQA (acc_norm). Report it as DCLM-style centered accuracy: centered_i = (acc_i - random_i)/(1 - random_i), with random = 0.0/0.25/0.25/0.50/0.25/0.25, then average the 6. Report the raw per-task accuracy + stderr underneath. Explicitly EXCLUDE from the aggregate: BoolQ, MMLU, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, SIQA, CommonsenseQA, GSM8K, HumanEval, MBPP — print them in a separate 'at/near chance — do not use for decisions' block.**  
  why: FineWeb's three criteria (low inter-run variance, monotonic over training, above random baseline at this model size) plus Ai2's SNR ranking (ARC-Easy 21.0 high; PIQA 4.2, GSM8K 1.2 low) plus DataDecide (ARC-Easy predictable at 5 orders of magnitude less compute; BoolQ/SocialIQA minimal signal) all converge on this set. BoolQ's 62.2% majority baseline exceeds what OpenELM-270M (53.98), TinyLlama-3T (57.83) and Gemma-3-270M (61.4) achieve — including it in an average is actively misleading. DCLM's centered accuracy is the standard way to make heterogeneous chance levels averageable.  
  gain: A single number with a real SE of ~0.6-0.8pp (inverse-variance weighted across 6 tasks totalling ~21k items) that moves monotonically with training, instead of a 25-item number with SE 10pp. You can finally answer 'is 1.1 better than 1.0' with a p-value.  
  risk: PIQA has high absolute accuracy but low SNR (4.2) — it will contribute level but not discrimination. Keep it for comparability with published tables; if it flattens, drop it from the aggregate and report separately.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Use the CLOZE formulation (rank continuations by likelihood), never the A/B/C/D multiple-choice formulation, for every Plasma eval until/unless Plasma exceeds ~2B params. Apply OLMES per-task normalization exactly: PMI (unconditional-likelihood) normalization for ARC-Challenge, CommonsenseQA, OpenBookQA; character-length normalization (harness `acc_norm`) for ARC-Easy, HellaSwag, PIQA, SocialIQA, MMLU; NO normalization (raw `acc`) for BoolQ and WinoGrande. Report both `acc` and `acc_norm` for every task in the JSON regardless, so you can retro-fit.**  
  why: OLMo's ladder paper: 'MC capability does not emerge until the model has several billion parameters', with three phases (random -> jump -> growth). OLMES evaluates both and takes the better; below ~7B, CF wins. OLMES's Table 1 shows setup choices alone swing OpenBookQA by 29 points (26.0 to 55.2) — so the setup must be pinned, not improvised. PMI requires a second forward pass on the bare answer string; budget for it.  
  gain: Moves ARC-C/CSQA/OBQA off the random floor onto a real signal, and makes your numbers directly comparable to SmolLM2's 'MMLU (cloze)' column (35.8 for 360M) rather than to an unrelated MCF number.  
  risk: PMI normalization roughly doubles the forward passes for 3 tasks. Character-length normalization in the harness is char-based despite docs saying bytes (issue #3278) — irrelevant for pure-ASCII English benchmarks, but note it if you ever add non-ASCII evals.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **For every benchmark where accuracy is at chance (MMLU, ARC-C, GSM8K, HumanEval, MBPP, TriviaQA, WinoGrande), log the CONTINUOUS metric instead: bits-per-byte of the gold answer (BPB = NLL_nats / ln(2) / len(answer.encode('utf-8'))), plus character-normalized 'Correct Prob'. Track these from step 0 even while accuracy sits at 25%. Concretely: add a `--tasks mmlu,gsm8k,humaneval` run with `metric: bits_per_byte` alongside the accuracy run, or compute gold-answer BPB directly in your own eval loop.**  
  why: Ai2 measured SNR going from 27.9->61.8 (TriviaQA), 2.0->41.8 (MBPP), 1.2->7.0 (GSM8K) by switching to BPB, and decision accuracy from 46%->77% (GSM8K), 68%->93% (MBPP), 51%->90% (Minerva MATH); 90.0% of all benchmarks improved. DataDecide independently ranks Correct Prob > Total Prob > Norm Correct Prob > Margin > Accuracy at small scale, with character-normalized beating token-normalized, and shows HumanEval/MBPP reaching 80% decision accuracy only via Correct Prob. This is exactly the 'discontinuous metric hides smooth progress' effect.  
  gain: You get a smooth, monotone, low-noise curve for code and math from step ~5,000 onward instead of a flat 0% line for the whole 9-day run. This is the single change that lets you tell whether the code/math data is doing anything before the run ends.  
  risk: BPB is not comparable to published pass@1 numbers, so you still need the accuracy run for external comparison. Also BPB on HumanEval rewards memorized solutions — pair it with the decontamination in rec #10.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Build a two-tier in-training eval loop. TIER-0 every 2,000 optimizer steps (~65.5M tokens): (a) bits-per-byte on 5 fixed held-out domain slices — FineWeb-Edu, code, Wikipedia, StackExchange, arXiv — at 1M tokens each; (b) 4 accuracy tasks: LAMBADA-OpenAI (5,153), SciQ (1,000), ARC-Easy (2,376), PIQA (1,838); (c) gold-answer BPB on 500-item subsamples of MMLU, GSM8K, HumanEval. Estimated ~1.3M scored tokens ~= 35s at ~40k tok/s forward-only. TIER-1 every 20,000 steps: the full Plasma-CORE 6 + the at-chance block, MMLU subsampled to 1,000 balanced items. Estimated ~6.5M tokens ~= 3-5 minutes.**  
  why: At 2.4 s/step you get ~36,000 steps/day and 320,000 steps ~= 8.9 days. Tier-0 at 160 invocations x 35s = 1.6 h = 0.7% overhead; Tier-1 at 16 invocations x 5 min = 1.3 h = 0.6% overhead. Total eval overhead ~1.3% — negligible against a 9-day run, and it converts a blind 9-day run into an instrumented one. Paloma's guidance (100k tokens/domain minimum, 1M/source; variance falls as 1/n) sets the 1M-token slice size.  
  gain: Detects a broken data shard, a bad LR, or a stalled capability within ~1.5 hours instead of at day 9. Given that both 1.0 and 1.1 previously landed at ~0% on code and reasoning, early detection is worth more than any single hyperparameter change.  
  risk: Eval must run with `torch.no_grad()`, gradient checkpointing DISABLED, and the optimizer state untouched; allocate eval batches after `torch.cuda.empty_cache()` to avoid fragmenting the 16GB during a run. Keep eval micro-batch <= 16 at seq 1024 to stay under ~2GB of activations.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Carve the validation set out at the DOCUMENT level BEFORE shard construction, not as a slice of built shards: hold out 1M tokens each from FineWeb-Edu, code, Wikipedia, StackExchange and arXiv (5M tokens = 0.066% of the 7.52B corpus), write them to `data/val_slices/<domain>.bin`, and never let those documents enter any training shard. Report BPB per domain AND the corpus-weighted average (0.597 FineWeb-Edu, 0.129 code, 0.124 Wiki, 0.102 StackExchange, 0.047 arXiv). Never report raw perplexity for cross-model comparison against Plasma 1.0.**  
  why: Plasma trains 10.5B tokens from a 7.52B-token corpus = ~1.4 epochs, so any val slice taken from within the shard set is seen during training and will show artificially falling loss. Paloma demonstrates a single model spanning perplexity 391,171 (arXiv) to 14 (peS2o) across related domains, and that CC-only models go NON-MONOTONIC on some domains while total loss improves — a single aggregate ppl would hide exactly that. Plasma 1.0 and 1.1 have different tokenizers (48k SentencePiece for 1.1), so perplexity is not comparable across them; BPB is, because it normalizes by UTF-8 bytes.  
  gain: A trustworthy, tokenizer-independent loss curve that you can compare to Plasma 1.0 and to published BPB numbers, plus per-domain diagnosis (e.g. 'code BPB has been flat for 40k steps' is actionable in a way that 'val loss 3.2' is not).  
  risk: Losing 5M tokens of training data is negligible. The real risk is forgetting to re-derive the holdout when you rebuild shards — add an assertion in the shard builder that hashes of val documents are absent from the training shards.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do NOT trust the raw final checkpoint. Save a checkpoint every 2,000 steps, and for reporting/release average the last 8-10 checkpoints (spacing 2,000 steps = 65.5M tokens, window 16-20k steps) with a simple moving average of fp32 state dicts computed ON CPU. Concretely: load each `.pt` to CPU, accumulate `sum += sd[k].float()`, divide by N, cast back, save as `<name>_soup.pt`. Report both the raw-final and the souped numbers.**  
  why: LAWA measured, at exactly Plasma's scale, GPT2-medium 355M perplexity 2.855 -> 2.819 and 125M zero-shot average 47.89 -> 51.15 (+3.3 points, LAMBADA 27.65 -> 33.77), beating both EMA (2.845) and SWA (2.837); optimal k = 5, spacing 1K steps. PMA found N=15 ~1 point better than N=3 and settled on N=10, with the merge interval scaling with model size. Ai2 additionally uses averaging purely to cut evaluation noise: +2.4% decision accuracy on a 30-task average, using the final 5 checkpoints for small models.  
  gain: Realistically +1 to +3 points on Plasma-CORE and a measurable BPB drop, for zero extra training compute. Also cuts checkpoint-to-checkpoint eval noise (Ai2 measured a 1.7pp accuracy range across a 1B model's final 30 checkpoints on ARC-C).  
  risk: Disk: 10 fp32 checkpoints x 521M x 4B = 20.8GB (save bf16 at 1.04GB each = 10.4GB if space is tight, but average in fp32). VRAM: do the averaging on CPU — a second fp32 GPU copy costs 2.08GB you do not have alongside fused-AdamW states. IMPORTANT CAVEAT: PMA found that at the END of a cosine decay the checkpoints are 'already tightly converged within a specific local optimum', so gains shrink; harvest checkpoints from the region where LR is still >= ~3e-5, not the final 1.2e-5 tail.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Seriously consider replacing the cosine-to-1.2e-5 schedule with WSD (warmup-stable-decay): warmup 3,000 steps to 1.2e-4, hold constant until ~step 280,000, then linear/1-sqrt decay to 1.2e-5 over the last ~40,000 steps. Keep saving every 2,000 steps during the stable phase.**  
  why: Two independent payoffs. (1) LAWA: 'models trained with higher LR show pronounced gains' from averaging; low-LR training 'showed minimal gains'. A constant 1.2e-4 stable phase is exactly the regime where averaging pays. (2) PMA: merging checkpoints trained at a constant learning rate 'achieves significant performance improvements and enables accurate prediction of annealing behavior' — i.e. you can predict the final annealed score from the stable phase without paying for the decay, and PMA-init lets you branch multiple short anneals (e.g. different SFT mixes) from a merged stable checkpoint with 'smoother GradNorm curves' and recovery from loss spikes.  
  gain: Lets you run 2-4 cheap 10-20k-step anneal/SFT branches off one merged stable checkpoint instead of committing the whole 9-day budget to one guess, plus recovers the ~1-3 point averaging gain that a fully-decayed cosine forfeits.  
  risk: Changing the LR schedule mid-project invalidates comparability with the current 1.1 run. Only do this if you are starting a new run. WSD's stable-phase loss looks worse than cosine's at the same step — do not panic-abort; the decay recovers it.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Decontaminate before publishing any number. (a) Pretrain corpus: run HuggingFace datatrove's `NGramsDecontFilter` with n_grams=13 (override the default 12 to match the GPT-3/FineWeb convention), find_overlap_ngrams=True, index built from your eval task set, removing whole documents on match. (b) SFT set: n-grams are NOT sufficient — run an embedding-top-k + LLM-comparison pass (lm-sys/llm-decontaminator) over MetaMath vs GSM8K/MATH test, Alpaca/CodeAlpaca vs HumanEval/MBPP, and WizardLM/SlimOrca vs MMLU. (c) Report the removal rate; expect ~0.1-0.3% of pretrain documents.**  
  why: Plasma's exact SFT sources are the ones with measured rephrased-contamination: CodeAlpaca 12.8% HumanEval, MATHInstruct 15.0% MATH-test, StarCoder-Data 15.9% HumanEval, The Stack (4G) 18.9% HumanEval, RedPajama (16G) 8.5% HumanEval, FLAN-CoT 0.5% MMLU, WizardLM-Evol-Instruct 0.5% MMLU. MetaMath is GSM8K-derived by construction. Plasma's pretrain corpus also has 0.97B code and 0.77B StackExchange tokens. FineWeb removed 0.16% of English docs at 13-gram; Dolma removed 0.02% of docs at paragraph level. Reference points: FineWeb uses document-level removal, Dolma uses paragraph-level (>13 Unicode tokens, Bloom filter, overlap threshold 1.0) and found paragraph-level 'preferred over document-based due to higher detection'.  
  gain: Protects the headline claim. Without this, any nonzero GSM8K or HumanEval score from Plasma 1.1 is unfalsifiable — and the current SFT mix makes a fake nonzero score more likely than a real one. Costs <0.3% of training data.  
  risk: The LLM-decontaminator pass needs an external judge model and is O(n) over the SFT set (168k sequences) — budget a few hours and some API spend, or run it only on the ~40k math/code sequences that actually matter.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Set these as Plasma 1.1's realistic post-pretrain targets at 320k steps / 10.5B tokens (0-shot, cloze, harness metrics), and treat anything above them as suspicious. LAMBADA-OpenAI acc 32-45% (ppl 15-30); SciQ acc_norm 72-85%; PIQA acc_norm 62-67%; ARC-Easy acc_norm 44-52%; HellaSwag acc_norm 32-38%; OpenBookQA acc_norm 29-34%; CommonsenseQA 20-28%; WinoGrande 50-53% (chance); ARC-Challenge acc_norm 24-28% (chance); MMLU cloze 25-28% (chance); BoolQ 55-62% (at/below majority); TriviaQA 0.5-4%; GSM8K 0-2%; HumanEval pass@1 0%; MBPP pass@1 0-1%. Plasma-CORE (centered, 6 tasks) target: 18-26%.**  
  why: Anchored on Pythia-410M at 22x Plasma's compute (LAMBADA 51.4, HellaSwag 40.6, PIQA 66.9, ARC-e 52.1, ARC-c 24.6, WinoGrande 53.8, MMLU 27.25) and OpenELM-270M at 1.5T tokens (ARC-e 45.08, HellaSwag 46.71, PIQA 69.75, SciQ 84.70), discounted for Plasma's 3.3e19 FLOPs but credited for FineWeb-Edu-grade data (DCLM/FineWeb show dataset choice is worth several CORE points at fixed compute; 400M-1x, 412M/8.2B, is validated as giving real ranking signal). SmolLM2-360M (265x compute) at HellaSwag 54.5 / ARC-avg 53.0 / MMLU 35.8 is NOT a reachable target and should not be framed as one.  
  gain: Replaces a vague 'beat 1.0' goal with 14 falsifiable numbers, each with a known SE, and pre-commits you to not over-claiming. Also sets correct expectations with stakeholders: a 3.3e19-FLOP model at 0% HumanEval is normal, not a failure.  
  risk: These are my projections, not measured values — the compute-matched public data point (DCLM 400M-1x per-task accuracies) is in an appendix I could not retrieve. Treat the ranges as priors to be overwritten by Plasma's own first Tier-1 eval, and widen them by +/-3pp.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Rebuild the generative/instruct eval as a paired, powered, judged comparison: >= 500 prompts (target 1,000) held out from all SFT data, stratified across the categories you already use (math, fact, format, conv, essay, reason, expl, creative); run Plasma 1.0 and 1.1 on the identical prompts; score by pairwise A/B with a strong external judge with randomized response order and a tie option; report win-rate with CLUSTERED standard errors (cluster by prompt template/category) and a paired bootstrap CI. Format every reported number as 'x.x% (s.e.)'.**  
  why: Miller (Anthropic): 'new evals should contain at least 1,000 questions'; ~969 questions are needed to detect a 3pp difference at 80% power; clustered SEs on real benchmarks are 1.10x-3.05x naive SEs, 'over 3X larger'; paired differences on identical questions cut variance ~33% at r=0.5. Your current 25 keyword-matched prompts fail all of these. Keyword matching also mis-scores: 'inst1' passes if the word 'apple' appears anywhere, and 'reason1' passes on the word 'same' regardless of reasoning.  
  gain: Reduces the SE on the 1.0-vs-1.1 behavioural comparison from ~9.9pp to ~1.5-2.2pp, making a 5-point real improvement detectable. Also removes the keyword-gaming failure mode.  
  risk: Judge cost and judge bias (position bias, length bias). Mitigate with order randomization, a tie option, and a small human-labelled calibration subset (~50 pairs) to measure judge-human agreement.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **For MMLU specifically, do not use all 57 subjects. Precompute per-subject SNR from Plasma's own checkpoint series (signal = spread across your 1.0/1.1/ablation checkpoints; noise = std dev across the final 5 checkpoints of a run), keep the ~16 highest-SNR subjects, and report 'MMLU-16 (cloze, char-norm)' alongside the full 57-subject number. Subsample to 1,000 balanced items and keep the SAME items across all checkpoints.**  
  why: Ai2 found that using only 16 of MMLU's 57 subtasks gave a higher SNR than the full test set, +2.6% decision accuracy, and 32% lower scaling-law prediction error; the same trick gave +5% on AutoBencher with 6 subtasks. OLMES already prescribes subsampling to 1,000 when a set exceeds 1,500. Fixing the sample across checkpoints makes checkpoint-to-checkpoint comparisons paired, which is the single cheapest variance reduction available.  
  gain: Turns MMLU from a flat 25-27% noise line into something with a usable derivative, and cuts MMLU eval cost from ~6.7M scored tokens to ~0.5M (13x faster).  
  risk: MMLU-16 is not comparable to published MMLU numbers — always report the full-57 number too. Subject selection based on Plasma's own checkpoints risks overfitting the metric to your run; freeze the subject list once and never re-tune it.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Log every eval to an append-only CSV/JSONL keyed by (step, tokens_seen, task, metric, value, stderr, n_items, eval_config_hash), and when reporting a checkpoint's score, report the mean of the last 5 evaluated checkpoints, not the single last one. Fit and plot value vs log(tokens) per task; flag any task whose fitted slope is within 1 SE of zero over the last 50k steps as 'saturated/uninformative for this run'.**  
  why: Ai2's noise metric is literally the relative std dev of the final n checkpoints, and their intervention 1 (average the last 5 for small models, 30 for 13B) bought +2.4% decision accuracy on a 30-task average. ARC-Challenge alone spans 1.7pp across a 1B model's final 30 checkpoints from noise. FineWeb's criterion (2) is monotonicity over training — a slope test operationalizes it. The config hash prevents the classic failure of comparing scores across silently-changed prompt formats (OLMES Table 1: 29-point swing on OpenBookQA from setup alone).  
  gain: Cuts reported eval noise by roughly sqrt(5) = 2.2x for free, and automatically retires dead metrics instead of letting them clutter the dashboard.  
  risk: Averaging the last 5 evaluated checkpoints lags the true current score during rapid improvement — use it only for reporting/comparison, not for the live training dashboard.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **For the code claim specifically, replace 'HumanEval pass@1' as the primary code metric with a three-part measure: (1) HumanEval/MBPP gold-solution BPB (continuous, works from step 0); (2) a syntactic-validity rate — fraction of generations that `ast.parse()` successfully and define the requested function name (n=164+500, real signal at 500M); (3) pass@1 with the bigcode-evaluation-harness convention of 20 samples at temperature 0.2, top-p 0.95, reported with its stderr, expected to be ~0.**  
  why: HumanEval has only 164 problems; at a true 2% pass rate the SE is 1.09pp, so 0% and 2% are indistinguishable — the metric cannot support the claim either way. Ai2 measured MBPP SNR going 2.0 -> 41.8 by switching to BPB, and decision accuracy 68% -> 93%. DataDecide shows Correct Prob lifts HumanEval/MBPP to 80% decision accuracy where accuracy gives none. SmolLM used exactly the bigcode convention (temperature 0.2, top-p 0.95, 20 samples) and SmolLM-1.7B reached 24 pass@1 at ~10^22 FLOPs — 300x Plasma's budget, confirming pass@1 is not a reachable signal here.  
  gain: Gives you a code-capability curve that actually moves during the run, plus an honest 0% pass@1 with an error bar instead of an unqualified 0.  
  risk: Syntactic validity is gameable by a model that emits `def f(): pass` — pair it with BPB so trivial-but-parseable output is penalized.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **If you add an EMA of the weights during training, keep the EMA buffer in fp32 on PINNED CPU memory and update it every 100-200 steps (not every step), e.g. `ema.mul_(d).add_(param.detach().float().cpu(), alpha=1-d)` with d tuned so the effective horizon is ~10-20k steps. Do not allocate it on the GPU.**  
  why: A GPU-resident fp32 EMA copy of 521M params costs 2.08GB. Plasma already holds fp32 params (2.08GB) + fp32 grads (2.08GB) + fused-AdamW m,v (4.17GB) = 8.33GB of optimizer/parameter state on a ~17.1GB card before activations; adding 2.08GB is the difference between fitting and OOM once activations and fragmentation are counted. LAWA also found EMA underperforms latest-k averaging at exactly these sizes (GPT2-medium 355M: EMA 2.845 vs LAWA 2.819 vs baseline 2.855), so the checkpoint-soup route (rec #8) is both cheaper and better.  
  gain: Avoids an OOM at ~step 200k, and avoids spending VRAM on a technique that measured worse than the free CPU-side alternative.  
  risk: CPU-side EMA at every step would be a throughput killer (PCIe transfer of 2GB); the every-100-200-steps cadence keeps overhead under ~1%. If you skip EMA entirely and just use checkpoint souping, you lose nothing.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Maximizing training throughput for Plasma 1.1 (521M) on a single RTX 5080 (16GB, sm_120, Windows 11, PyTorch 2.12+cu128) — measured on the actual machine, not estimated
### Findings
- **The premise is wrong: RTX 5080 bf16 dense peak is ~112-121 TFLOP/s, NOT 450. The 450 figure is FP4 with 2:1 sparsity. You are at ~37-50% of real peak, not 12%. Headroom is ~2x, not 8x.**  
  Measured on this GPU: model-shaped bf16 GEMMs hit 102-117 TFLOP/s (M=2048: 101.8 qkv / 102.2 ffn_up / 110.7 ffn_down / 116.5 lm_head; M=16384: 110-118). Sustained 8192^3 loop = 3300 matmuls in 30s = 121 TFLOP/s (while sharing the GPU with the live run, so uncontended peak is somewhat higher). TechPowerUp/NVIDIA list RTX 5080 FP16/BF16 at 112.6 TFLOPS; NVIDIA's '1801 AI TOPS' marketing number is FP4 sparse. Memory bandwidth measured 817 GB/s of 960 GB/s spec. Your actual work: 3.334 GFLOP/token (6*521.5e6 params + causal attention 0.205 GFLOP/tok) x 13,500 tok/s = 45.0 TFLOP/s useful, 60.0 TFLOP/s executed including the gradient-checkpoint recompute.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_roofline.py, power_test.py on this machine); https://www.techpowerup.com/gpu-specs/geforce-rtx-5080.c4217 ; https://images.nvidia.com/aem-dam/Solutions/geforce/blackwell/nvidia-rtx-blackwell-gpu-architecture.pdf_
- **SMOKING GUN: the training run pulls only 68-91 W of a 360 W limit at 33-36 C, while nvidia-smi reports 100% 'utilization'. A genuinely compute-bound kernel on the same GPU at the same moment pulls 285-288 W at 54-56 C. nvidia-smi 'utilization' means 'a kernel was resident', not 'the SMs were busy' — the real signal is power draw.**  
  nvidia-smi dmon during training: pwr 68-91 W, gtemp 33-36 C, sm 100%, mem-controller 0-14% (mostly 1-3%), pclk pinned 2887-2902 MHz (NOT throttled). Same command while an 8192^3 bf16 matmul loop ran concurrently: pwr 285-288 W, gtemp 54-56 C. The GPU is capable of 4x the power it is currently drawing. Clocks at full boost rule out thermal/power throttling.  
  _src: Direct measurement, nvidia-smi dmon -s pucm on this machine_
- **CRITICAL: this PyTorch Windows build has NO FlashAttention compiled in at all. torch.backends.cuda.is_flash_attention_available() returns False. SDPA silently falls back to the Ampere-era CUTLASS memory-efficient kernel. Official PyTorch Windows wheels ship with USE_FLASH_ATTENTION=0.**  
  torch._C warning: 'Torch was not compiled with flash attention. (sdp_utils.cpp:810)'. is_flash_attention_available() = False. Profiler shows the kernels actually running are _Z40fmha_cutlassF_bf16_aligned_64x64_rf_sm80... (forward) and _Z52fmha_cutlassB_bf16_aligned_64x64_k64_seqaligned_sm80... (backward) — note 'sm80', Ampere-generation CUTLASS on a Blackwell sm_120 card. Forcing SDPBackend.FLASH_ATTENTION raises 'RuntimeError: No available kernel. Aborting execution.'  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_sdpa.py, torch.profiler) on this machine, torch 2.12.0.dev20260408+cu128_
- **BIGGEST SINGLE WIN: cuDNN attention is 2.94x faster than the memory-efficient kernel currently being selected, and is available but not chosen by default. Attention is ~25% of step time, so this alone is worth ~1.2x end-to-end.**  
  Contention-robust interleaved A/B, min of 60 reps at the exact training shape (B=2, 20Q/4KV heads, S=1024, D=64, bf16, fwd+bwd): default path (what your code does) 1.304 ms; forced MEM_EFF 1.319 ms (confirming default==mem_eff); forced CUDNN 0.444 ms = 2.94x. Median spread was tight (1.334 / 1.357 / 0.515). Numerics verified: cuDNN vs fp32 math reference max abs diff 7.84e-3, rel err 1.86e-3 — normal bf16 noise. Extrapolated: 26 layers x 0.86 ms saved x 16 accum ~ 360-425 ms off a 2420 ms step.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_sdpa3.py, bench_final.py) on this machine_
- **TRAP: enable_gqa=True — the 'obvious' GQA optimization — is 3.5x SLOWER on this build, because it disqualifies the memory-efficient backend and silently drops to the math path. It only helps if cuDNN is forced first.**  
  Interleaved min-of-60: expand-to-20-heads (current code) 1.304 ms; enable_gqa=True on default backends 4.560 ms (0.29x) with profiler showing NO fmha kernel at all (pure elementwise/math); enable_gqa=True + forced cuDNN 0.451 ms (2.89x), with output bit-identical to the expand version (max abs diff 0.00e+00). Backend acceptance matrix for enable_gqa=True: FLASH:X, MEM_EFF:X, CUDNN:ok, MATH:ok.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_sdpa3.py, bench_final.py) on this machine_
- **torch.compile works on Windows + Blackwell sm_120 and is worth 1.5-1.64x — but Triton was NOT INSTALLED on this machine, meaning torch.compile could not have emitted a single GPU kernel. Installing triton-windows required no Visual Studio (no MSVC/cl.exe present on this box) because a C compiler is bundled in the wheel.**  
  Before: 'import triton' -> ModuleNotFoundError. 'pip install -U "triton-windows<3.8"' installed triton 3.7.1 (from triton-windows 3.7.1.post27) and torch.compile then successfully JIT-compiled Triton kernels on sm_120. No Visual Studio found on this system ('NO VS FOUND', cl.exe not on PATH) yet compilation succeeded. Compatibility table: PyTorch 2.12-2.13 -> Triton 3.7. Measured speedups on a real-vocab proxy, interleaved min-of-25: at L=3, compile(max-autotune-no-cudagraphs)+cuDNN+CCE = 1.59x vs baseline; at L=6 = 1.64x. The trend rises with depth, so L=26 should land at ~1.7-1.85x.  
  _src: Direct measurement on this machine; https://github.com/woct0rdho/triton-windows (compatibility table, bundled TinyCC since post13)_
- **Blackwell sm_120 has only 101,376 bytes of usable shared memory per SM, so max-autotune emits a wall of scary-looking 'No valid triton configs / out of resource' errors for large-block matmul configs. These are non-fatal — Inductor discards those configs and picks a smaller block — but they will flood your logs.**  
  During max-autotune: 'RuntimeError: No valid triton configs. OutOfMemoryError: out of resource: triton_mm Required: 147456 Hardware limit: 101376' and Required: 196608. Autotune still completed and selected triton_mm_573 (BLOCK_M=128, BLOCK_N=64, BLOCK_K=32, num_stages=3, num_warps=4) at 0.358 ms, and the compiled model ran correctly and faster.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_launch.py autotune log) on this machine_
- **Apple's Cut Cross-Entropy (CCE) is the correct fused-loss choice here: at your micro_batch=2 it is BOTH 1.09x faster AND uses 1,776x less memory than the current full-logits cross-entropy. Liger Kernel is the wrong choice at this scale — its FusedLinearCrossEntropy is 5.5x SLOWER at mb=2.**  
  Measured at vocab=48000, hidden=1280, 2048 tokens (your exact mb=2 shape): full-logits CE 19.12 ms / 1054.7 MB peak; Apple CCE 17.62 ms (1.09x faster) / 0.6 MB (1775x less); chunked CE (8 chunks) 24.63 ms / 537.6 MB; Liger FLCE 104.67 ms (0.18x) / 374.8 MB. At mb=8 the ranking flips on time: full 44.22 ms / 4574.7 MB, chunked8 47.72 ms / 1823.3 MB, Liger 63.18 ms / 394.1 MB, CCE 65.78 ms / 0.6 MB. CCE installs cleanly on Windows ('pip install cut-cross-entropy'); Liger needs '--no-deps' because it pins upstream 'triton' which conflicts with 'triton-windows' (ResolutionImpossible across all 32 versions).  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_loss.py) on this machine; https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09009 (ICLR 2025 Oral, Gemma-2 2B: loss memory 24 GB -> 1 MB)_
- **Gradient checkpointing costs you 22-23% throughput and you do not need it — the memory budget says mb=2 without checkpointing fits comfortably.**  
  Measured 4-layer proxy: eager+ckpt 48.67 ms vs eager no-ckpt 39.58 ms = 1.23x. Interleaved real-vocab proxy at L=6: +cuDNN+ckpt 41.82 ms vs +cuDNN ckpt-OFF 35.64 ms = 1.17x. Memory budget for the real 521.5M model: fp32 params 2.09 + fp32 grads 2.09 + AdamW fp32 state 4.17 + autocast bf16 cache 1.04 = 9.39 GB fixed, leaving ~6.11 GB of ~15.5 GB usable. Activations without checkpointing ~50 KB/token/layer bf16 -> mb=2 needs ~2.66 GB (+1.05 GB if you keep full-logits CE, ~0 with CCE). mb=4 needs ~5.32 GB — fits only with CCE and no margin.  
  _src: Direct measurement + memory arithmetic on this machine_
- **NEGATIVE RESULT: the dataloader is not remotely a bottleneck — it delivers 776x more data than the GPU consumes. Do not spend time on num_workers/prefetch tuning.**
