  StreamingShardDataset + DataLoader measured standalone at your exact settings: num_workers=2/prefetch=2 -> 10,478,343 tok/s; num_workers=4/prefetch=4 -> 10,728,060 tok/s (no meaningful difference). Training consumes 13,500 tok/s. Shards are 205 MB each, 74 of them; the whole-shard np.fromfile inside __iter__ is amortized over ~100k sequences.  
  _src: Direct measurement on this machine, data/shards_1.1_v2_
- **NEGATIVE RESULT: increasing micro_batch is NOT a lever here. mb=2 is already 94% as efficient per token as the optimum, and it plateaus at mb=4. The GEMMs at M=2048 are already near peak.**  
  Per-token cost, L=3 real-vocab proxy, no ckpt, cuDNN, CCE: mb=1 12.494 us/tok; mb=2 10.435; mb=4 9.839 (1.06x vs mb=2); mb=8 9.814 (1.06x); mb=12 10.109; mb=16 10.135 (regresses). Corroborated by the roofline sweep: lm_head GEMM already hits 116.5 TFLOP/s at M=2048 vs 117.6 at M=16384.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_mb.py, bench_roofline.py) on this machine_
- **NEGATIVE RESULT: two commonly-recommended fixes do nothing here. (a) loss.item() per micro-step costs only ~3%, not the 16-sync disaster it looks like. (b) Replacing the complex-number RoPE with real cos/sin makes things 5.7x WORSE in eager, and the complex version causes ZERO torch.compile graph breaks.**  
  (a) 16 micro-steps with GPU-side accumulation 21.038 ms vs with .item() per step 21.792 ms = 3% — each micro-step is already ~150 ms so the sync is amortized. (b) RoPE fwd+bwd at your shape: complex (current) 0.066 ms; real cos/sin eager 0.378 ms (0.17x — it spawns many small kernels); real cos/sin compiled 0.041 ms (1.62x). torch._dynamo.explain on the real model: complex RoPE -> graph_count 1, graph_break_count 0, op_count 53; real RoPE -> graph_count 1, break_count 0, op_count 72. RoPE is only ~2.3% of step time regardless.  
  _src: Direct measurement (bench_parts.py, bench_graphbreak.py) on this machine_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Force cuDNN attention globally. Add to src/train/train.py right after torch.set_float32_matmul_precision("high") (line 72):

torch.backends.cuda.enable_cudnn_sdp(True)
torch.backends.cuda.enable_mem_efficient_sdp(False)
torch.backends.cuda.enable_flash_sdp(False)   # not built on Windows anyway
torch.backends.cuda.enable_math_sdp(False)    # fail loudly instead of silently going slow

Verify it took effect with:
from torch.profiler import profile, ProfilerActivity
with profile(activities=[ProfilerActivity.CUDA]) as p: model(x); torch.cuda.synchronize()
print([e.key for e in p.key_averages() if e.self_device_time_total>0 and 'sdpa' in e.key.lower()])
# want: cudnn_generated_fort_native_sdpa_..._flash_fprop_wmma_...
# NOT:  fmha_cutlassF_bf16_aligned_64x64_..._sm80**  
  why: PyTorch's Windows wheels are built without FlashAttention, so SDPA silently selects the Ampere-era CUTLASS mem-efficient kernel. cuDNN ships a genuine flash-attention kernel that is 2.94x faster at your exact shape, and it is present but not default-selected. Disabling math_sdp is important: it turns a future silent 3.5x slowdown into a loud error.  
  gain: 2.94x on the attention op (1.304 -> 0.444 ms per layer at mb=2). Attention is ~25% of step time, so ~1.18-1.22x end-to-end: 13,500 -> ~16,000-16,500 tok/s. One line of config, zero risk to convergence.  
  risk: Very low. Numerics verified against an fp32 math reference: max abs diff 7.84e-3, rel err 1.86e-3 — ordinary bf16 rounding. The one real risk is that disabling math_sdp will hard-error on your KV-cache inference path if cuDNN rejects the explicit attn_mask; scope the global switch to training only, or re-enable math around generation.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Install Triton and turn on torch.compile. First:

pip install -U "triton-windows<3.8"      # -> triton 3.7.1, matches torch 2.12, no MSVC needed

Then in src/train/train.py after the model is built and moved to device (line ~83), compile the model but NOT the loss:

model = Transformer(model_cfg).to(device)
...
if train_cfg.get("compile", True):
    torch._dynamo.config.cache_size_limit = 32
    model_fwd = torch.compile(model, mode="max-autotune-no-cudagraphs", dynamic=False)
else:
    model_fwd = model

Use model_fwd(x) in the training loop but keep model.parameters() / model.state_dict() for the optimizer, clipping, and checkpointing (torch.compile wraps, it does not copy). Keep drop_last=True so the shape never changes — with dynamic=False a changing batch shape triggers a full recompile.**  
  why: Triton was not installed on this machine at all, which means torch.compile was incapable of generating GPU kernels. The workload is dominated by hundreds of tiny memory-bound elementwise kernels (RMSNorm, SwiGLU, residuals, RoPE) — exactly what Inductor fuses. This is the direct fix for the 70W-at-100%-utilization signature. Use max-autotune-no-cudagraphs rather than reduce-overhead: CUDA graphs measured no better (20.99 vs 19.03 ms) and add capture fragility.  
  gain: Measured 1.59x at L=3 and 1.64x at L=6 for the full stack vs baseline, with the ratio rising as depth increases, so ~1.7x at L=26. Combined with cuDNN and no-checkpointing this is the bulk of the win. Expect one-time compile cost of 2-6 minutes on the first step.  
  risk: Medium-low. max-autotune will print a wall of 'No valid triton configs / out of resource: Required 147456, Hardware limit 101376' errors — these are sm_120's 100KB shared-memory limit rejecting large-block configs and are non-fatal; Inductor falls back correctly. Start with mode="default" (measured 1.50-1.52x, far less log noise) if you want the safer path. Watch for silent recompiles: set TORCH_LOGS=recompiles for the first 200 steps.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Turn OFF gradient checkpointing. In configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml:

  gradient_checkpointing: false

Keep micro_batch_size: 2 and gradient_accumulation: 16 exactly as they are — do not change the effective batch.**  
  why: Checkpointing recomputes every forward, adding ~33% FLOPs for memory you do not actually need. The budget: 2.09 (fp32 params) + 2.09 (grads) + 4.17 (AdamW fp32) + 1.04 (autocast bf16 cache) = 9.39 GB fixed, leaving ~6.11 GB of ~15.5 GB usable. mb=2 activations without checkpointing are ~2.66 GB. You have roughly 2.4 GB of headroom even keeping the current loss.  
  gain: 1.17-1.23x measured (48.67 -> 39.58 ms on the 4-layer proxy; 41.82 -> 35.64 ms at L=6 with cuDNN on). Stacks multiplicatively with cuDNN and compile.  
  risk: Medium — this is the one change that can OOM mid-run, and it would do so at step ~100k rather than immediately. Mitigate by launching with PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True and watching torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() for the first 500 steps. If it is above ~13 GB, re-enable checkpointing or pair this with the CCE loss below. Do NOT also raise micro_batch — see the next item.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Replace the full-logits cross-entropy with Apple's Cut Cross-Entropy.

pip install cut-cross-entropy

Add a hidden-state path to Transformer.forward (src/model/transformer.py) so the LM head can be fused into the loss:

    def forward(self, tokens, start_pos=0, kv_caches=None, return_hidden=False):
        ...
        x = self.norm(x)
        if return_hidden:
            return x                      # skip self.output
        return self.output(x)

Then in the training loop (src/train/train.py, replacing lines 206-217):

from cut_cross_entropy import linear_cross_entropy

with torch.autocast(device_type=device.type, dtype=dtype, enabled=use_amp):
    h = model_fwd(x, return_hidden=True)
    w = model.output.weight.to(torch.bfloat16)      # CCE requires a bf16/fp16 head
    loss = linear_cross_entropy(h.view(-1, h.size(-1)), w, y.view(-1)) / grad_accum

For the SFT runs with assistant-only masking, pass ignore_index instead of multiplying by a mask: set y = y.masked_fill(mask == 0, -100) and call linear_cross_entropy(..., y.view(-1), ignore_index=-100).**  
  why: The 48,000-vocab logits tensor is the single largest transient allocation in the step — 1,054.7 MB at mb=2 — and it buys nothing. CCE computes only the target logit and streams the log-sum-exp, so global memory for the loss is 0.6 MB. Uniquely at your micro-batch it is also faster, not just smaller, because it never writes the logit matrix to HBM.  
  gain: 1.09x on the loss (19.12 -> 17.62 ms) AND frees 1.05 GB, which is what makes turning off gradient checkpointing safe. Roughly 1.03-1.05x end-to-end on its own; its real value is de-risking the checkpointing change.  
  risk: Low-medium on correctness, but this touches the loss so validate before committing: run 50 steps and confirm the loss matches the current implementation to within ~1e-3. Note the tied-embedding subtlety — model.output.weight IS model.tok_emb.weight, and the .to(torch.bfloat16) cast is differentiable so gradients still flow to the tied parameter, but verify grad_norm stays in its current ~0.80 band. If you want zero risk, chunked CE (8 chunks) is a pure-PyTorch alternative: 537.6 MB, 0.78x speed, no new dependency.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Use enable_gqa=True ONLY after cuDNN is forced. In src/model/attention.py, delete the manual expansion (lines 51-57) and pass the 4-head K/V straight through:

        # expand kv heads  <- DELETE this whole block
        # KV_S = k.shape[2]
        # if self.num_kv_groups > 1: ...

        out = F.scaled_dot_product_attention(
            q, k, v,
            attn_mask=attn_mask,
            dropout_p=self.dropout if self.training else 0.0,
            is_causal=is_causal,
            enable_gqa=self.num_kv_groups > 1,
        )

This is only safe once enable_mem_efficient_sdp(False)/enable_math_sdp(False) are in place.**  
  why: The current .expand(...).reshape(...) materializes a real 5x copy of K and V (4 KV heads -> 20), because reshape cannot view a stride-0 expanded tensor. cuDNN handles GQA natively inside the kernel. Output is bit-identical (max abs diff 0.00e+00 vs the expand version).  
  gain: Same speed as expand+cuDNN (0.451 vs 0.444 ms — within noise) but removes the K/V materialization: saves ~2 x 16 x 1024 x 64 x 2 bytes x 26 layers ~ 170 MB at mb=2, plus 52 fewer kernel launches per forward. Small but free once cuDNN is on.  
  risk: HIGH IF APPLIED ALONE — this is a trap. On the default backend order enable_gqa=True disqualifies both flash and mem_efficient and drops to the math path: measured 4.560 ms, a 3.5x REGRESSION. It is only a win with cuDNN forced. Apply it in the same commit as the cuDNN switch, never before.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Do NOT change micro_batch_size, gradient_accumulation, num_workers, prefetch_factor, the RoPE implementation, or the loss.item() accumulation. Leave micro_batch_size: 2 / gradient_accumulation: 16. The only free cleanup worth doing is adding non_blocking=True to the H2D copies in src/train/train.py (lines 198, 203) since pin_memory=True is already set:

    x, y = x.to(device, non_blocking=True), y.to(device, non_blocking=True)**  
  why: All four of these are measured dead ends on this setup, and three of them are things a generic optimization guide would tell you to do. Raising micro_batch gains only 1.06x and regresses past mb=8. The dataloader already delivers 776x more than the GPU consumes. The complex RoPE is 5.7x FASTER than the real cos/sin version in eager and causes zero graph breaks. loss.item() costs 3%. Spending effort here is pure opportunity cost against the three changes that actually matter.  
  gain: ~0-1%. The value of this recommendation is negative work avoided, and specifically not destabilizing a run that is already 40,800 steps deep by changing the effective batch size.  
  risk: None. Note the inverse risk: changing micro_batch/grad_accum in a way that alters the effective batch (32 sequences / 32,768 tokens per step) would change the optimization trajectory mid-run. If you ever do change them, keep micro_batch x grad_accum = 32.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Apply in this order, measuring 200 steps between each, and restart from the latest checkpoint each time (currently step 40,800, ckpt prefix 1.1_v2_step):

1. cuDNN attention (config-only)            -> expect ~16,000 tok/s
2. + gradient_checkpointing: false          -> expect ~19,000 tok/s
3. + triton install & torch.compile         -> expect ~21,000-24,000 tok/s
4. + CCE loss (only if step 2 is memory-tight)

Add a throughput guard to the training loop so a regression is obvious:

if step % 500 == 0:
    print(f"peak_mem {torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated()/1e9:.2f} GB  "
          f"power {torch.cuda.power_draw()/1000:.0f} W")
    torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()

Watch power_draw, not nvidia-smi utilization. You are done optimizing when it sits above ~200 W.**  
  why: Each lever was measured independently under GPU contention; stacking them introduces interaction risk (notably enable_gqa x backend selection, and no-checkpointing x compile memory). Incremental application with a fixed 200-step measurement makes any regression attributable. Power draw is the only honest saturation metric on this workload — 100% 'utilization' at 70 W is what got you here.  
  gain: Combined 1.5-1.8x: 13,500 -> 20,000-24,000 tok/s. On the 279,200 remaining steps that is 7.82 days -> 4.3-5.6 days, saving 2.2-3.5 days of wall clock. Independently corroborated two ways: the interleaved stack measurement (1.64x at L=6, rising with depth) and the roofline (3.334 GFLOP/token at 55-65% of the 121 TFLOP/s measured sustained peak).  
  risk: The 1.8x end of the range assumes ~65-70% MFU, which is optimistic for a single consumer GPU on Windows/WDDM; 1.5-1.6x is the confident band. All timings were taken while sharing the GPU with your live training run, so absolute milliseconds are inflated — the RATIOS are trustworthy (interleaved, min-of-N) but the absolute extrapolations to L=26 are not. Re-measure once the GPU is free.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Plasma 1.1 (521M) at step 40,800/320,000: continue (A), restart (B), or continue-with-modified-recipe (C) — evidence-backed verdict and exact protocol
### Findings
- **This is NOT the standard continual-pretraining setting the re-warming papers study. At step 40,800 the LR is 1.1626e-4 = 96.9% of peak — the cosine has barely begun decaying. Every re-warming paper studies resuming from a fully-decayed eta_min checkpoint (a 10x jump, 3e-5 -> 3e-4). Raising to 3e-4 here is a 2.6x jump from a non-decayed point, which is strictly easier and lower-risk than anything in the literature.**  
  logs/pretrain_v2.jsonl step 40,750: {"lr": 0.00011627, "loss": 3.153, "grad_norm": 0.7985}. Cosine formula in src/train/scheduler.py with peak 1.2e-4, min 1.2e-5, warmup 3000, max 320000 gives 1.16255e-4 at step 40,800 = 96.9% of peak. 12.75% of steps done, 1.337B of 10.49B tokens.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\logs\pretrain_v2.jsonl ; C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\scheduler.py_
- **The LR of 1.2e-4 is objectively low for 521M params — it is exactly the value Ibrahim et al. used for their 10B model. For their 405M model (nearly Plasma's size) they used 3e-4 / min 3e-5, i.e. 2.5x higher.**  
  Table 14: '10B Model - Cosine Schedule: Max learning rate 1.2e-4, Min learning rate 1.2e-5, Warmup percent 1' and '405M Model - Cosine Schedule: Max learning rate 3e-4, Min learning rate 3e-5, Warmup percent 1'. Plasma 1.1 uses the 10B row verbatim for a 521M model.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.08763 (Ibrahim et al., TMLR 2024), Table 14_
- **But a controlled 4x LR sweep at 7B scale found the final loss and downstream scores are essentially IDENTICAL once you anneal properly. Higher LR helps early and is exactly compensated for by a better anneal; lower LR is worse early and is exactly compensated by a worse-needed anneal. This is the single strongest argument against restarting for LR reasons.**  
  OLMo 2 Table 8, peak LR 3e-4/6e-4/9e-4/12e-4 after 300B pretrain + 50B anneal: OLMES 62.5 / 63.9 / 64.1 / 63.6. With 100B anneal: 64.6 / 64.5 / 64.2. At 2T pretrain + 100B high-quality anneal: 3e-4 -> 73.8, 6e-4 -> 73.9. Text: 'a higher learning rate does make mid-training more effective, but it does so by exactly the amount that the pretraining is worse. All four variants show the same training loss at the end of the procedure.' Also: 'This finding contradicts machine learning folk wisdoms such as "higher learning rates are always better".'  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656 (OLMo 2), Section 4.1, Table 8_
- **Rewinding to an earlier checkpoint provably does not help. Continued pretraining does not induce loss of plasticity, so there is no 'the early LR is baked in' effect to escape by restarting.**  
  'we see in Fig. 7 that, in our setup, selecting earlier checkpoints for later fine-tuning does not lead to improvement in downstream performance. Therefore, selecting the latest checkpoint is the best option. We can conclude that the pre-training did not lead the model into a loss of plasticity that would make the model difficult to re-warm.' (Pythia-410M pretrained on 300B Pile, continued on 297B SlimPajama.)  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.04014 (Gupta et al., 'How to (re)warm your model?'), Takeaway 4 / Section 4_
- **The loss spike from re-warming is small and quantified — and the user's case is milder than any measured case. Same-data re-warm from 3e-5 to 3e-4 (10x) costs a peak +0.10 nats; to 6e-4 (20x) costs +0.20 nats. With a distribution shift it is +0.35 / +0.45.**  
  Section 7.1: 're-warming leads to a peak increase of the Pile validation loss of 0.1 relative to its initial value with a eta_max = 3e-4 as we continue pre-training on Pile ... contrasted with the Pile validation loss increase of 0.35 with the same learning rate schedule when continuing to pre-train on SlimPajama ... eta_max = 6e-4 curve when continuing to pre-train on Pile (with a peak loss increase of 0.2) vs SlimPajama (peak loss increase of 0.45).' Fig 8 also shows the constant-3e-4 baseline (no re-warm) has NO spike at all.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.08763, Section 7.1, Figure 8_
- **Warmup length is nearly irrelevant to adaptation but does suppress the transient spike. 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 2% all converge to the same loss within 50B tokens; the 0% run showed a chaotic transient spike.**  
  Section 6.1.1: 'models using shorter linear warmup initially forget and adapt faster ... the model without any warmup adapts and forgets the fastest - even undergoing an initial chaotic phase ... its large initial learning rate causes a transient spike in validation loss ... these initial differences diminish throughout training, leaving all models with relatively similar forgetting and adaptation after 50B tokens ... we set a linear warmup duration of 1% of training iterations for all subsequent experiments.'  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.08763, Section 6.1.1, Figure 3_
- **Truncating a cosine schedule mid-run and replacing the tail with a linear/1-sqrt decay to zero is established practice and costs little — OLMo 2 did exactly this at 80% of a planned run.**  
  'Previous experience with OLMo-0424 suggests that the last part of a cosine decay schedule can be cut off and replaced by a linear decay to zero with little loss of performance. Accordingly, for the 7B variant, we stop the schedule at 4T tokens and then switch to mid-training' — a cosine fitted to 5T tokens, cut at 4T.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656, Section 4.1_
- **Constant LR + cooldown (WSD) matches a well-tuned cosine at equal budget, is less LR-sensitive, and removes the need to commit to a token budget. Optimal cooldown is 10-20% of total steps; the (1-sqrt) shape beats linear at those ratios.**  
  'we observe an almost perfect match between the performance of the best cosine and cooldown schedule even for different training durations, all while exhibiting slightly less sensitivity to variations in the LR' ... 'benefits of extended cooldown periods plateau at around 20%' ... 'the cooldown surpasses cosine between 10-20% of steps' ... 'using only 5% decay with the (1-sqrt) cooldown can nearly match the performance of the cosine schedule on a 20B token run'. Also: 'The constant + cooldown schedule allows for continual learning by default. Here, the natural approach is to use checkpoints before the cooldown to continue training with a high LR; this avoids loss spikes and brittle training when rewarming.' Their reference LRs: 210M/360M models = 1e-3 cosine / 5e-4 constant; 1B model = 8e-4 at 1.8M batch.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.18392 (Hagele et al.), Sections 3.1-3.2, Figs 3-6, Tables 2-3_
- **A 10% decay phase is sufficient; 2.5% is not. Confirmed independently by MiniCPM and by the analytic S1/S2 scaling law, which predicts a parabola with an optimum at 10-20%.**  
  MiniCPM: 'among all three stable training checkpoints in 40N, 60N, and 80N training data, having a decay of 10% of the total tokens is sufficient to achieve the best results, while a decay of 2.5% of total tokens falls short.' Tissue et al.: 'a moderate annealing ratio, typically around 10% to 20%, is optimal, as it balances S1 and S2'. Caveat found: 1-sqrt beats cosine at 10% annealing but LOSES to cosine at 50% annealing.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06395 (MiniCPM) Section 4.3 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11029 Sections 4.5-4.6_
- **Quantifying the LR upgrade: using the fitted scaling law L(s) = L0 + A*S1^-alpha - C*S2 (S1 = area under the LR curve, alpha = 0.550, A = 0.429, C = 0.411), switching from the current cosine to constant-3e-4 + 15% (1-sqrt) decay raises S1 from 21.10 to 78.60 (3.72x) and predicts about -0.045 nats final loss, i.e. perplexity x0.956 (~4.4% better). Raising to 6e-4 only buys -0.057 nats. Changing min_lr from 1.2e-5 to 0 alone is worth about -0.001 nats.**  
  Fitted equation from the paper: 'L(s) = 2.628 + 0.429*S1^(-0.550) - 0.411*S2' at eta_max = 2e-4, eta_min = 0, lambda in [0.99, 0.999]. My computation over Plasma's actual schedule (scratchpad/calc.py): S1_banked = 4.669, S1_optionA = 21.102, S1 at eta_const 2e-4/3e-4/4.5e-4/6e-4 = 54.00/78.60/115.49/152.38; predicted delta vs A = -0.036/-0.045/-0.052/-0.057 nats. Note the re-warm itself makes S2 negative and eats most of the extra annealing area, which is why the gain saturates. This estimate is an extrapolation outside the fitted regime and OLMo 2's controlled experiment suggests the true value may be near 0 — so treat 0 to 0.05 nats as the honest range.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11029 (Tissue et al., 'Scaling Law with Learning Rate Annealing'), Eq. 1 + Section 3 ; computed in C:\Users\eb\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\c--Users-eb-Downloads-1386-ai\dc3b45aa-4183-49e6-8ee1-b61d44398b5c\scratchpad\calc.py_
- **The single highest-value missing ingredient is a mid-training / annealing phase on math + code — worth 10+ absolute points at small scale, i.e. 100-200x more than the LR fix. This is precisely the failure mode reported (0% executable code, 0% multi-step reasoning, 10-15% arithmetic).**  
  OLMo 2 1B, 50B Dolmino mid-training tokens on top of 4T pretrain (1.25% extra compute): Avg 31.9 -> 43.7 (+11.8), GSM8K 3.3 -> 43.8, MMLU 26.9 -> 44.3, ARC-C 26.1 -> 51.3, DROP 25.1 -> 34.0, AGIEval 24.5 -> 36.3. OLMo 2 7B: +10.6 avg, GSM8K 24.1 -> 67.5.  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656, Table 9_
- **The annealing effect is LARGER for small models, which is exactly Plasma's regime. Llama 3 measured +24.0% on GSM8k and +6.4% on MATH from annealing on math data at 8B, and explicitly negligible improvement at 405B.**  
  'We find that annealing improved the performance of a pre-trained Llama 3 8B model on the GSM8k and MATH validation sets by 24.0% and 6.4%, respectively. However, the improvements on the 405B model are negligible, suggesting that our flagship model has strong in-context learning and reasoning capabilities and does not require specific in-domain training samples.'  
  _src: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21783 (Llama 3 Herd of Models), Section 3.1.3_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Choose OPTION C. Resume from checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt with a modified recipe. Do NOT restart from step 0, and do NOT run the current cosine unchanged to 320,000.**  
  why: Every one of the four suspected 'mistakes' is either not a mistake or is fixable from the current checkpoint. (a) LR: OLMo 2's controlled 3e-4/6e-4/9e-4/12e-4 sweep found identical final loss and OLMES within 1.6 points once annealed, so restarting for LR buys ~0; Gupta et al. showed rewinding to earlier checkpoints never helps and there is no plasticity loss. (b) Batch 32k tokens is 45x below the critical batch size (B* = 93.20*N^0.47 -> 1,763 seqs for 521M) — that is the token-efficient side, and on one GPU a bigger batch has no wall-clock upside. (c) Short-context-first is the recommended protocol (Llama 3, Qwen 2.5, SmolLM2), not an error. (d) No math in the corpus is the ONLY genuine defect and is best fixed late anyway — SmolLM2 deliberately excluded math from stage 1, OLMo 2 reserved all math for mid-training, and Blakeney showed the upsampled mix must not be used for the whole run. Restarting would cost 21.7h and would forfeit 22.1% of the run's already-banked LR-area S1.  
  gain: Avoids ~21.7h of pure waste and, more importantly, redirects the decision toward the intervention that is actually worth 10+ points instead of the one worth <=0.05 nats.  
  risk: None relative to A or B. The main risk is over-editing: change the schedule and the data, not the architecture, tokenizer or seq_len-during-stable-phase.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add a mid-training + annealing stage — this is the change that matters. Keep max_steps = 320000. Switch data mixture at step 240,000 (75%) and begin the decay at step 272,000 (85%), giving a 48,000-step / 1.57B-token annealing window = 15% of the run. Mid-training mix (steps 240k-272k): ~15% FineMath-4+, ~20% code (existing code + stack shards upsampled), ~55% existing web/wiki mix as replay, ~10% arXiv/StackExchange. Anneal mix (steps 272k-320k): ~20% FineMath-4+, ~24% code, ~50% highest-scoring FineWeb-Edu + Wikipedia, ~6% arXiv/StackExchange.**  
  why: This is the single largest lever and the current plan has none of it. OLMo 2 1B gained +11.8 average points (GSM8K 3.3 -> 43.8, MMLU 26.9 -> 44.3, ARC-C 26.1 -> 51.3) from 50B mid-training tokens = 1.25% extra compute. Llama 3 8B gained +24.0% GSM8k and +6.4% MATH from annealing on math, explicitly larger at small scale. Blakeney's 7B/1T replication: +6.90pp MMLU, +8.26pp GSM8K, +6.17pp HumanEval, with 10-20% of training being the optimal window (MMLU peaks at 10%, aggregate at 20%, GSM8K monotonic). 15% is the balanced choice. Token budgets check out: 15% math over both phases is ~0.47B tokens against ~0.85-0.9B available in data/raw_1.1/finemath_clean.txt, i.e. well under 1 epoch and far from the ~10-epoch plateau.  
  gain: By far the largest expected effect. Directly targets the reported 0% executable code, 0% multi-step reasoning and 10-15% arithmetic. Scaled-down analogues suggest +5 to +12 points on the overall benchmark; the arithmetic/GSM-style subscore is where the biggest jump should appear.  
  risk: Low. Mid-run mixture changes are standard (Llama 3 405B, SmolLM2 at 6T/8T/10T). Keep >=50% of the original mix as replay — Ibrahim et al. show 1-5% replay already kills forgetting while 50% replay starts hurting adaptation, so 50-55% is the conservative end. Watch for a transient loss bump at the switch (SmolLM2 saw a spike at stage 3 that recovered by end of stage). Decontaminate FineMath against your eval set with 13-gram matching before use.

- [high] **Convert the schedule from cosine to WSD (the WSDScheduler already in src/train/scheduler.py). Concretely: re-warm linearly from 1.1626e-4 to 3.0e-4 over steps 40,800 -> 43,300 (2,500 steps = 0.78% of the run); hold constant 3.0e-4 from 43,300 -> 272,000; then (1-sqrt) decay 3.0e-4 -> 0 over steps 272,000 -> 320,000 (48,000 steps = 15%). Set min_lr: 0.0.**  
  why: 3.0e-4 is exactly what Ibrahim et al. used for their 405M model (they used Plasma's current 1.2e-4/1.2e-5 for their 10B). It sits inside the 2.4e-4 to 6.9e-4 band implied by DeepSeek's validated law (eta_opt = 1.13e-3 at B_opt = 710k tokens) corrected to a 32.8k batch, and it is safely below the 6e-4 where OLMo 2 still saw no benefit and well below the 30e-4 that diverged. Hagele et al. show constant+cooldown matches the best cosine while being less LR-sensitive and removing the fixed token budget; MiniCPM shows 10% decay suffices and 2.5% does not; Tissue et al. put the optimum at 10-20% and confirm 1-sqrt beats linear/cosine at those ratios (but loses at 50%, so do not exceed ~20%). Re-warming 2.6x from a NON-decayed checkpoint is far milder than the 10x-from-eta_min case where Ibrahim measured only +0.10 nats on same-distribution data.  
  gain: 0 to -0.05 nats final loss (predicted -0.045 nats, ppl x0.956, by the fitted S1/S2 law; OLMo 2's controlled experiment suggests it could be ~0). The larger, more certain gain is structural: you can start the cooldown from any point, so the run stops being hostage to the 320,000-step commitment.  
  risk: Medium-low. Guardrails: keep checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt untouched as a rollback; log grad_norm every 50 steps (currently 0.76-0.80 against clip 1.0); abort and roll back if grad_norm exceeds ~2.0 for more than ~200 consecutive steps or if train loss stays >0.15 above the pre-rewarm value for >3,000 steps. Expect a small transient bump of order 0.02-0.05 nats over the first few thousand steps. Do NOT go to 6e-4 or 1e-3 — the marginal predicted gain over 3e-4 is only -0.012 nats and the instability risk rises sharply (OLMo 2 abandoned 30e-4 for unrecoverable warmup spikes).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Change Adam betas from (0.9, 0.95) to (0.9, 0.9977) in configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml at the same time as the re-warm.**  
  why: At 32,768 tokens/optimizer step, beta2 = 0.95 gives a second-moment half-life of only 0.44M tokens — about 23x shorter than the ~10M-token half-life Marek et al. find optimal, and their evidence is that small batches with an un-adjusted beta2 is exactly the configuration that makes small-batch training look bad. beta2 = 0.9977 restores t2 = 9.86M tokens. Rule: beta2_new = beta2_old^(B_new/B_old). This is free — no compute cost, no schedule change.  
  gain: Small but essentially free; in their reproduction of Xiao's setup, fixing the half-life removed the entire apparent small-batch penalty (their Figure 5, beta2=0.95 vs t2=10M panels).  
  risk: Low. Increasing beta2 mid-run only slows the decay of an already-populated second-moment buffer, so the transition is smooth. If you are cautious, ramp 0.95 -> 0.9977 linearly over the same 2,500 re-warm steps. Do not also change beta1 — 0.9 is confirmed good across all batch sizes.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Before committing, run a micro-anneal validation: from checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt, run 2 x 4,000 steps (0.13B tokens each, ~2h each at 17.1k tok/s) with LR linearly decayed to 0 — arm A on the current mix, arm B on 30% FineMath-4+ / 70% current mix — and benchmark both with scripts/benchmark_v2.py.**  
  why: This is the exact protocol Llama 3 used ('annealing the learning rate of a 50% trained Llama 3 8B model linearly to 0 on 40B tokens ... 30% weight to the new dataset and the remaining 70% weight to the default data mix'), SmolLM2 used (60/40 for math), and OLMo 2 calls 'microannealing'. It converts a 6-day bet into a 4-hour measurement, and it also gives you an honest early read on whether the whole run is on track to beat the 43% baseline.  
  gain: Removes the main remaining uncertainty (does FineMath actually help THIS model at THIS scale) for ~4h of the ~150h remaining. Also produces a real intermediate benchmark number, which you currently do not have between step 40k and the end.  
  risk: Very low; it is a side branch that does not touch the main run. Caveat: absolute scores from a 4,000-step anneal at 1.34B tokens will be far below the final model's — use it for the A-vs-B delta only, never as a level prediction.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Fix the in-flight LR ablation: rerun scripts/ablate.py with --resume checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt and at least 6,000-8,000 steps per variant, and narrow the grid to [1.2e-4, 3e-4, 4.5e-4]. Drop the 1e-3 arm.**  
  why: scripts/ablate.py currently starts every variant from random init for 2,000 steps (logs/ablate_lr.log confirms 4 variants x 2000 steps, no --resume). Short from-scratch sweeps systematically favour the largest LR — OLMo 2 measured the 3e-4 vs 6e-4 crossover at 'well past 200B tokens' and warns explicitly that 'a shorter hyperparameter experiment might come to the wrong conclusion'. The decision-relevant question is not 'which LR is best from scratch' but 'which re-warm target is best from step 40,000', which only a resumed ablation can answer.  
  gain: Prevents choosing 6e-4 or 1e-3 on the strength of a 2,000-step artefact — the failure mode most likely to destabilise the run.  
  risk: Costs ~4x more ablation compute (3 variants x 8,000 steps = 0.79B tokens = ~13h at 17.1k tok/s). If that is too expensive, skip the ablation entirely and take 3e-4 on the size-matched Ibrahim 405M precedent — the evidence says the choice barely matters within 2.4e-4 to 6e-4.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Apply the throughput fix now, before resuming: set micro_batch_size: 4, gradient_accumulation: 8 (global batch unchanged at 32 seqs / 32,768 tokens), keep gradient_checkpointing: true, and enable torch.compile.**  
  why: Your own benchmark already proves it: logs/bench_final.json BEST = {micro_batch 4, accum 8, grad_ckpt true, compile true, tok_s 17120, peak_gb 11.91}, a measured 1.41x over the current mb2 configuration, with 5.2 GB of VRAM headroom. Keeping the global batch identical means the LR/beta2 reasoning above is unaffected.  
  gain: 148.4h remaining instead of 188.2h — saves ~40 hours (1.65 days), which is roughly the entire cost of adding a 15% annealing phase.  
  risk: Low, but torch.compile adds a one-time warmup of a few minutes and can be brittle across resume boundaries. Verify the first 200 steps produce the same loss trajectory as the pre-switch run before leaving it unattended. Do not chase the '2-3x headroom' figure — at ~8ND with gradient checkpointing you are already near 25% MFU and 1.41x is the real ceiling here.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Anneal 2-3 times from the step-272,000 checkpoint with different data-order seeds and average the resulting weights (uniform weight average, not EMA). If that is too expensive, instead uniformly average the last 4-5 checkpoints of a single anneal.**  
  why: OLMo 2: 'averaging weights of different checkpoints trained on same mixture but different data order seeds consistently improves over individual checkpoints' (they used 3 x 50B for the 7B). Llama 3 applies Polyak averaging over checkpoints during annealing. Hagele et al.: 'Irrespective of the schedule and without additional overhead, SWA improves' (and found EMA worse than SWA). Because you keep the pre-cooldown checkpoint under WSD, extra anneal runs are cheap re-rolls of only the last 15%.  
  gain: Consistent small gain (typically a few tenths of a point on aggregate benchmarks) with zero risk of regression, since you can always keep the best single checkpoint.  
  risk: Each extra anneal costs 48,000 steps = 25.5h at 17.1k tok/s. The single-run last-k-checkpoint average is nearly free and captures most of the benefit — start there. Averaging is only valid across checkpoints on the same trajectory or from the same starting point; never average across the re-warm boundary.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **OPTIONAL and lowest priority: extend context 1024 -> 2048 in a dedicated 10,000-step window at steps 262,000-272,000 (0.33B tokens = 3.1% of the run), at constant 3.0e-4, immediately BEFORE the anneal. Set rope_theta: 50000.0, seq_len: 2048, micro_batch_size: 2, gradient_accumulation: 8 (keeps 32,768 tokens/step and roughly the same VRAM), and pack the data as 40% documents >=2048 tokens / 60% the phase-3 mixture.**  
  why: Order and placement follow Llama 3 (long-context stage, then anneal) and SmolLM2 (extend from an intermediate stage-4 checkpoint before the final 75B tokens, 40% long / 60% short, RoPE 10,000 -> 130,000); Qwen 2.5 uses the same 40/60 split and raises RoPE 10,000 -> 1,000,000 for a 8x extension. Do it at high LR, not during the anneal, so the model has the plasticity to adapt. rope_theta MUST change: the numerically-solved lower bound is 1.6e4 for 2k context, so the current 10,000 is below the floor; 50,000 clears 2k comfortably and also covers 4k (bound 2.7e4) if you later want it. Budget precedent: SmolLM2 spent 0.68% of tokens on a 4x extension, so 3.1% for a 2x extension is generous.  
  gain: Doubles usable context (better multi-turn chat and few-shot prompting headroom). It will NOT move factual QA, short writing, arithmetic or basic Python — do this only if you value the context window for its own sake.  
  risk: Medium, and it is the one recommendation I would drop under time pressure. Changing rope_theta perturbs position encoding the model has seen 1.3B+ tokens of; expect a visible loss bump for a few thousand steps. Attention FLOPs/token roughly double (12*n_layer*d*seq rises from 12.9% to 22.9% of total), so throughput drops ~8-10%. Verify VRAM stays under ~13GB before committing. If you skip it, keep seq_len 1024 and rope_theta 10000 — that pairing is correct and safe (bound for 1k is 4.3e3).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Do NOT remove gradient accumulation, and do NOT raise the global batch size, despite the 'batch too small' concern.**  
  why: The 32,768-token batch is ~45x below the critical batch size for a 521M model (B* = 93.20*N^0.47 -> 1,763 sequences; their measured log2(B*) = 10.62 at 604M). Below CBS you are on the token-efficient side of the curve — larger batches only buy wall-clock via data parallelism you do not have. Marek et al. go further and recommend against gradient accumulation on a single device, which would suggest dropping accum entirely (batch = 4 seqs = 4,096 tokens), but that is a regime change requiring a full LR + beta2 retune and would add 8x more optimizer steps; it is not worth attempting at step 40,800.  
  gain: Avoids a destabilising change that has no expected quality benefit.  
  risk: None from inaction. The only cost is leaving a possible small per-FLOP gain on the table — which is the right trade 87% of the way from the start of a run you do not want to destabilise. Revisit the accum=1 + beta2-rescaled configuration for Plasma 1.2 from scratch.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Keep max_steps at 320,000 and treat 320,000 as a floor, not a ceiling. Under WSD you now have an explicit option: at step 272,000, evaluate; if the model is still improving strongly, push the constant phase further and shift the cooldown window rather than stopping.**  
  why: This is the central practical advantage of the change. 'The main advantage of the constant schedule is that it does not require one to specify the number of training steps in advance ... the cooldown can be initiated at any time to observe model behavior and decide whether to stop' (Hagele et al.). MiniCPM demonstrated a 0.036B model continuously trained far past Chinchilla still had headroom, and matched a 0.17B model for ~4x compute. Plasma's corpus is 7.52B unique tokens for a 521M model = 14.4 tokens/param, slightly UNDER Chinchilla's 20, so the 10.5B-token plan is already at 1.4 epochs; the binding constraint is unique data, not schedule length.  
  gain: Optionality worth more than any single hyperparameter here. It also means a bad outcome at step 272,000 costs you a 15% re-anneal, not a 7-day restart.  
  risk: Repetition: pushing much past 10.5B tokens on a 7.52B-token corpus increases epochs beyond ~1.5. SmolLM2 targets the 4-5 epoch threshold from Muennighoff et al., so you have room, but adding FineMath (+~0.88B unique) and any further FineWeb-Edu you can pull is the better way to extend.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Set min_lr from 1.2e-5 to 0.0 and use the (1-sqrt) cooldown shape f(n) = 1 - sqrt((n - (N - N_decay)) / N_decay), not linear and not cosine, for the 15% window.**  
  why: Decaying fully to zero maximises the annealing area S2: 'setting the minimum LR to 0 maximizes the annealing amount, thereby [minimising loss]'. Hagele et al. find (1-sqrt) 'consistently outperforms the standard linear decay' and beats cosine for sufficiently long cooldowns; Tissue et al. refine this — 1-sqrt wins at 10% annealing but LOSES to cosine at 50%, so it is the right choice specifically at the 10-20% ratio being recommended. src/train/scheduler.py already implements shape='1-sqrt'.  
  gain: Small on its own (my S1/S2 estimate puts min_lr -> 0 at about -0.001 nats because the lost forward area nearly cancels the gained annealing area), but it is free, and the shape choice is the difference between matching and beating cosine at 15%.  
  risk: Essentially none. Just confirm WSDScheduler is instantiated with decay_steps=48000 and min_lr=0.0, and that the resume path reconstructs the schedule from the absolute step number rather than a relative counter.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 

## Small-model (~300M–1B) from-scratch training failure modes, diagnosed against the live Plasma 1.1 run (521M, RTX 5080 16GB, step 40,750/320,000)
### Findings
- **ROOT CAUSE OF THE LOSS OSCILLATION: the pretrain shards are domain-stratified by shard index, and the dataloader drains one entire shard per worker before switching. Code content falls from 8–24% in shards 0–48 to 0–2% in shards 56–72.**  
  Measured directly by decoding 300 random packed sequences per shard from data/shards_1.1_v2 (73 shards x 99,462 seqs). Code fraction by shard: s0=8%, s8=14%, s16=14%, s24=12%, s32=11%, s40=15%, s48=24%, s56=1%, s62=1%, s66=2%, s70=0%, s72=1%. Mechanism is in scripts/build_repretrain_shards.py: choose_source() greedily picks the max-deficit source, sources exhaust at different points (meta.yaml achieved shares vs targets: fineweb 59.7% vs 42%, wikipedia 12.4% vs 20%, code 12.9% vs 15%, stackexchange 10.3% vs 13%, arxiv 4.7% vs 10% — every source except FineWeb-Edu ran dry), and flush_sequences() only permutes within a 100,000-sequence chunk, so shards are written in stream order and the tail of the corpus is nearly pure web.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\scripts\build_repretrain_shards.py (lines 80–97, 100–121, 216–250); data/shards_1.1_v2/meta.yaml; direct measurement_
- **The oscillation period is set by the dataloader, not the data: StreamingShardDataset yields every sequence of a shard before moving on, so with num_workers=2 / micro_batch=2 / grad_accum=16 each worker supplies 16 sequences per optimizer step and a shard lasts 99,462/16 = 6,216 optimizer steps = 4.18 hours at 2.42 s/step. Both workers flip shards at ~the same time, so the mixture changes as a step function.**  
  src/data/dataset.py lines 139–164: `while True: rng.shuffle(order); for si in order: ... for idx in indices: yield`. Shard order is shuffled but consumption is sequential-within-shard. Measured train loss confirms the magnitude: after removing a smooth log/log^2 trend over steps 8,000–40,800, residual std = 0.1728 nats with peak-to-peak 0.5859 nats — matching the reported 2.75<->3.20 (0.45 nat) cycling. Autocorrelation shows a peak at lag 18,750 steps (~3x the predicted 6,216); the 32,800-step record is only ~5 shard periods long so the fundamental cannot be isolated cleanly, but the code-fraction swing of 0–24% times a ~2.1 nat web-vs-code entropy gap predicts ~0.50 nats, very close to the observed 0.586.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\data\dataset.py lines 120–164; logs/pretrain_v2.jsonl (816 points)_
- **The fix already exists in the repo but was never applied to the corpus being trained on.**  
  scripts/shuffle_shards.py implements a correct two-pass external shuffle (96 random buckets, scatter then in-RAM permute then re-emit 100k-seq shards) and its own docstring names this exact failure: 'when sources exhaust at different points every shard ends up with a different domain mix (e.g. the tail of the corpus is nearly pure web).' data/shards_1.1_v2/meta.yaml contains no `globally_shuffled` key, so the running corpus is the unshuffled one.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\scripts\shuffle_shards.py; data/shards_1.1_v2/meta.yaml_
- **VALIDATION NOISE FLOOR, measured empirically: 100 sequences gives +/-10.8% on perplexity and is unusable; the run's current 2048 gives +/-2.3%. Intra-sequence token correlation means a 1024-token sequence is worth only ~34 independent tokens.**  
  Bootstrap over 6,144 held-out sequences scored with checkpoint 1.1_v2_step_40000. Per-sequence NLL: mean 3.0573, std 0.5184, p1 1.31, p99 4.10. Standard error of the mean by val size: n=100 -> 0.0521 nats (95% CI width 0.204, ppl +/-10.75%); n=256 -> 0.0325; n=512 -> 0.0231; n=1024 -> 0.0161; n=2048 -> 0.0115 (ppl +/-2.28%); n=4096 -> 0.0080 (+/-1.58%); n=8192 -> 0.0058 (+/-1.14%). Naive iid SE assuming independent tokens at n=2048 would be 0.00211 — the true SE is 5.5x larger (30x in variance), giving ~34 effective independent tokens per 1024-token sequence.  
  _src: Measured on data/shards_1.1_v2/val_0000.bin (73,349 sequences available) with checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt_
- **The step 35,000 -> 40,000 validation increase (2.9067 -> 2.9844, ppl 18.30 -> 19.77) is a REAL regression at +4.7 sigma, not measurement noise. It would have been invisible under the old 100-sequence protocol.**  
  Using the measured SE of 0.0115 nats at n=2048, the SE of a difference between two independent evals is 0.0165 nats. Observed delta +0.0777 nats = +4.7 sigma. For contrast, the 30k->35k improvement (-0.0374) is only -2.3 sigma, i.e. marginal. Under a 100-sequence protocol the difference SE would be 0.074 nats and the real +0.078 regression would read as ~1 sigma — indistinguishable from noise.  
  _src: logs/pretrain_v2.jsonl val entries + measured bootstrap SE_
- **TIED-EMBEDDING PATHOLOGY, measured: embedding norm is monotonically inversely proportional to token frequency, with a 1.9x spread. Never-trained rows have the LARGEST norms and are nearly identical to each other.**  
  checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt, per-token embedding L2 norm bucketed by corpus frequency (counted over 677M tokens from 7 shards): never seen (123 tokens) mean 1.6097 std 0.0023; 1–100 occurrences (830) 1.5336; 100–1k (15,403) 1.2519; 1k–10k (25,090) 1.0012; 10k–100k (5,898) 0.8856; 100k+ (656) 0.8496. Global mean 1.0761, std 0.2027. The mechanism is the tied output head: every token is a negative class at every position, so unused rows are pushed along -x until their logit is maximally suppressed, and train.py puts tok_emb/output in the no_decay group so nothing shrinks them. The tiny std (0.0023) on never-seen rows shows they have all converged to the same suppressed configuration.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py lines 98–111; direct checkpoint analysis_
- **The final RMSNorm gain has grown to 2.58x its initialization to compensate for the small tied-embedding scale — the network is learning its softmax temperature through a norm gain, which is slow and is exactly what z-loss/logit-scaling exists to avoid.**  
  checkpoints/1.1_v2_step_40000.pt: final norm.weight mean 2.5802 (min 2.0387, max 3.8530) vs init 1.0. Embedding entry RMS 0.03061 vs init 0.02. Effective logit std = 0.03061 * sqrt(1280) * 2.58 = 2.83. Per-layer weight stds are healthy and flat (q_proj 0.0187–0.0225, down_proj 0.0208–0.0236 across layers 0–25), so this is specific to the output path, not general blow-up. Per-layer RMSNorm gains drift with depth (ffn_norm: 0.836 at layer 0 -> 1.171 at layer 20), consistent with depth_scaled_init being OFF.  
  _src: Direct checkpoint analysis; configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml (no depth_scaled_init key, so src/train/train.py line 87 defaults it to False)_
- **TOKENIZER: 123 vocabulary entries are never used, ~889 tokens are seen fewer than 1000 times in the whole 7.52B-token corpus, and the rarest live tokens are orphaned BPE prefixes that are pure dead weight.**  
  Token frequency over 677.14M tokens (7 shards): 123 never used (0.26% of 48,000), 230 seen <10x, 953 seen <100x (1.99%); projected to the full 7.52B corpus, 889 tokens (1.85%) are seen <1000 times. The 123 never-used are 115 byte-fallback pieces plus <unk>, <s>, <pad>. Rarest non-zero pieces are all truncated word-prefixes orphaned by longer merges: '_earthqu', '_passeng', '_supermark', '_councill', '_portfol', '_maneu', '_embra', '_suspic', '_noteb', '_Azerbai', '_Workshe', '_upris', '_errone'. Separately, byte-fallback pieces fire on 2.92% of all tokens (19,795,762 of 677M) — roughly 220M of the 7.52B corpus is being encoded one byte at a time. BOS '<s>' (id 1) never appears because build_repretrain_shards.py only appends eos_id.

- **SILENT BUG, high severity: every --resume replays the data stream from the very beginning. Checkpoints carry no dataloader or RNG state, and the streaming dataset's seed is hard-coded.**  
  src/train/utils.py save_checkpoint() persists only {step, model_state_dict, optimizer_state_dict, config}. src/data/dataset.py StreamingShardDataset defaults seed=42 and src/train/train.py lines 163–168 never pass a seed, so `rng = default_rng(42 + worker_id*1000003)` reproduces the identical shard order and identical within-shard permutation on every restart. The run is currently paused at step 40,750 and will resume from checkpoint 40,000, so the first ~1.28M sequences get a second pass while the tail gets none. A 9-day Windows run that restarts every ~40k steps would never see beyond the first ~18% of the corpus.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\utils.py lines 18–34; src/data/dataset.py lines 86–136; src/train/train.py lines 140–143, 161–168_
- **SILENT BUG: the SFT masked-loss + gradient-accumulation normalization weights tokens unequally. Assistant tokens are effectively weighted between 0.62x and 2.62x (p5–p95) instead of uniformly, systematically upweighting short assistant turns.**  
  src/train/train.py lines 253–258 compute `masked_loss = (loss_per_token*mask).sum() / (mask.sum()+1e-8)` per micro-batch, then `loss = masked_loss/grad_accum`. The correct full-batch quantity is sum(nll) over ALL micro-batches divided by sum(mask) over all micro-batches, which makes micro-batch i's implicit token weight mean(c)/c_i rather than 1. Simulated 3,000 optimizer steps against the real masks in data/sft_shards_1.1_v2 (100,000 sequences, mean 684.0 assistant tokens/seq, std 211.9, p1=94, p99=982): heaviest micro-batch weight per step averages 1.77x (p95 2.62x, max 14.0x), lightest averages 0.72x (p5 0.62x, min 0.10x). Also 0.26% of SFT sequences contain zero assistant tokens (~438 of 168,539) and contribute nothing; they avoid NaN only because the numerator is also zero.  
  _src: C:\Users\eb\Downloads\1386.ai\src\train\train.py lines 250–265; simulation over data/sft_shards_1.1_v2 mask shards; https://unsloth.ai/blog/gradient_
- **The corpus contains the exact loss-spike trigger OLMo 2 filters for: 0.63% of packed sequences contain a run of 32 or more consecutive repeated n-grams. At batch 32 that is ~18% of optimizer steps.**  
  Scanned 6,000 random packed sequences across shards 0/20/40/60/72 for the longest run of consecutive identical n-grams (n in 1,2,3,5,8,13): >=8 repeats 1.80%, >=16 0.97%, >=32 0.63%, >=64 0.38%. Worst observed run was 288 consecutive repeats. Projected over 7,260,697 sequences that is ~45,984 sequences with >=32 repeats and ~27,832 with >=64. Probability that a 32-sequence optimizer batch contains at least one = 1-(1-0.0063)^32 = 18.3%. OLMo 2 explicitly filters documents containing 'a sequence of 32 or more repeated n-grams, where an n-gram is any span of 1 to 13 tokens' after finding repeated n-grams coincide with gradient spikes.  
  _src: Direct scan of data/shards_1.1_v2; https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.00656_
- **Current optimization is NOT unstable — gradient norms are healthy and there is no divergence risk at LR 1.2e-4. The stability gap is in missing instrumentation and missing safeguards, not in a live problem.**  
  logs/pretrain_v2.jsonl steps 8,000–40,800: grad_norm mean 0.679, p50 0.675, p99 0.823, max 2.498; only 0.2% of logged steps exceeded the clip threshold of 1.0. No spikes, no NaNs. However grad_norm is only recorded every 50 steps (log_interval), so 98% of steps are unobserved; there is no spike detector, no skip-batch logic, no z-loss, no qk-norm, and AdamW eps is left at the PyTorch default 1e-8.  
  _src: logs/pretrain_v2.jsonl; src/train/train.py lines 121–126 (no eps argument), 267–298_

### Recommendations
- [high] **Before resuming, run the existing global shuffle: `python scripts/shuffle_shards.py --in-dir data/shards_1.1_v2 --out-dir data/shards_1.1_v2_shuf --buckets 96 --seqs-per-shard 100000`, then point configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml at `data/shards_1.1_v2_shuf`. Verify afterwards that meta.yaml contains `globally_shuffled: true` and re-run the per-shard domain probe to confirm code fraction is ~13% in every shard rather than 24% -> 0%.**  
  why: This is the direct fix for the measured domain stratification (code 8–24% in shards 0–48 vs 0–2% in shards 56–72) that produces the 0.586-nat peak-to-peak loss oscillation. The script already exists and implements the standard two-pass external shuffle; it was simply never applied to the corpus in use. Because --resume restarts the data stream from scratch anyway, re-shuffling costs nothing in lost progress.  
  gain: Eliminates the 0.45–0.59 nat train-loss cycling entirely; makes every 6,216-step window i.i.d. w.r.t. domain; removes the 4.2-hour blocks during which the model sees zero code. Should also remove most of the spurious val-loss wobble (the +4.7 sigma regression at 40k). Disk: one extra ~15 GB copy, ~30–60 min of I/O.  
  risk: Needs ~15 GB free disk for the output plus ~15 GB transient for buckets. Peak RAM is one bucket (~7.26M/96 * 1025 * 2 bytes ~ 155 MB) — trivial. No VRAM impact.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Make the data stream resumable. Add to save_checkpoint(): `"data_seed": data_seed, "samples_consumed": step * batch_size`. In train.py, pass `seed=cfg.get('data_seed', 42) + start_step` into StreamingShardDataset, and skip `start_step * 32` sequences on resume (or simply derive the seed from start_step so each resume walks a different order).**  
  why: src/train/utils.py stores no dataloader state and StreamingShardDataset's seed is hard-coded to 42, so every restart replays the identical shard order from position zero. The run is paused at 40,750 and about to resume from 40,000; on a 9-day Windows run with several restarts this silently confines training to the first fraction of the corpus while reporting normal-looking losses.  
  gain: Guarantees the full 7.52B-token corpus is actually seen. In the worst realistic case (restart every ~40k steps) this is the difference between training on ~18% of the corpus and 100% of it — easily worth several points of benchmark score.  
  risk: Seed-derivation is a 2-line change and safe. Exact sequence-skipping is more faithful but costs a few minutes of I/O on resume; the seed-perturbation variant is nearly as good and free.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Fix the SFT masked-loss normalization. Accumulate unnormalized sums across the accumulation window: in the micro-step loop compute `tok_sum += (loss_per_token*mask_flat).sum()` and `tok_cnt += mask_flat.sum()`, call `.backward()` on `(loss_per_token*mask_flat).sum() / expected_tokens_per_step` where `expected_tokens_per_step` is a running estimate (or do two passes to get the exact denominator), instead of dividing each micro-batch by its own mask.sum() and then by grad_accum.**  
  why: Measured on the real masks in data/sft_shards_1.1_v2 (mean 684 assistant tokens/seq, std 212), the current scheme weights individual assistant tokens between 0.62x and 2.62x (p5–p95, worst 0.10x–14.0x) within a single optimizer step, systematically favouring short assistant turns. This is the documented gradient-accumulation loss bug.  
  gain: Restores uniform per-token weighting in SFT. Unsloth measured L2 weight-error reduction of over an order of magnitude from the equivalent fix at 16 accumulation steps — which is exactly Plasma's grad_accum. Most visible on instruction-following and long-form answer quality.  
  risk: Only affects the finetune stage (use_loss_mask: true). A running-estimate denominator introduces a small transient at step 1; using a fixed constant equal to micro_batch*grad_accum*mean_assistant_tokens (= 2*16*684 = 21,888) is simpler and stable. Recalibrate LR if the loss scale shifts noticeably.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Raise validation to 4096 sequences and keep the fixed seeded subset: set `val_sequences: 4096` and `val_batch_size: 8` in configs/pretrain_1.1_v2.yaml. Additionally log per-domain validation loss, and change `eval_interval` from 5000 to 5000 only after the shuffle lands (before then use 6217, coprime to the 6,216-step shard period).**  
  why: Measured SE is 0.0115 nats at n=2048 and 0.0080 at n=4096 (perplexity +/-2.28% vs +/-1.58%). Late in training, 5,000-step improvements shrink to ~0.01–0.02 nats, which n=2048 cannot resolve at 2 sigma. The current 5000-step eval cadence also aliases against the 6,216-step shard period, so each eval samples a different phase of the domain cycle — which is a plausible contributor to the +4.7 sigma bump at step 40k.  
  gain: Halves the noise floor on the primary training signal, making genuine 0.02-nat improvements detectable and preventing both false alarms and missed regressions. Cost: 4.19M tokens forward-only per eval, ~105 s at ~40k tok/s = ~0.9% wall-clock overhead at eval_interval 5000.  
  risk: None beyond the ~1% overhead. Fits easily in 16 GB at val_batch_size 8 (the earlier OOM in my own probe was contention with the concurrently running LR-sweep ablation, not a memory-model problem).  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add z-loss with coefficient 1e-4 and a spike guard. In the loss: `lse = torch.logsumexp(logits.float(), dim=-1); loss = ce + 1e-4 * (lse**2).mean()`. Separately, track grad_norm every step (not every 50) into a running median, and skip the optimizer step when `grad_norm > 5 * running_median` (zero the grads and continue).**  
  why: Wortsman et al. use z-loss at exactly 1e-4 to prevent output-logit divergence, and OLMo 2 uses 1e-4*log^2(Z) for the same reason. Plasma has no z-loss, and its final RMSNorm gain has already drifted to 2.58x init to manage logit scale. Grad norms are currently healthy (p99 0.823, max 2.498, 0.2% clipped) but only 2% of steps are observed, and 18.3% of optimizer batches contain a >=32-repeat n-gram sequence — the OLMo-2 spike trigger.  
  gain: Removes the logit-drift failure mode for the remaining 280,000 steps and caps the damage from any single pathological batch. z-loss typically also gives a small perplexity improvement by keeping logits well-scaled. Memory cost is one [B,S] logsumexp tensor — negligible, no VRAM concern on 16 GB.  
  risk: Adding z-loss at step 40k slightly changes the objective; the loss value will shift a little and is not directly comparable to earlier logs. Coefficient 1e-4 is small enough that this is cosmetic. Skip-batch logic must zero grads correctly or it will silently double-count.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Set AdamW epsilon explicitly to 1e-12: `torch.optim.AdamW(param_groups, lr=..., betas=..., eps=1e-12, fused=True)` in src/train/train.py line 121.**  
  why: eps is currently unset, so PyTorch's 1e-8 default applies. Wortsman et al. found 1e-8 measurably harmful at scale, that decreasing to 1e-15 improves loss, and that increasing to 1e-6 induces instability. 1e-12 is a conservative point in the beneficial direction that stays clear of bf16/fp32 denormal territory.  
  gain: Small but free perplexity improvement and reduced LR sensitivity, particularly for the rarely-updated embedding rows where the second-moment estimate is tiny and eps dominates the denominator.  
  risk: Very low. Changing eps mid-run alters effective step sizes for parameters with very small second moments (exactly the rare embeddings); expect a brief, small loss transient over a few hundred steps.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Filter the corpus for repeated n-grams before any future rebuild: drop documents containing a run of >=32 consecutive repeated n-grams for n in 1..13, matching OLMo 2's rule. Apply it in scripts/build_repretrain_shards.py inside source_iter().**  
  why: Measured 0.63% of packed sequences (~45,984 of 7.26M) already contain >=32-repeat runs, worst case 288 consecutive repeats, giving an 18.3% chance per optimizer step. OLMo 2 identified these as coinciding with gradient spikes. These sequences also teach the model to repeat, which is directly counterproductive given the degeneration concern.  
  gain: Removes the dominant data-side spike trigger and ~0.6% of actively harmful training signal. Also expected to reduce generation-time repetition independent of decoding settings.  
  risk: Costs a corpus rebuild, so this is for the next rebuild rather than the current resume. The 1..13 n-gram scan is O(L*13) per document — budget extra build time. Do not apply it as a mid-run dataloader filter; that would reintroduce non-stationarity.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **For code and math evaluation, set repetition_penalty=1.0 (not the 1.2 default) and prefer greedy or temperature<=0.2. Change the default in src/inference/generate.py or override it in scripts/benchmark_v2.py per task category.**  
  why: apply_repetition_penalty divides the logit of every token seen in the last 256 tokens by 1.2. Python requires repeated indentation, 'return', '=', ':' and repeated identifiers within any 256-token window, so the penalty fights correct syntax. The decoding implementation itself is verified correct (nucleus filtering, RoPE positions, KV cache), so this is an independent, purely configurational handicap on the benchmark that currently reads ~0% on executable code.  
  gain: Directly removes a known handicap on the executable-code and arithmetic categories at zero training cost. Likely worth several points on those categories once the base model is competent.  
  risk: None for code. For open-ended prose, dropping the penalty to 1.0 may increase visible repetition while the model is still undertrained — so make it per-category rather than global.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Extend the token budget rather than the parameter count. Keep 521M, and push toward 25–30B tokens seen (roughly 3–4 epochs of the current 7.52B corpus, or fewer epochs once the in-flight cosmopedia/stack_edu downloads land). Adjust max_steps from 320,000 toward ~900,000, or keep 320,000 and grow the corpus to ~10B unique tokens first.**  
  why: Plasma is at 14.4 unique tokens per parameter and 20 tokens/param seen — Chinchilla-compute-optimal but 100–1000x below what capable small models use (SmolLM2-1.7B ~6,470 tokens/param, Qwen2.5-0.5B ~36,000). Knowledge capacity of 2 bits/param requires ~1000 exposures per fact; Plasma is at ~1.4. Muennighoff et al. show up to 4 epochs of repeated data yields negligible loss difference versus unique data, so repetition is a legitimate lever here. The binding constraint is data exposure, not the 521M parameter budget.  
  gain: This is the single largest lever on the stated goal of beating 43%. Moving from 20 to ~60 tokens/param should produce a large, broad gain; the ~0% executable-code and multi-step-reasoning scores are primarily an exposure problem, not a capacity wall.  
  risk: Wall-clock: at 13,500 tok/s, 30B tokens is ~26 days, versus ~9 for the current plan. This is the main cost. Beyond ~4 epochs, returns decay toward zero. Mitigate by prioritising the throughput work (see below) and by adding unique tokens from the downloads already in flight.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Set explicit expectations for what 521M at this budget cannot do, and stop optimizing for those: reliable multi-digit arithmetic without a scratchpad, long-tail factual recall, and multi-step symbolic reasoning without chain-of-thought. Target instead: short factual QA, short-form writing, single-step instruction following, and short Python snippets.**  
  why: At ~1.4 exposures per fact against a 2-bits-per-parameter ceiling that needs ~1000 exposures, long-tail recall is unreachable at this budget regardless of tuning. Multi-step reasoning at sub-1B without CoT supervision is not a data-quantity problem. Meanwhile SmolLM2-360M reaches HellaSwag 54.5% and ARC-e ~65%, which shows the 43% overall baseline is comfortably beatable in the achievable categories.  
  gain: Redirects effort away from unwinnable categories. If arithmetic is wanted, the effective lever is explicit scratchpad/CoT formatting in SFT plus digit-level tokenization (already enabled via split digits), not more general pretraining.  
  risk: Judgement call on where the line sits; a strong math-heavy mid-training stage (OLMo 2's Dolmino approach — high-quality synthetic math injected during LR annealing) could move arithmetic more than this framing implies. Worth one experiment, not an open-ended campaign.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [high] **Add three cheap standing diagnostics: (a) log embedding-norm spread each eval — `E.norm(dim=1)` bucketed by a precomputed corpus frequency table, alerting if the never-seen/frequent ratio exceeds ~2.5x (currently 1.61/0.85 = 1.9x); (b) log `norm.weight.mean()` (currently 2.58, alert above ~4); (c) log max attention logit, alerting above 1e3 (Wortsman: everything above 1e4 diverged).**  
  why: All three are the leading indicators for the failure modes Plasma is otherwise unprotected against, and all are essentially free to compute at eval time. The embedding-norm-vs-frequency curve is already measurably skewed and will keep steepening for the remaining 280,000 steps.  
  gain: Turns three silent, slow-onset failure modes into observable ones, at ~zero cost. The embedding metric in particular is the single best early warning for tied-embedding rare-token pathology.  
  risk: None. Max attention logit requires either a forward hook or an eval-only recomputation of q@k^T for one batch — do it on one batch only, since materializing full attention scores for all layers would be memory-heavy on 16 GB.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Handle the dead vocabulary explicitly at the next tokenizer rebuild: reduce vocab from 48,000 to ~32,000, or retrain SentencePiece on a sample drawn from the final corpus mixture. Also investigate the 2.92% byte-fallback rate — decode a sample of sequences containing byte pieces to determine whether it is legitimate (CJK/math/emoji) or mojibake from the cleaning pipeline.**  
  why: 123 vocabulary entries are never used and ~889 are seen fewer than 1000 times across the entire 7.52B corpus; the rarest live tokens are orphaned BPE prefixes ('_earthqu', '_passeng', '_supermark', '_councill') that will never be well-trained. With tied embeddings, each dead row still occupies 1280 parameters and contributes a large-norm, badly-calibrated logit. Byte-fallback firing on 2.92% of tokens means roughly 220M of 7.52B tokens are single bytes — a real efficiency loss if it is mojibake rather than genuine multilingual content.  
  gain: Dropping to 32k vocab frees 16,000*1280 = 20.5M parameters (~4% of the model) to be spent on depth or width, and removes the worst glitch-token candidates. Fixing avoidable byte fallback would recover a meaningful slice of the token budget.  
  risk: Requires a full re-tokenization and corpus rebuild, so this is strictly for Plasma 1.2, not the current run. Do not change vocab mid-run — train.py correctly hard-fails on a vocab mismatch against shard meta.yaml.  
  verdict: (unverified) — 
- [medium] **Two low-risk cleanups: exclude the duplicated tied weight from checkpoints (`sd = model.state_dict(); sd.pop('output.weight')` in save_checkpoint, since load re-ties in __init__), and remove the per-micro-step GPU sync by accumulating `loss.detach()` on-device and calling `.item()` only at log_interval.**  
  why: Checkpoints are 6.26 GB and contain `output.weight` as a byte-identical 245 MB copy of `tok_emb.weight`; with keep_last_checkpoints=4 that is ~1 GB of wasted disk. Separately, `accum_loss += loss.item()` at line 261 forces 16 host-device syncs per optimizer step, which is directly relevant to the 13,500 tok/s (~56 TFLOP/s against a ~450 TFLOP/s bf16 peak) gap.  
  gain: ~245 MB smaller checkpoints and faster saves. The sync removal is one of several throughput candidates — a sibling benchmark of this exact change (step_bench.py --item-sync 0) is already running, so measure rather than assume.  
  risk: Popping output.weight requires load_checkpoint to use strict=False or for the tie to be re-established before loading (it is — transformer.py line 51 runs in __init__). Verify one checkpoint round-trips before adopting. The .item() change is behaviour-neutral.  
  verdict: (unverified) —
